# TypeScript Mastery

TypeScript isn't "JavaScript with type annotations bolted on" — it's a full **structural type system** running on top of a **compiler**. To master it at an architect level you stop merely *describing* values and start *modelling* them: thinking in sets of values, driving inference, building reusable type-level tools, and drawing a hard line between compile-time guarantees and runtime reality. This module takes you from the mental model all the way to type-driven architecture.

---

<h2 id="module-0">Module 0 — How to Think in Types</h2>

Before you write a single annotation, you need the right mental model. TypeScript isn't "JavaScript with labels glued on" — it's a separate language of **types** that runs only while you code and then disappears completely. Get these five ideas right and everything else in TypeScript stops feeling like magic.

---

### 🏗️ Structural Typing — Compatible by Shape, Not by Name

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Structural typing** means two types are compatible when they have the **same shape** (the same fields and methods) — *regardless of what they're named*. TypeScript never asks "is this officially a `Point`?" It asks "does this thing *have everything a `Point` needs*?"

The opposite is **nominal typing** (used by Java and C#), where a value is a `Point` only if it was explicitly declared as one — the *name* is part of the identity.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
JavaScript values are just bags of properties — `{ x: 1, y: 2 }` doesn't carry a label saying "I am a Point." A type system bolted onto JavaScript has to judge values by what they *look like*, because that's all there is. Structural typing makes TypeScript feel flexible: you can pass any object that "fits" without inheriting from a special class.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
TypeScript compares the **set of members** each type requires. If the value supplies (at least) everything the target needs, it's assignable.

```
   Does this value FIT the shape the slot wants?

   wants: { x, y }          has: { x, y }        FITS  ✅
   wants: { x, y }          has: { x, y, z }     FITS  ✅  (extra ok)
   wants: { x, y }          has: { x }           MISSING y  ❌

   Names ("Point" vs "Vector") are ignored — only shape matters.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **power socket** doesn't check the brand of your charger. It only cares about the *shape of the plug*. Any plug with the right prongs fits — Sony, Anker, no-name, doesn't matter. Structural typing is the socket: it checks the prongs (the shape), never the label.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface Point  { x: number; y: number; }
interface Vector { x: number; y: number; }   // different NAME, same SHAPE

const p: Point = { x: 1, y: 2 };
const v: Vector = p;   // ✅ OK — Vector and Point have identical shapes

function logName(o: { name: string }) {
  return o.name;
}
const dog = { name: "Rex", legs: 4 };
logName(dog);          // ✅ OK — dog HAS a `name`; the extra `legs` is fine
// (verified: compiles clean under --strict in TS 5.9)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is structural typing, and how does it differ from nominal typing?**
A: In **structural** typing (TypeScript), two types are compatible if their *shapes* match — same properties and methods — no matter what they're called. In **nominal** typing (Java, C#), compatibility depends on the *declared name/identity*: a value is a `Point` only if it was created as a `Point`. So in TS, two unrelated interfaces with the same fields are interchangeable; in Java they wouldn't be.

---

### 🔢 Types Are Sets of Values — The Single Most Useful Mental Model

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **type is a set** — the collection of all the values that are allowed to have that type. `string` isn't a "kind of thing"; it's literally *the set of every possible string*. `boolean` is the two-element set `{ true, false }`. Once you see types as sets, the weird ones suddenly make sense.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Beginners memorize rules like "a union is `A or B`" and "`never` means impossible" as disconnected trivia. The **sets** model unifies them: a **union** (`A | B`) is just set **union**, an **intersection** (`A & B`) is set **intersection**, and assignability is just the **subset** question — *"is every value of this set also in that set?"* One idea explains the whole lot.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Picture every value in the world inside one big circle. Each type is a region inside it.

```
   unknown  = the WHOLE box (every value)
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ┌───────────┐     ┌───────────┐      │
   │  │  string   │     │  number   │      │   string | number
   │  │ "a" "hi"  │     │  0  1  42 │      │   = both circles together
   │  └───────────┘     └───────────┘      │     (set UNION)
   │                                        │
   │   "hello"  = a one-element set ({"hello"})  ← a literal type
   │   never    = the EMPTY set (no values)      ← • (a dot, nothing in it)
   └──────────────────────────────────────┘

   X is assignable to Y  ⇔  set X ⊆ set Y  (every X-value is a Y-value)
```

- `unknown` = the **universal set** — every value belongs. (Safe "anything".)
- `never` = the **empty set** — *no* value belongs. It's what you get when a type is impossible (e.g. `string & number` — nothing is both).
- A **literal** type like `"hello"` is a **one-element set**.
- A **union** `A | B` is the set of values in A *or* B; an **intersection** `A & B` is values in A *and* B.

> **Note (simplification):** `any` is the deliberate escape hatch that ignores the set rules entirely — it's treated as *both* the universal set and assignable everywhere, which is exactly why it's unsafe. Reach for `unknown` instead.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of types as **club memberships**. `string` is "everyone in the Readers Club." `number` is "everyone in the Math Club." `string | number` is "anyone in *either* club" (union). `never` is "members of the *Empty* Club" — a club with literally no one in it, so no person can ever satisfy "you must be in the Empty Club." `unknown` is "every human alive."

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Bit = 0 | 1;            // a 2-element set: { 0, 1 }
const b: Bit = 1;            // ✅ 1 is in the set
// const c: Bit = 2;         // ❌ 2 is NOT in {0,1}

type Impossible = string & number;  // nothing is both → never (empty set)
const imp: Impossible = "x" as never; // only `never` can fill an empty set

let u: unknown = "could be anything"; // universal set: any value fits
// u.toUpperCase();          // ❌ must NARROW first — unknown gives no powers
if (typeof u === "string") {
  u.toUpperCase();           // ✅ inside here, u is in the `string` set
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Explain `any` vs `unknown` vs `never` using the "types as sets" idea.**
A: `unknown` is the **universal set** — every value fits, but you must narrow before using it, so it's the *safe* "anything." `never` is the **empty set** — no value fits; it shows up for impossible types and unreachable code. `any` opts out of the set model entirely (it's treated as everything *and* assignable to everything), which is why it's unsafe and "infectious." Assignability is just the subset test: `X` fits `Y` when every value of `X` is also a value of `Y`.

---

### 🌗 Type Space vs Value Space — The Two Parallel Worlds

#### 🧩 What is it?
TypeScript code lives in **two separate worlds** at once. **Value space** is ordinary runtime JavaScript — variables, functions, the things that *exist when the program runs*. **Type space** is the world of types — `interface`, `type`, annotations after a `:` — things that exist *only while type-checking* and are then erased.

A single name can mean **different things** in each world.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
This trips up every beginner: `typeof x` means one thing in a normal expression and a *different* thing after a `:`. Or you write `interface User {}` and then try to `console.log(User)` — and TypeScript says `User` doesn't exist. Once you know there are two worlds, these stop being mysterious: you were reaching into the wrong world.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Imagine a vertical line splitting your code. The left side is erased before the code runs; the right side survives.

```
        TYPE SPACE                 │        VALUE SPACE
   (erased at compile time)        │     (real JS at runtime)
   ────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────
   type / interface declarations   │   const / let / var
   the part after a `:`            │   function bodies, calls
   T extends U, keyof, generics     │   if / for / +  / objects
                                    │
   A name can live on ONE side, or on BOTH (a "dual" name):
     • interface User {}     → type space only
     • const User = {}       → value space only
     • class User {}         → BOTH (a type AND a value)
```

A few names exist in **both** worlds simultaneously — `class` and `enum` create a *type* (for annotations) **and** a *value* (a real runtime object). And some keywords switch meaning by location: `typeof foo` in type space asks "what is foo's *type*?", but in value space it returns a runtime string like `"number"`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like a word that's a **homonym**. "Bat" means a flying animal *or* a piece of sports equipment — same spelling, totally different thing, and *context* tells you which. In TypeScript, the context is *which side of the line you're on*: before a `:` you're talking types; in a normal statement you're talking values.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// `User` as a TYPE (type space) — exists only at compile time
interface User { id: number; }

// `User` as a VALUE (value space) — a different, runtime thing
const User = { table: "users" };

const u: User = { id: 1 };       // `User` here = the TYPE
console.log(User.table);         // `User` here = the VALUE  → "users"

// `typeof` lives in BOTH worlds with different jobs:
const count = 42;
type T = typeof count;           // type space → T is `number`
const kind = typeof count;       // value space → kind is the string "number"

// class is a DUAL name — a type AND a value:
class Animal { name = "rex"; }
const a: Animal = new Animal();  // `Animal` (type) ... `Animal` (value, the constructor)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why can `typeof x` mean two different things, and how can one name be both a type and a value?**
A: TypeScript has two namespaces: type space (compile-time) and value space (runtime). After a `:` (type space) `typeof x` produces *x's static type*; in an expression (value space) it's the JS operator returning a string like `"number"`. Names can be declared in either or both spaces — `interface` is type-only, `const` is value-only, while `class` and `enum` declare *both* a type and a runtime value, which is why you can annotate with a class name *and* call `new` on it.

---

### 🌫️ Type Erasure — Why Types Guarantee Nothing at Runtime

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Type erasure** means that when TypeScript compiles to JavaScript, **all the types are deleted**. Annotations, interfaces, generics — gone. The JavaScript that actually runs has no idea those types ever existed. So TypeScript can promise things to *you, the author*, but it promises **nothing to the running program**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
This is the bug that bites every team in production: you typed an API response as `{ age: number }`, the server actually sent `"age": "42"` (a string), and your code does `age * 2` and gets garbage — with **no error**. TypeScript checked your *assumptions*, not the *real data*, because by the time the data arrives, the types are already erased. Knowing this tells you exactly where you still need real runtime checks.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The compiler does two things and then throws the types away.

```
   YOUR .ts                  COMPILER                EMITTED .js (runs)
   ┌─────────────────┐                              ┌─────────────────┐
   │ const n: number │  ──►  1. check types   ──►   │ const n = 5;    │
   │   = 5;          │       2. delete types        │ // : number GONE│
   │ interface U {…} │                              │ // interface GONE│
   └─────────────────┘                              └─────────────────┘
        types exist            types vanish              no types left
                            here ────────────►  runtime can't see any type
```

There's no "reflection" — at runtime you cannot ask "is this value a `User`?", because `User` doesn't exist anymore. The only type information left is what *plain JavaScript* already had (`typeof`, `instanceof` on real classes, checking properties).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Types are like the **pencil guidelines an architect sketches before inking a blueprint**. They help draw everything straight — then they get erased before the blueprint is printed. The builder on site (the runtime) never sees the guidelines. If the *measurements in the real world* differ from the sketch, the pencil lines can't stop the wall from being built wrong.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface ApiUser { age: number; }

// We TRUST the response shape — but nothing checks it at runtime:
const res = JSON.parse('{ "age": "42" }') as ApiUser; // server sent a STRING
console.log(res.age * 2);
// TypeScript sees age as `number`, so this compiles with NO error.
// At runtime "42" * 2 → 84 here by coincidence, but res.age.toFixed()
// would throw: "res.age.toFixed is not a function". The type LIED.
// (verified: the `as` assertion compiles clean under --strict)
```

> **Note (simplification):** a few opt-in features *do* keep some type info around — e.g. `enum` emits a runtime object, and legacy decorator metadata can be emitted — but the default rule holds: **types are erased and give you no runtime guarantees**. To trust external data, validate it at the boundary (Module 11).

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Your code typed an API field as `number`, but the server sent a string. What happens, and why didn't TypeScript catch it?**
A: Nothing catches it — the code compiles and runs, then misbehaves (e.g. `value.toFixed()` throws because it's really a string). TypeScript only checks *static assumptions* at compile time and then **erases all types**; the running JavaScript has no type information and never verifies the actual payload. The fix is runtime validation at the trust boundary (a type guard or a schema like Zod) so the unknown input is *parsed* into a typed value before you use it.

---

### ⚖️ Two Jobs & Deliberate Unsoundness — Check, Emit, and a Few Honest Holes

#### 🧩 What is it?
The TypeScript compiler does **two independent jobs**: (1) **type-check** your code (report errors), and (2) **emit** — translate it to JavaScript and *downlevel* it (rewrite newer syntax for older runtimes). These are *separate concerns*: by default, **type errors do not stop the JavaScript from being emitted**.

And TypeScript is, on purpose, **unsound** in a few places — *"unsound"* meaning it sometimes calls code type-safe when it actually isn't, trading a few holes for everyday convenience.

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know this? (the problem)
Two practical surprises. First: a build tool like esbuild or Babel can *emit* your JS super fast precisely because it **skips type-checking** — so you still run `tsc` separately to catch errors. And `tsc` will happily produce output even when your types are broken (unless you opt into `noEmitOnError`). Second: if you believe "it compiled, so it's 100% safe," you'll get burned — knowing *where* TypeScript chose convenience over safety tells you where to be careful.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The two jobs run side by side; emit doesn't wait for a clean check.

```
                ┌──────────────────┐
   your .ts ──► │  1. TYPE-CHECK   │ ──► errors (reported, but...)
                ├──────────────────┤
           └──► │  2. EMIT/DOWNLEVEL│ ──► .js  ← produced even WITH errors
                └──────────────────┘        (unless noEmitOnError is set)
```

A classic deliberate hole is **array covariance**: TypeScript lets you treat a `Cat[]` as an `Animal[]`, which *feels* right — but then you can `push` a plain `Animal` into it, and later code that expected only `Cat`s breaks. The compiler allows it because forbidding it would block tons of harmless everyday code. The `as` assertion is another opt-in hole: you *override* the checker and it trusts you.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
**Two jobs:** a print shop has a *proofreader* (the type-checker) and a *printer* (the emitter). By default the printer runs even if the proofreader flagged typos — you get printed pages with mistakes unless you tell the shop "don't print if there are typos." **Unsoundness:** it's like a bouncer who, to keep the line moving, waves through anyone holding *a* ticket without scanning it — almost always fine, occasionally lets in a fake. A faster door, with a known small risk.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// --- Two jobs: emit happens despite a type error ---
const x: number = "oops"; // ❌ type error TS2322 is reported...
// ...but `tsc` STILL writes the .js (var x = "oops";) unless noEmitOnError.
// (verified: out/x.js was produced even though tsc exited with an error)

// --- Deliberate unsoundness: array covariance ---
class Animal { name = "a"; }
class Cat extends Animal { meow() {} }

const cats: Cat[] = [new Cat()];
const animals: Animal[] = cats;   // ✅ allowed (Cat[] seen as Animal[])
animals.push(new Animal());       // ✅ compiles — but we just added a non-Cat!
cats[1].meow();                   // 💥 runtime: "cats[1].meow is not a function"
// The compiler approved every line. (verified under --strict in TS 5.9)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Is TypeScript sound? Give an example, and why do type errors not stop the build?**
A: No — TypeScript is **deliberately unsound** in spots, trading perfect safety for productivity. The classic example is **array covariance**: a `Cat[]` is assignable to `Animal[]`, but you can then `push` an `Animal` into it and crash code that assumed only `Cat`s (`as` assertions and bivariant method params are other holes). Separately, type-checking and emit are *independent jobs*: `tsc` emits JavaScript even when there are type errors by default — that's why a fast transpiler (esbuild/Babel) can strip types without checking, and why you set `noEmitOnError` if you want errors to block the build.

---

<h2 id="module-1">Module 1 — The Type System: Primitives to Unions</h2>

Every value your program touches has a **type** — a label that says what kind of thing it is. TypeScript's whole job is to track those labels and warn you *before* you run the code when they don't line up. This module is the vocabulary of that system: the basic building blocks (`string`, `number`), the strange-but-essential special types (`any`, `unknown`, `never`), and the two ways to combine them — **union** ("one of these") and **intersection** ("all of these at once"). Get these right and the rest of TypeScript clicks into place.

---

### 🔤 Primitive Types — The Atoms of the Type System

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **primitive** is one of JavaScript's most basic value kinds, and TypeScript gives each one a type name: `string` (text), `number` (any number, including decimals), `boolean` (`true`/`false`), `bigint` (huge whole numbers), `symbol` (unique tokens), plus the two "nothing" types `null` and `undefined`. These are the atoms — every bigger type is built from them.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
In plain JavaScript, a variable can silently change kind: a `name` that was text becomes a number, and you don't find out until the app crashes in production. Annotating a variable as `string` tells the compiler "this is text, forever" — so the moment you try to put a number in it, you get a red squiggle in your editor instead of a 2 a.m. bug report.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You attach a type with a colon after the name — this is called a **type annotation** (a label you write by hand):

```
  let title: string = "Hi"
       │       │        │
     name    type    value must
            (label)   match the label
```

Often you don't even need to write the label: TypeScript performs **inference** (guessing the type from the value). `let n = 5` is automatically `number`. Annotate when inference can't see your intent; let it infer otherwise.

> **Note (simplification):** lowercase `string` is the type you almost always want. There is *also* an uppercase `String` (the object-wrapper type) — avoid it; it means something subtly different and is considered a mistake here.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of labeled jars in a kitchen: one says "Sugar", another "Salt". The label doesn't change what's already inside — it tells you (and anyone else) what is *allowed* in that jar. Pour salt into the jar marked "Sugar" and the label has been violated. A type annotation is that label, and the compiler is the person who checks it before you cook.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
let title: string = "TypeScript";
let count: number = 42;          // 42, 3.14, -7 are all `number`
let isReady: boolean = true;
let nothing: null = null;
let missing: undefined = undefined;

// title = 99;
// ❌ Error: Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string'.

let inferred = "no annotation needed"; // TS infers: string
// inferred = 5;
// ❌ Error: Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: If TypeScript can infer types, why annotate at all?**
A: Inference is great for local variables initialized on the spot. You annotate where inference can't read your intent or where it would guess too loosely — chiefly **function parameters** (TS can't see how callers will use them) and **public API boundaries**, where an explicit type is documentation and a guardrail against accidental changes.

---

### 🎯 Literal Types & Widening — When `"GET"` Is Its Own Type

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **literal type** is a type whose only allowed value is one *specific* constant — not "any string", but exactly `"GET"`, or exactly `42`, or exactly `true`. **Widening** is TypeScript's automatic move of broadening a literal up to its general type (`"GET"` becomes `string`) in places where it expects the value to change.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes "a string" is too loose. An HTTP method should be `"GET" | "POST" | "PUT" | "DELETE"` — not *any* string. Literal types let you say exactly which values are legal, so a typo like `"GTE"` is caught at compile time. But TypeScript has to decide: when you write a value, do you mean "this exact value" or "a value of this kind that I'll reassign later"? That decision is widening.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The rule is driven by **mutability** — whether the binding can be reassigned:

```
  let x = "GET"     ──▶  type widens to:  string
        (reassignable, so TS assumes
         it might hold other strings)

  const y = "GET"   ──▶  type stays:      "GET"
        (can never change, so the exact
         value IS the type)
```

`let` widens because you *could* later write `x = "POST"`. `const` can never be reassigned, so its type is pinned to the exact literal. To pin a literal *inside* a `let` or an object, add **`as const`** (covered below).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `let` variable is a **whiteboard**: you wrote "GET" today, but the board exists to be rewritten, so the label is just "writing surface" (`string`). A `const` is an **engraved plaque**: "GET" is carved in permanently, so the label *is* "the plaque that says GET" (`"GET"`).

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
let method1 = "GET";        // type: string   (widened — let is reassignable)
const method2 = "GET";      // type: "GET"     (kept — const never changes)

const obj = { url: "GET" }; // type: { url: string }
// Object PROPERTIES widen even under const, because the object
// itself is mutable: you can still do obj.url = "POST".

type HttpMethod = "GET" | "POST";
function request(m: HttpMethod) { /* ... */ }

request(method2);  // ✅ "GET" matches
// request(method1);
// ❌ Error: Argument of type 'string' is not assignable
//    to parameter of type 'HttpMethod'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does `let x = "hello"` infer `string` but `const x = "hello"` infer `"hello"`?**
A: Because of literal widening. A `const` can never be reassigned, so its narrowest accurate type is the literal `"hello"`. A `let` is expected to change, so TypeScript widens it to the general `string` to keep future assignments legal.

---

### 🧊 `as const` — Freezing Values into Their Most Precise Type

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`as const`** is a special assertion you append to a value (`[...] as const`, `{...} as const`) that tells TypeScript: "infer the *narrowest, deepest, read-only* type possible." It pins literals to their exact values, makes every property `readonly`, and turns arrays into fixed **tuples** (covered later) of literals.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
By default, object properties and array elements **widen**: `{ method: "GET" }` becomes `{ method: string }`, and `["GET", "/users"]` becomes `string[]`. That loses the precise information you need to build a union or a typed lookup table. `as const` stops the widening so the exact values survive into the type.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`as const` applies three changes at once, all the way down (deeply):

```
  ["GET", "/users"]            ──▶  string[]
  ["GET", "/users"] as const   ──▶  readonly ["GET", "/users"]
                                     │         │
                                 read-only   exact literals,
                                  + fixed     fixed positions
                                   length    (a tuple)

  { role: "admin" }            ──▶  { role: string }
  { role: "admin" } as const   ──▶  { readonly role: "admin" }
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A normal object is a **draft document** — fields are placeholders you can still edit and broaden ("name: some text"). `as const` is **laminating** it: every field is frozen with its exact current value, and nothing can be rewritten. What you see is locked in, precisely.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
const route = ["GET", "/users"] as const;
// type: readonly ["GET", "/users"]   (verified with tsc 5.9)
// route[0] = "POST";
// ❌ Error: Cannot assign to '0' because it is a read-only property.

const routeNoConst = ["GET", "/users"];
// type: string[]  — values widened, length not fixed

// Classic use: derive a union from a real array of values
const ROLES = ["admin", "editor", "viewer"] as const;
type Role = typeof ROLES[number];
// Role = "admin" | "editor" | "viewer"  — one source of truth
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `as const` actually do?**
A: It applies a "const assertion": literals are kept as their exact literal types instead of widening, every property/element becomes `readonly`, and array literals become fixed-length tuples. It's the standard way to keep precise values so you can derive unions or build typed config objects from real data.

---

### ☢️ `any` — The Infectious Opt-Out

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`any`** is the "turn off type-checking for this value" type. A value typed `any` can be assigned anything, do anything, and be passed anywhere — TypeScript stops checking it entirely. It's an escape hatch back to plain, unchecked JavaScript.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Occasionally you genuinely don't know or can't express a type (legacy code, a migration in progress). `any` lets you keep moving. But it solves a small short-term pain by creating a large hidden one: `any` is **infectious** — it spreads. Touch an `any` and the result is `any` too, silently disabling checks far beyond the one line you meant.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`any` disables every check, and that "off switch" flows outward through any expression that touches it:

```
   const data: any = JSON.parse(input)
            │
            ▼   every access off `any` is ALSO any
   data.user.name.toUpperCase()
            │
            ▼   no error, even if `user` is undefined here
   💥 crashes at runtime, with zero compile-time warning
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`any` is a **VIP pass that skips all security**. Handy for the one person who truly needs it — but anyone they hand it to also walks past every checkpoint. One pass quietly disables the security you built for the whole building.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
let value: any = "hello";
value.toFixed(2);     // no error... value is a string, this CRASHES at runtime
value();              // no error... not a function either. CRASHES.

const data: any = { count: 5 };
const n: number = data.count; // `any` flows into `n` unchecked
// Nothing here is verified. `any` turned off the whole safety net.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why is `any` considered dangerous, and what makes it "infectious"?**
A: `any` disables type-checking for that value, so real bugs (calling a non-function, reading a missing property) compile silently and blow up at runtime. It's infectious because any expression derived from an `any` is *also* `any`, so a single `any` can quietly switch off checking across large parts of a codebase. Prefer `unknown` at boundaries.

---

### 🛡️ `unknown` — The Safe Top Type

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`unknown`** is the type that means "this could be literally any value, and I refuse to assume which." Like `any`, *anything* is assignable **to** it. Unlike `any`, you can't *do* anything with an `unknown` value until you **narrow** it — prove what it is with a check first. It's the **top type**: the set of all possible values.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Data crossing into your program from the outside — `JSON.parse`, a network response, user input — is genuinely unknown at compile time. Typing it `any` is a lie that disables safety. Typing it `unknown` is honest: TypeScript forces you to *check* the shape before you trust it, turning "hope it's right" into "prove it's right."

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You accept anything into `unknown`, but TypeScript **blocks all use** until you narrow it with a runtime check (`typeof`, `instanceof`, a property check). After the check, inside that branch, the type is refined to something usable:

```
   unknown  ──── you may NOT call/read/index it directly
      │
      ▼   add a runtime check: `if (typeof x === "string")`
   inside the branch the type is now `string`  ✅ safe to use
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`unknown` is a **sealed package left at your door**. You can accept it (assign it) — but you don't shake it or open it blindly. You scan and inspect it first (narrowing), and only act on it once you know what's inside. `any` is opening every package with your eyes closed.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
function handle(input: unknown) {
  // input.toUpperCase();
  // ❌ Error TS18046: 'input' is of type 'unknown'.

  if (typeof input === "string") {
    // narrowed to `string` inside this block — now safe
    return input.toUpperCase(); // ✅ no error
  }
  return "not a string";
}

const parsed: unknown = JSON.parse('{"id": 1}');
// You MUST validate `parsed` before trusting its shape — exactly the point.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: `any` vs `unknown` — when do you use each?**
A: Both accept any value, but `unknown` is the safe one: it forbids using the value until you **narrow** it with a runtime check, whereas `any` disables checking entirely. Use `unknown` for untrusted input at boundaries (parsed JSON, API responses) and narrow before use. Reserve `any` for rare, deliberate escapes — ideally never.

---

### 🕳️ `never` — The Bottom Type (the Empty Set)

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`never`** is the type of a value that can **never exist**. It's the **bottom type** — the *empty set* of values. A function that always throws, or loops forever, "returns" `never` because it never actually returns a value at all.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You need a way to say "this can't happen" and have the compiler enforce it. `never` powers **exhaustiveness checks** (covered fully in Module 2): if you've handled every case of a union, the leftover type is `never`, and adding a new case you forgot to handle makes that `never` slot break — a compile error that points straight at the missing branch.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`never` sits at the bottom of the type system, which gives it two mirror-image rules:

```
  never  ──▶  assignable TO every type
              (the empty set fits inside any set)

  nothing (except never) ──▶ assignable to never
              (no real value belongs to the empty set)
```

So `const s: string = neverValue` is fine, but `const x: never = "hi"` is an error.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`never` is a **list of people who are simultaneously taller than 10 feet and shorter than 1 foot**. The list is genuinely empty — no one qualifies. Because it's empty, the statement "everyone on this list can fly" is trivially true (nobody contradicts it), which is exactly why `never` slots into any type without complaint.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
function fail(msg: string): never {
  throw new Error(msg); // never returns normally
}

const n: never = fail("boom");
const asString: string = n; // ✅ never is assignable to string

// const bad: never = "hello";
// ❌ Error TS2322: Type '"hello"' is not assignable to type 'never'.

// Exhaustiveness preview (full version in Module 2):
type Shape = "circle" | "square";
function area(s: Shape) {
  if (s === "circle") return 1;
  if (s === "square") return 2;
  const exhaustive: never = s; // ✅ s is `never` here — every case handled
  return exhaustive;
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is `never` and where would you actually use it?**
A: `never` is the bottom type — the empty set of values, the type of code that never returns (throws or loops forever). Practically, it's used for functions that always throw and for **exhaustiveness checking**: assigning the remaining value to a `never` variable in the final branch makes the compiler error if a new union member is added but left unhandled.

---

### 🚫 `void`, `null` & `undefined` — The "No Useful Value" Types

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`void`** is the type of "I return, but with nothing meaningful" — the default for a function with no `return` value. **`undefined`** is the value a variable has before it's assigned, or a missing property. **`null`** is a deliberate "intentionally empty" value you set yourself.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Not every function produces a result — `console.log` does work and hands back nothing. `void` names that. And you need to distinguish "I never set this" (`undefined`) from "I purposely set it to empty" (`null`). With `strictNullChecks` on (the recommended setting), these aren't allowed to sneak into other types, which kills a huge class of "cannot read property of undefined" crashes.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`void` differs from `undefined` in one important, practical way: a function typed to return `void` is allowed to return *something* — callers just must ignore it. This is what lets `array.forEach(x => arr.push(x))` work even though `push` returns a number.

```
  () => void         "callers will ignore whatever comes back"
       │
       ▼   so a void-typed callback MAY return a value
  arr.forEach(x => out.push(x))   ✅ push returns number, ignored
```

> **Note (simplification):** `void` and `undefined` are *almost* interchangeable, but not equal. The `void`-callback rule above is the main difference you'll hit in practice.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`void` is **mailing a letter**: the action happens, but the mailbox hands you nothing back. `undefined` is **a form field you never filled in** (blank by default). `null` is **a field where you wrote "N/A"** — deliberately, explicitly empty.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
function logIt(msg: string): void {
  console.log(msg);     // does work, returns nothing
}

let name: string | null = null;  // explicitly "no name yet"
name = "Ada";                    // later, a real value

let age: number | undefined;     // undefined until assigned
// const x: number = age;
// ❌ With strictNullChecks: 'number | undefined' not assignable to 'number'

// The void-callback exception:
const nums = [1, 2, 3];
const out: number[] = [];
nums.forEach((n) => out.push(n)); // ✅ push returns number; forEach ignores it
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `void` and `undefined`, and between `null` and `undefined`?**
A: `void` describes a function's "returns nothing useful" — and notably a `void`-typed callback may still return a value that's ignored. `undefined` is the actual "not assigned / missing" value. `null` is an *intentional* empty value a developer sets; `undefined` is the default "never given a value" state. Under `strictNullChecks`, both must be explicitly included in a type before they're allowed.

---

### 🔀 Union Types — Modeling "One Of"

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **union type**, written `A | B`, means a value is *one of* several types — either an `A` or a `B` (or more). `string | number` is "a string **or** a number." It's how you model a value that legitimately has more than one possible shape.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Real values are often "one of a few things": an ID might be a `string` or a `number`; a status is `"loading" | "success" | "error"`; a setting is `boolean | "auto"`. Without unions you'd be forced to use `any` and lose all safety. Unions express the real set of possibilities *exactly*, and the compiler then makes you handle each.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A union is a **set union** of the allowed values. Crucially, until you **narrow** it, you can only use members common to *all* the types in the union — because TypeScript can't yet know which one you have:

```
   value: string | number
            │
            ▼   before narrowing: only shared operations allowed
   typeof value === "string" ?
        │                    │
        ▼ yes                ▼ no
   value is string      value is number
   value.toUpperCase()  value.toFixed(2)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A union is a **"cash or card" sign** at a shop. Payment is *one of* those two — not both at once. Before you know which a customer will use, you can only assume what's common to both (they'll pay *somehow*). Once they pull out a card (narrowing), you know to use the card reader specifically.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Id = string | number;

function printId(id: Id) {
  // id.toUpperCase();
  // ❌ Error: 'toUpperCase' does not exist on type 'number'.
  //    (only operations common to BOTH are allowed pre-narrowing)

  if (typeof id === "string") {
    console.log(id.toUpperCase()); // ✅ narrowed to string
  } else {
    console.log(id.toFixed(0));    // ✅ narrowed to number
  }
}

type Status = "loading" | "success" | "error"; // a union of literals
let state: Status = "loading"; // only these three strings are allowed
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why can't I call `.toUpperCase()` on a `string | number` directly?**
A: Because pre-narrowing, TypeScript only allows operations valid for *every* member of the union, and `number` has no `.toUpperCase()`. You must **narrow** first — e.g. `typeof id === "string"` — after which, inside that branch, the value is treated as `string` and the call is allowed.

---

### 🔗 Intersection Types — Modeling "All Of"

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **intersection type**, written `A & B`, means a value is *all of* several types at once — it has every property of `A` **and** every property of `B`. Where a union is "one of," an intersection is "both, combined."

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes you want to *merge* shapes: a `Person` who is also `Serializable`, or props that are the base props **plus** some extras. Intersection composes existing object types into a bigger one without rewriting them, keeping each piece reusable.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
For object types, `A & B` produces a type that requires **the combined set of all members** — you must satisfy every requirement from both sides:

```
   { name: string }   &   { age: number }
            │                   │
            └─────────┬─────────┘
                      ▼
        { name: string; age: number }
        (must provide BOTH to satisfy it)
```

> **Note (simplification):** intersecting *primitives* like `string & number` yields `never` (no value is both at once). Intersection is mainly useful for combining **object** shapes.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A union "cash or card" is one payment method. An intersection is a **job posting requiring "driver's license AND first-aid certificate"** — a single candidate must hold *both* credentials at once to qualify. Not one or the other: both, together.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface Named { name: string; }
interface Aged  { age: number; }

type Person = Named & Aged; // must have BOTH name and age

const p: Person = { name: "Ada", age: 36 }; // ✅
// const q: Person = { name: "Ada" };
// ❌ Error: Property 'age' is missing in type '{ name: string; }'.

// Common real use: extend props without rewriting the base
type ButtonProps = { label: string };
type WithIcon    = { icon: string };
type IconButton  = ButtonProps & WithIcon; // { label: string; icon: string }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `A | B` and `A & B`?**
A: `A | B` (union) is "**one of** A or B" — the value is one type or the other, and you must narrow before using type-specific members. `A & B` (intersection) is "**both at once**" — for object types it merges all members, so the value must satisfy every requirement of both. Union models alternatives; intersection models combination.

---

### 📦 `type` Alias vs `interface` — Two Ways to Name a Shape

#### 🧩 What is it?
Both `type` and `interface` let you give a name to an object shape so you can reuse it. `interface User { id: number }` and `type User = { id: number }` look almost identical — but they have different powers. The big two differences: **`interface` supports declaration merging**, and **`type` can name things interfaces can't** (unions, primitives, tuples).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You don't want to repeat `{ id: number; name: string }` everywhere. Naming it once keeps code DRY and readable. The question is *which* tool — and the answer depends on what you're naming and whether you want it to be open to extension.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Here's the practical decision table:

```
                          interface    type alias
  name an object shape       ✅            ✅
  name a union (A | B)       ❌            ✅
  name a primitive/tuple     ❌            ✅
  declaration merging        ✅            ❌
  extend (extends / &)       ✅ extends    ✅ via &
```

**Declaration merging** means: declare the same `interface` name twice and TypeScript **combines** them into one. Types can't do this — a duplicate `type` name is an error. Merging is powerful for libraries (augmenting existing types) but can also be a footgun, because a shape can grow from a distant file.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
An `interface` is a **shared Google Doc**: multiple people can append sections and they all merge into one document. A `type` alias is a **printed-and-signed contract**: it says exactly one thing, and you can't staple extra clauses onto the same signed copy — you'd write a new contract that *references* it.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Declaration merging — only `interface` can do this:
interface Box { width: number; }
interface Box { height: number; }
const b: Box = { width: 10, height: 20 }; // ✅ both required — they merged

// Duplicate type alias is an error:
// type Cup = { size: number };
// type Cup = { color: string };
// ❌ Error: Duplicate identifier 'Cup'.

// Only `type` can name a union / primitive / tuple:
type Id = string | number;        // union  — interface can't
type Pair = [number, number];     // tuple  — interface can't

// Both can extend:
interface Animal { legs: number; }
interface Dog extends Animal { bark(): void; }      // interface extends
type Cat = Animal & { meow(): void };               // type via &
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: `type` vs `interface` — which do you use and why?**
A: For plain object shapes either works; many teams default to `interface` for objects (it supports declaration merging, which is needed to augment third-party types) and reach for `type` when naming a union, primitive, tuple, or a more complex/computed type that interfaces can't express. The key technical differences: only `interface` merges across declarations; only `type` can alias non-object types.

---

### 🎭 Type Assertions — `as`, `!`, and Why They're Smells

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **type assertion** is you overriding the compiler: "trust me, I know this value's type better than you do." The forms are `value as SomeType` (re-label a value), the **non-null assertion** `value!` ("this isn't null/undefined, I promise"), and `as const` (the precise-freeze we saw earlier). Assertions change what the *compiler believes* — they do **nothing** at runtime.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Occasionally you truly do know more than TypeScript — e.g. you just checked `document.getElementById` returns a real element. But assertions are usually a **code smell**: they silence the checker instead of proving the fact, so if you're wrong, the bug returns at runtime with no warning. They override safety rather than establishing it.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
An assertion is a **compile-time-only relabel**. It's erased before the code runs, so a wrong assertion gives you zero runtime protection:

```
   value as Foo     ──▶  compiler now treats value as Foo
        │                 (NO runtime check, NO conversion)
        ▼
   if you were wrong, the lie surfaces later as a crash
```

Prefer **narrowing** (`typeof`, `instanceof`, a property check) — it *proves* the type and the compiler verifies your proof. An assertion just asserts.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Narrowing is **showing your ID at the door** — the bouncer checks it. An assertion is **shouting "I'm on the list!" and walking past** — nobody verified anything. If you lied, the problem only shows up later, inside, when it's harder to deal with.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Non-null assertion (!): "I promise this isn't null"
const el = document.getElementById("app")!; // type: HTMLElement (not null)
// If #app doesn't exist, el is actually null → runtime crash on use.

// `as`: relabel — the compiler believes you, blindly
const input = {} as { id: number };
input.id;        // compiles fine... but `id` is undefined at runtime 💥

// The dangerous "double assertion" — forcing an impossible cast:
const n = "hello" as unknown as number; // compiles, completely a lie

// ✅ Prefer narrowing — it PROVES the type:
const maybe: string | null = Math.random() > 0.5 ? "x" : null;
if (maybe !== null) {
  maybe.toUpperCase(); // safe: checked, not asserted
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why are `as` and `!` considered code smells?**
A: They tell the compiler to *believe* a type rather than *verify* it, and they're erased at runtime — so a wrong assertion gives no protection and surfaces as a runtime crash. They bypass the safety TypeScript exists to provide. Prefer narrowing (runtime checks the compiler can follow) or proper validation; reserve assertions for the rare cases where you genuinely have knowledge the type system can't express.

---

### 🎚️ Tuples & Readonly Tuples — Fixed-Length, Position-Typed Arrays

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **tuple** is an array with a **fixed length** where **each position has its own type**. `[string, number]` is "exactly two elements: a string then a number." A **readonly tuple** (`readonly [string, number]`) is the same but frozen — you can't reassign its elements. Tuples can also have **named members** for readability: `[x: number, y: number]`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A regular array type like `(string | number)[]` says "any number of strings-or-numbers, in any order" — too loose for things that are positional. Coordinates `[x, y]`, a `[key, value]` pair, or React's `[state, setState]` from `useState` all rely on *position meaning something*. Tuples capture that exact structure so the wrong slot or wrong length is a compile error.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A tuple pins each index to a type and fixes the length:

```
   [string, number]
     │       │
   index 0  index 1
   string   number     ← length is exactly 2, order matters

   t[0] → string   t[1] → number   t[2] → ❌ out of bounds
```

Add `readonly` and the elements can't be reassigned — the same protection `as const` gives, made explicit in the type.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A tuple is a **form with labeled boxes in a fixed order**: box 1 is "First Name" (text), box 2 is "Age" (number). You can't swap them or add a third box — the form's structure is fixed. A plain array is a **blank notepad**: any number of lines, any content, no positions enforced.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
let point: [number, number] = [10, 20]; // exactly two numbers
// point = [10];
// ❌ Error: Source has 1 element(s) but target requires 2.

// Each position is its own type:
let pair: [string, number] = ["age", 30];
// pair = [30, "age"];
// ❌ Error: 'number' is not assignable to 'string' (position 0).

// Readonly tuple — frozen:
const coords: readonly [number, number] = [1, 2];
// coords[0] = 9;
// ❌ Error: Cannot assign to '0' because it is a read-only property.

// Named members (labels are for readability only):
type Point = [x: number, y: number];

// Real-world shape: React's useState returns a tuple
// const [count, setCount]: [number, (n: number) => void] = ...
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between a tuple and a regular array in TypeScript?**
A: An array type like `number[]` allows any length with every element the same type. A tuple fixes the length and gives each position its own type — `[string, number]` is exactly a string then a number. Tuples model positional data (coordinates, key/value pairs, `useState`'s return). Adding `readonly` (or building the value with `as const`) freezes the elements against reassignment.

---

<h2 id="module-2">Module 2 — Narrowing & Control-Flow Analysis</h2>

A union type like `string | number` says *"this value is one of these, but I'm not telling you which."* So how does code like `value.toUpperCase()` ever type-check? The answer is **narrowing**: as the compiler walks your `if`s, `switch`es, and returns, it shrinks the type at each point — turning a vague union into the one specific type that's actually possible *right there*. Master this and you'll write code where the compiler *proves* you handled every case.

---

### 🔬 Control-Flow Narrowing — How the Compiler Tracks Your Type

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Narrowing** is the compiler taking a **wide type** (a value that could be several things) and, as it reads your code top-to-bottom, deducing a **narrower type** (fewer possibilities) at each spot — based on the checks you wrote. A **union type** (written `A | B`, meaning "an `A` *or* a `B`") is the thing most often narrowed.

The engine that does this is **Control-Flow Analysis (CFA)** — "control flow" just means *the order your code runs in* (which branch of an `if`, which `case`, what happens after a `return`).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A value typed `string | number` has *no* methods you can safely call: `.toUpperCase()` exists on `string` but not `number`; `.toFixed()` is the reverse. Without narrowing, you'd have to cast (`as string`) everywhere and lose all safety. Narrowing lets you **write a normal runtime check** (`typeof v === "string"`) and have the *types* follow that check automatically — no casts, no lies.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The compiler models your function as a **control-flow graph** — boxes (statements) joined by arrows (the paths execution can take). At each arrow it carries the *current narrowed type*. A check like `typeof v === "string"` **splits** the flow: the type going into the `then` branch loses everything that failed the check; the `else` branch gets the remainder.

```
            v: string | number
                   │
        typeof v === "string" ?
            ┌──────┴───────┐
          true            false
            │                │
       v: string        v: number
       (.toUpperCase)   (.toFixed)
```

The two branches carry **different** types for the same variable `v`. That is the whole idea: a name's type depends on *where you are* in the flow.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **bouncer** at a club checks IDs at the door. *Before* the door, the crowd is "anyone" (the wide type). The bouncer's rule ("21 or older") splits the line: everyone who walks **in** is now known to be 21+, everyone turned **away** is under 21. Same crowd, but past each side of the door you *know more*. Your `if` check is the bouncer; the branches are the two doors.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
function format(v: string | number): string {
  if (typeof v === "string") {
    // here v is narrowed to: string
    return v.toUpperCase();   // ✅ ok — strings have toUpperCase
  }
  // here (after the return above) v is narrowed to: number
  return v.toFixed(2);        // ✅ ok — numbers have toFixed
}

format("hi");   // "HI"
format(3.14159); // "3.14"
```
Notice the second branch needs no `else`: the `return` inside the `if` removes `string` from every path that continues, so the compiler *knows* `v` is `number` afterward.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: A parameter is typed `string | number`. How can `v.toUpperCase()` ever compile?**
A: It can't on the raw union — but inside a branch guarded by `typeof v === "string"`, **control-flow analysis** narrows `v` to `string`, and *only there* is `.toUpperCase()` valid. The type of a variable isn't fixed; it changes per location based on the checks the compiler has seen on the path to that point.

---

### 🧰 Built-in Narrowing — `typeof`, `instanceof`, `in`, Truthiness, Equality

#### 🧩 What is it?
TypeScript recognises a handful of **ordinary JavaScript checks** as narrowing signals. You don't learn special syntax — you write the runtime check you'd write anyway, and the compiler reads it as a type instruction.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Values arrive as unions all the time: a function takes `Error | string`, a config field is `string | null`, an event payload is one of several shapes. You need a *runtime-real* way to tell them apart — one that's true both when the code runs **and** in the type-checker. These five built-ins are exactly the checks JavaScript already has, taught to the type system.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each built-in narrows a different way:

```
 typeof x === "string"   → primitives: string|number|
                            boolean|symbol|bigint|
                            undefined|object|function
 x instanceof Error      → class instances (uses prototype)
 "swim" in animal        → presence of a property/key
 if (x) { } / if (!x) { }→ truthiness: drops null,undefined,
                            "" , 0 , NaN , false  in the branch
 a === b , x === null    → equality: matches values & shared types
```

- `typeof` is for **primitives** (it returns one of those 8 strings).
- `instanceof` is for **class instances** — it checks the prototype chain (the runtime "is-a" link), so it only works on things made with `new`.
- `in` checks **does this property exist?** — handy when shapes differ by which keys they have.
- **Truthiness** (`if (x)`) removes the *falsy* members (`null`, `undefined`, `""`, `0`, `NaN`, `false`) inside the truthy branch.
- **Equality** narrows by comparing values; `=== null` peels off `null`, and comparing two unions narrows both to their shared types.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Sorting mail. `typeof` reads the **stamp's category** (letter vs. parcel — the basic *kind*). `instanceof` checks the **return address brand** (is this from "Acme Corp"?). `in` asks **does the envelope have a tracking number printed on it?**. Truthiness tosses the **empty envelopes**. Equality matches against a **specific address you're looking for**. Different tools, same goal: figure out *exactly* what you're holding.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// instanceof — class instances
function describe(e: Error | string): string {
  if (e instanceof Error) {
    return e.message;        // e: Error
  }
  return e.toUpperCase();    // e: string
}

// in — distinguish shapes by which key exists
type Fish = { swim: () => void };
type Bird = { fly: () => void };
function move(a: Fish | Bird): void {
  if ("swim" in a) {
    a.swim();                // a: Fish
  } else {
    a.fly();                 // a: Bird
  }
}

// equality across two unions — keeps only the SHARED type
function same(a: string | null, b: string | undefined): void {
  if (a === b) {
    a.toUpperCase();         // a: string  (null can't === undefined here)
    b.toUpperCase();         // b: string
  }
}
```
> **Note (simplification):** `typeof someObjectOrArray` is the string `"object"` for *both* objects and arrays (and even `null`!), so `typeof` alone can't tell an array from a plain object — use `Array.isArray(x)` for that. `instanceof` is the right tool for class instances, not `typeof`.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When would you reach for `in` instead of `typeof` or `instanceof`?**
A: When the union members are **plain object shapes** (not primitives, not class instances) that differ by **which properties they have**. `typeof` only distinguishes primitive kinds and lumps all objects under `"object"`; `instanceof` needs a constructor/class. `in` checks for a property's presence, which is the natural discriminator for object-literal unions like `Fish | Bird`.

---

### 🏷️ Discriminated (Tagged) Unions — The Pattern You'll Reach for Daily

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **discriminated union** (a.k.a. *tagged union*) is a union of object types that all share one **literal-typed property** — the **discriminant** (or "tag"). Each member uses a *different literal value* for that tag (e.g. `kind: "circle"` vs. `kind: "square"`). Checking the tag instantly narrows to exactly one member.

A **literal type** means the type is one specific value, not the whole category — `"circle"` (only that string), not `string` (any string).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Real domains are "one of these shapes": an API result is **loading**, **success** (has `data`), or **error** (has `message`); a shape is a **circle** (has `r`) or a **square** (has `side`). If you model these as one big object with everything optional, you get *illegal states* — a "loading" result that somehow also has an error message — and endless `if (result.data)` guards. A discriminated union makes each shape **its own member**, so the impossible combinations *can't even be written*.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Give every member the same key (`kind`, `type`, `status` — any name) with a **distinct literal value**. A `switch` (or `if`) on that key narrows the whole object:

```
   Shape = {kind:"circle";r} | {kind:"square";side}
                  │
          switch (s.kind)
          ┌───────┴────────┐
     case "circle"     case "square"
     s.kind="circle"   s.kind="square"
     s.r available     s.side available
```

Because the tags are *disjoint* literals, knowing `s.kind === "circle"` lets the compiler rule out every other member — so `s.r` becomes available and `s.side` becomes an error (it doesn't exist on a circle).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **shipping form** with a checkbox at the top: *Letter*, *Parcel*, or *Pallet*. Tick "Parcel" and the form reveals **only** the parcel fields (weight, dimensions) and hides the pallet fields. The checkbox is the discriminant; ticking it decides which set of fields is valid. You can never fill in "pallet height" on a form marked "Letter".

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Shape =
  | { kind: "circle"; r: number }
  | { kind: "square"; side: number };

function area(s: Shape): number {
  switch (s.kind) {
    case "circle":
      return Math.PI * s.r ** 2;   // s: { kind:"circle"; r:number }
    case "square":
      return s.side ** 2;          // s: { kind:"square"; side:number }
  }
}

area({ kind: "circle", r: 2 }); // 12.566...
area({ kind: "square", side: 3 }); // 9

// Trying to mix shapes is a compile-time ERROR:
// const bad: Shape = { kind: "circle", side: 4 };
//   ❌ Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'side'
//      does not exist in type '{ kind: "circle"; r: number; }'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Model an async API result as a discriminated union and explain why it beats a flat object.**
A:
```typescript
type Result<T> =
  | { status: "loading" }
  | { status: "success"; data: T }
  | { status: "error"; message: string };
```
A flat `{ data?, error?, loading? }` allows **illegal states** (success *and* error at once) and forces defensive `?.` everywhere. The tagged version makes each state a distinct shape: `data` exists *only* in `success`, `message` *only* in `error`. Narrowing on `status` then gives you exactly the fields that state owns — illegal combinations are unrepresentable.

---

### 🛂 User-Defined Type Guards — Custom Checks That Can *Lie*

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **user-defined type guard** is a function whose return type is a special **type predicate** written `arg is Foo`. When such a function returns `true`, the compiler narrows the argument you passed to `Foo` in the calling code. It lets you bottle up a complex check and reuse it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Built-in narrowing handles primitives and simple shapes, but sometimes "is this value a `User`?" takes several lines (check it's an object, check it has an `id`, check `id` is a string...). You can't inline that in every `if`. A type guard packages the check **once** and teaches the compiler what a `true` result *means* for the type.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? — and where it LIES
You write a normal boolean function, but annotate the return as `arg is Foo` instead of `boolean`:

```
   function isFoo(x): x is Foo { ...return boolean }
                       ▲                      │
                       │  the compiler        │
                       └── BELIEVES this ◀────┘
                          WITHOUT checking the body!
```

**Critical, interview-grade truth:** a type predicate is an **unchecked assertion**. The compiler does **not** verify that your function body actually proves `x is Foo`. If your logic is wrong, the compiler still trusts the predicate — and you get a type that's a *lie*, with no error until it explodes at runtime. The guard is only as sound as you make it.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **self-certified form**: "I declare this package contains no liquids — *signed, me*." Customs (the compiler) stamps it through based on your **signature**, not by opening the box. If you signed falsely, nothing catches it at the desk — the problem surfaces later, mid-flight. A type guard is you signing "this value *is* a `Foo`"; the compiler takes your word.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type User = { id: string; name: string };

// A CORRECT guard: the body genuinely proves the type.
function isUser(x: unknown): x is User {
  return (
    typeof x === "object" && x !== null &&
    "id" in x && typeof (x as User).id === "string" &&
    "name" in x && typeof (x as User).name === "string"
  );
}

// A LYING guard — compiles fine, but the check is wrong:
function isString(x: unknown): x is string {
  return typeof x === "number"; // ❌ logic bug, but NO compiler error here
}

const value: unknown = 42;
if (isString(value)) {
  // The compiler thinks value: string — but at RUNTIME it's the number 42.
  console.log(value.toUpperCase());
  // 💥 TypeError: value.toUpperCase is not a function
}
```
The lying guard type-checks cleanly. The compiler never inspects whether `typeof x === "number"` actually implies `x is string` — that's the unsafe part you own.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Write a type guard and explain when it "lies".**
A: A guard is a function returning `arg is T` (see `isUser` above). It "lies" because the predicate is an **unchecked assertion**: TypeScript trusts the `is T` annotation without verifying the body proves it. If the body's logic is wrong (or drifts out of sync with `T` as the type evolves), the compiler keeps trusting it, so you get an unsound `T` and a runtime crash. Guards must be reviewed like any safety-critical code — ideally generated from a schema validator so the check can't drift from the type.

---

### 🚨 Assertion Functions — Narrow by Throwing

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **assertion function** narrows a value as a **side effect** of *not throwing*. Instead of returning a boolean, it returns `void` but is annotated with `asserts ...`. If it returns normally, the compiler narrows; if the condition fails, the function throws and execution never continues. There are two forms: `asserts arg is Foo` (narrow `arg` to `Foo`) and `asserts condition` (narrow based on a boolean you pass in).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes you don't want to branch — you want to **demand** a value is correct and bail out loudly if it isn't (think: validating input at the top of a function, like a "this must be true or we stop"). With a type guard you'd write `if (!isUser(x)) throw ...; /* now narrowed */`. An assertion function folds that into **one line** and reads like a precondition.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The magic is the **return type annotation**, and the rule the compiler enforces: *the function must throw if the assertion is false* (you write that throw; the compiler doesn't check the logic — same unchecked-assertion caveat as guards).

```
  assertIsString(x)
        │
   throws? ──yes──▶ execution STOPS
        │           (lines below unreachable)
        no
        ▼
   x is now string for the REST of the scope
   (no branch — narrowing flows straight down)
```

Unlike a type guard (which narrows *inside an `if`*), an assertion narrows **everything after the call** in a straight line.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **security checkpoint you must pass to board**. You don't get a "true/false" slip — you either **pass and walk straight to the gate** (narrowed, the rest of the journey assumes you're cleared) or you're **stopped at the checkpoint** and your trip ends right there. There's no "carry on but unverified."

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Form 1: asserts arg is Foo
function assertIsString(x: unknown): asserts x is string {
  if (typeof x !== "string") throw new Error("Expected a string");
}

function shout(v: unknown): string {
  assertIsString(v);
  return v.toUpperCase();   // v: string for the rest of the function
}

// Form 2: asserts condition (narrows based on the boolean you pass)
function assert(cond: unknown, msg: string): asserts cond {
  if (!cond) throw new Error(msg);
}

function lengthOf(x: string | null): number {
  assert(x !== null, "x must not be null");
  return x.length;          // x: string (null ruled out by the assert)
}

shout("hi");      // "HI"
lengthOf("abc");  // 3
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How does an assertion function differ from a type guard?**
A: A **type guard** (`arg is T`) returns a *boolean* and narrows only **inside the branch** where it's `true` — you still write the `if`. An **assertion function** (`asserts arg is T`) returns `void`, **throws** when the condition fails, and narrows **everything after the call** with no branch. Use a guard when you want to handle both cases; use an assertion when an invalid value is a hard error that should stop execution. Both are *unchecked* — the compiler trusts your annotation without verifying the body.

---

### 🧱 Exhaustiveness Checking with `never` — The `default` That Can't Happen

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Exhaustiveness checking** is a trick that makes the compiler **fail the build** if you forget to handle a member of a union. It works by assigning the value, in the unreachable `default` branch, to the type **`never`** — the type with *no values at all* (the "this can never happen" type).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You handle every case of a discriminated union today. Six months later a teammate adds a new member (`{ kind: "triangle"; ... }`). Your `switch` silently doesn't handle it — no error, just a wrong result or `undefined` at runtime. Exhaustiveness checking turns "I forgot a case" from a **silent runtime bug** into a **compile-time error**, the moment the union grows.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
In the `default` case, all known members have been peeled off by the earlier `case`s, so the compiler narrows the value to **`never`**. Assigning it to a `never`-typed variable is fine *while every case is handled*. Add a new member and that member is **not** `never` in `default` — so the assignment errors and the build breaks.

```
  every case handled?
        │
      yes ──▶ in default, value is: never
              const _exhaustive: never = value;  ✅ compiles
        │
       no (new member added)
        │
        └──▶ in default, value is: {kind:"triangle"...}
             const _exhaustive: never = value;  ❌ ERROR
             "Type '{kind:"triangle"...}' is not
              assignable to type 'never'."
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **pre-flight checklist where the last line reads "all boxes ticked — nothing left."** If someone adds a new switch to the cockpit but forgets to add it to the checklist, the "nothing left" line is now a lie — and the inspector refuses to sign off. The `never` assignment is that final "nothing left should remain" line; a new unhandled case makes it fail.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Shape =
  | { kind: "circle"; r: number }
  | { kind: "square"; side: number };

function area(s: Shape): number {
  switch (s.kind) {
    case "circle":
      return Math.PI * s.r ** 2;
    case "square":
      return s.side ** 2;
    default: {
      // All cases handled → s is narrowed to `never` here.
      const _exhaustive: never = s;   // ✅ compiles today
      return _exhaustive;
    }
  }
}

// LATER someone adds a third member:
//   | { kind: "triangle"; base: number; height: number };
// Now the default no longer narrows to never, and the compiler reports:
//   error TS2322: Type '{ kind: "triangle"; base: number;
//     height: number; }' is not assignable to type 'never'.
// → the build breaks until you add a `case "triangle"`.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you guarantee a `switch` over a union handles every case?**
A: Add a `default` branch that assigns the switched value to a `never`-typed variable: `const _exhaustive: never = value;`. When all cases are handled, the value narrows to `never` and it compiles. If a new union member is added and not handled, that member reaches `default` *not* narrowed to `never`, so the assignment fails to compile — a **compile-time** guarantee that every case is covered. (You can wrap it in a helper like `assertNever(x: never): never { throw new Error(...) }`.)

---

### ⚠️ Narrowing Pitfalls — Aliasing, Closures & the Soundness Hole

#### 🧩 What is it?
Narrowing is powerful but **not bulletproof**. There are three situations where your mental "it's narrowed" and the compiler's view diverge — or where the compiler narrows but the *runtime* doesn't actually hold the narrowed value. Knowing these saves you from confusing errors and silent bugs.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You'll hit `'x' is possibly 'null'` even though "I literally just checked it" — or worse, code that compiles but crashes. These come from **aliasing** (checking one binding, using another), **closures** (narrowing not flowing into a nested function that could be called later), and the **function-call soundness hole** (the compiler keeps a property narrowed across a call that could secretly change it).

#### ⚙️ How does it work? — the three traps
**1. Aliasing.** Narrow `w.v`, then use a *copy* `alias` — the compiler narrowed `w.v`, not `alias`. Fix: narrow the binding you actually use.

**2. Closure narrowing-loss.** If a variable is **reassigned anywhere in the scope**, the compiler can't assume its narrowed type inside a nested function (which might run after the reassignment), so it falls back to the **declared** type.

**3. Function-call invalidation (the unsound part).** The compiler keeps a narrowed **object property** narrowed across a function call — even if that function could set the property to `null`. This is a deliberate **soundness hole**: it compiles, but can crash at runtime.

```
   narrow b.value to string
          │
      mutate()  ← could set b.value = null at runtime
          │
   b.value still typed `string`  (compiler does NOT recheck)
          │
   b.value.length  → compiles, may 💥 at runtime
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
You photocopy your passport (`alias`) and verify the **copy** is valid — but at the gate they scan the **original**, which expired (aliasing: wrong binding). Or you tell a friend "the door is unlocked," then **lock it** before they arrive — your earlier statement is now false (closure/function-call: the value changed after the check). The check was true *when you made it*, not necessarily *when it's used*.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// PITFALL 1 — aliasing: narrow w.v but use a separate `alias`
type Wrap = { v: string | null };
function aliasFail(w: Wrap): number {
  const alias = w.v;
  if (w.v !== null) {        // narrows w.v, NOT alias
    return alias.length;     // ❌ error: 'alias' is possibly 'null'
  }
  return 0;
}
// Fix: narrow the binding you use → `if (alias !== null) { alias.length }`

// PITFALL 2 — closure: x is reassigned in scope, so narrowing
// does NOT flow into the nested function.
function closureTrap(x: string | number): number {
  if (typeof x === "string") {
    const f = () => x.length; // ❌ 'length' does not exist on 'string | number'
    x = 42;                   // because x is reassigned somewhere
    return f();
  }
  return 0;
}

// PITFALL 3 — the soundness hole: narrowing survives a call
// that can secretly invalidate it. This COMPILES but is unsafe.
const box: { value: string | null } = { value: "x" };
function clear() { box.value = null; }
function unsafe(): number {
  if (box.value !== null) {
    clear();                  // sets box.value = null at runtime!
    return box.value.length;  // compiles — 💥 crashes at runtime
  }
  return 0;
}
```
> **Note (simplification):** narrowing a **`const`** is the safest case — a `const` can't be reassigned, so its narrowing is stable. Most trouble comes from mutable bindings (`let`) and mutable object **properties**. Prefer copying a narrowed value into a `const` before a function call when you need it to stay narrowed.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: You wrote `if (obj.value !== null)` then called a function and used `obj.value` — it compiled but crashed at runtime. Why?**
A: TypeScript's control-flow analysis keeps an object **property** narrowed across a function call, even though the called function could reassign that property (here, to `null`). It's a known, **deliberate unsoundness** — the compiler trades it for ergonomics. The fix is to copy the property into a local `const` *after* narrowing and use that, or re-check after the call: a `const` can't change under you, so the narrowed type stays true.

---

<h2 id="module-3">Module 3 — Functions, Generics & Variance</h2>

Functions are where TypeScript earns its keep — and where its trickiest rules live. This module answers three big questions: how do you type a function precisely (optional, default, rest, and `this` parameters)? How do you make a function work for *many* types at once with **generics** — code parameterised by a type? And the deep one most engineers can't explain: when is it *safe* to substitute one function type for another — the topic of **variance** 🧠 (which direction types are allowed to flex). Get these right and you can design APIs that infer the perfect type at every call site.

---

### 🎛️ Function Types & Parameters — Optional, Default, Rest & `this`

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **function type** describes a function's shape: what parameters it takes and what it returns — for example `` `(x: number) => string` `` ("takes a number, returns a string"). TypeScript also lets you mark parameters as **optional** (may be omitted), give them **defaults** (a fallback value), gather extras into a **rest** parameter (an array of the leftovers), and even declare what `this` must be inside the function.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Real functions aren't all "fixed list of required arguments". A logger might take an optional tag; a `greet` might default to "Hello"; a `sum` might take *any number* of values. And callbacks ripped off an object (`const f = obj.method`) can call `this` on the wrong thing. Without precise parameter typing, the compiler can't catch a wrong call or a misused `this`.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **Optional** `` `param?: T` `` — caller may omit it; inside, its type is `` `T | undefined` ``. Optional params must come **after** required ones.
- **Default** `` `param = value` `` — if the caller omits it (or passes `undefined`), the default is used. A defaulted param is automatically optional for callers.
- **Rest** `` `...rest: T[]` `` — collects all remaining arguments into an array; must be **last**.
- **`this` parameter** — a *fake* first parameter named `this`. It is **erased** at compile time (it is not a real argument); it only tells the checker what `this` must be when the function runs.

```
   greet(name: string, greeting = "Hi", ...tags: string[])
          │                │                   │
       required         default            rest -> string[]
                       (optional)        (must be last param)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A coffee order form. Some fields are **required** (size). Some are **optional** (extra shot — leave blank). Some have a **default** printed faintly ("milk: whole" unless you cross it out). And "**...tags**" is the blank notes line where any number of extra requests go. The **`this`** parameter is the rule "this form is only valid at *this* branch" — it doesn't add a field, it just constrains where the form can be used.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// optional (greeting?), default (loud), rest (...tags)
function greet(name: string, greeting = "Hi", ...tags: string[]): string {
  const banner = `${greeting}, ${name}!`;
  return tags.length ? `${banner} [${tags.join(", ")}]` : banner;
}

greet("Ada");                       // "Hi, Ada!"        (defaults used)
greet("Ada", "Hello");              // "Hello, Ada!"
greet("Ada", "Hey", "vip", "new");  // "Hey, Ada! [vip, new]"

// `this` parameter: declares what `this` must be (erased at runtime)
interface Deck { cards: number[]; pick(this: Deck): number; }
const deck: Deck = {
  cards: [1, 2, 3],
  pick(this: Deck) { return this.cards[0]; }, // `this` is typed as Deck
};
deck.pick(); // 1  -- called as a method, `this` is the deck
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does a `this` parameter do, and does it cost a runtime argument?**
A: It is a **compile-time-only** annotation written as the first parameter named `this`. It tells the type-checker what `this` must be inside the function (so calling the method on the wrong object is a type error), but it is **erased** during compilation — it adds no real argument, and callers still pass their normal arguments starting from the second slot.

---

### 🔀 Overloads vs Union Params vs Generics — Three Tools, One Decision

#### 🧩 What is it?
