Three ways to make one function accept more than one kind of input:
- **Overloads** — multiple call *signatures* (the public shapes) over one hidden implementation.
- **Union parameter** — a single signature whose parameter is `` `A | B` `` ("either A or B").
- **Generic** — a single signature with a *type variable* `` `<T>` `` that adapts to whatever you pass.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A function like `makeDate` might mean "from a timestamp" *or* "from month/day/year" — but **not** a meaningless mix like two arguments. A union param can't express "1 arg or 3 args, never 2". A generic can't restrict the *combinations* of arguments. Picking the wrong tool either lets bad calls through or makes the return type vague (`A | B` when the caller knows it's `A`).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Decision guide:

```
   Do the argument SHAPES come in fixed combos
   (e.g. 1 arg OR 3 args, never 2)?
        │ yes                        │ no
        ▼                            ▼
     OVERLOADS              Is the OUTPUT type linked
   (list each combo)        to the INPUT type?
                                 │ yes          │ no
                                 ▼              ▼
                              GENERIC       UNION PARAM
                          (T flows in→out)  (just "either")
```
- **Overloads:** write the call signatures above one *implementation* signature. The implementation signature is **not** callable from outside — only the listed overloads are.
- **Generic:** use when input type determines output type, e.g. `` `identity<T>(x: T): T` `` — pass a `string`, get a `string` back (not `string | number`).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A vending machine. **Overloads** are the fixed button combos on the front panel — "B4" and "C2" are valid, but you physically *can't* press a button that doesn't exist (no 2-arg `makeDate`). A **union param** is one slot that accepts a coin *or* a note — both work, the machine doesn't care which. A **generic** is the refund chute: whatever currency you put in is exactly what comes back out — your `string` returns as a `string`, never widened.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// OVERLOADS: two valid arg-combos (1 arg OR 3 args), never 2.
function makeDate(timestamp: number): Date;
function makeDate(m: number, d: number, y: number): Date;
function makeDate(mOrTs: number, d?: number, y?: number): Date { // impl: hidden
  return d !== undefined && y !== undefined
    ? new Date(y, mOrTs, d)
    : new Date(mOrTs);
}
makeDate(1700000000000);   // OK (timestamp overload)
makeDate(5, 5, 2025);      // OK (m/d/y overload)
// makeDate(1, 3);
//   ERROR TS2575: No overload expects 2 arguments, but overloads do
//   exist that expect either 1 or 3 arguments.

// GENERIC: output type is tied to input type.
function identity<T>(x: T): T { return x; }
const s = identity("hi");  // s: "hi"  (a string, NOT string | number)

// UNION PARAM: "either", but output isn't tied to which one came in.
function len(x: string | unknown[]): number { return x.length; }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When do you reach for an overload instead of a generic?**
A: Use an **overload** when the valid argument *combinations* are fixed and unrelated — e.g. "one argument or three, never two" — so each shape gets its own signature and bad combos won't type-check. Use a **generic** when the output type is *derived from* the input type and you want that relationship preserved (pass a `string`, get a `string`, not a widened union). If you only need "accept either type" with no link to the result, a plain **union parameter** is simplest.

---

### 🧬 Generics — One Function, Many Types

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **generic** is code parameterised by a **type variable** (a placeholder type, written `` `<T>` ``) that gets filled in per call. It lets you write `` `first<T>(arr: T[]): T` `` once and have it work for `number[]`, `string[]`, anything — while remembering the exact element type each time.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Without generics you'd either copy-paste the same function per type, or type it with `any` and **lose all safety** (`any` turns the checker off). A generic gives you reuse *and* precision: the type flows through the function so the return type stays exact.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **Type parameter** `` `<T>` `` — declared in angle brackets; usually **inferred** from the arguments, so you rarely write it at the call.
- **Constraint** `` `<T extends U>` `` — "T must be at least a U". This lets you safely use U's members inside, while still preserving T's narrower type. *(Constraint = an upper bound on what T may be.)*
- **Default type parameter** `` `<T = Fallback>` `` — used when T can't be inferred and the caller doesn't supply it.

```
   <T extends { length: number }>  --- the CONSTRAINT
        │  guarantees T has .length
        ▼
   logLength(item: T): T   --- still returns the EXACT T,
                               not the wider constraint type
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A generic is a **shipping box that remembers its label**. The box (`first<T>`) works for any contents, but it stamps the label of whatever you packed — open it and you get back *exactly* shoes, not "some unknown item". A **constraint** is a shipping rule: "contents must have a weight tag" (`extends { length }`) — the courier can read the weight, but the box still remembers it's shoes specifically. A **default** is the pre-printed label used when you forget to write one.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Type parameter, usually inferred:
function first<T>(arr: T[]): T | undefined { return arr[0]; }
const n = first([1, 2, 3]);     // n: number | undefined  (T inferred = number)
const w = first(["a", "b"]);    // w: string | undefined

// Constraint: T must have a `length`; we can read .length safely,
// and we still RETURN the exact T (not the wide constraint type).
function longest<T extends { length: number }>(a: T, b: T): T {
  return a.length >= b.length ? a : b;
}
const arr = longest([1, 2], [1, 2, 3]); // arr: number[]  (exact T kept)
// longest(10, 20);
//   ERROR TS2345: number is not assignable to { length: number }

// Default type parameter: used when T can't be inferred.
function makeBox<T = string>(): T[] { return []; }
const box = makeBox();          // box: string[]  (default kicked in)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `T extends U` give you that a plain `T` doesn't?**
A: It adds a **constraint** — an upper bound guaranteeing every `T` is at least a `U`, so inside the function you may safely use `U`'s members (e.g. read `.length`). Crucially it still **preserves the narrower `T`** at the call site: a constrained generic returns the exact type passed in, whereas just typing the param as `U` would widen the result and lose that precision.

---

### 🔎 Inference — How TS Guesses Your Type Arguments (and `NoInfer`)

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Inference** is the compiler *figuring out* a generic's type argument for you from the values you pass, so you almost never write `` `<T>` `` explicitly. **`NoInfer<T>`** (a built-in utility) is a way to tell the compiler: "use T here, but **don't** learn T from *this* position."

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Ergonomics: `first([1, 2, 3])` should just *know* `T` is `number` — forcing `first<number>([1,2,3])` everywhere would be miserable. But sometimes inference is *too* eager: a default value gets folded into the inferred type and **widens** it, silently accepting input you wanted to reject. `NoInfer` plugs that leak.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
TS gathers **candidates** for each type parameter from every spot it appears in the arguments, then picks a best common type. `NoInfer<C>` removes that spot from the candidate pool — the type is still *checked* there, just not used to *decide* `C`.

```
   createStreetLight(colors: C[], fallback: NoInfer<C>)
                        │              │
              C inferred HERE    NOT a candidate for C
              = "red"|"yellow"   (only checked against it)
              |"green"

   pass fallback "blue"  ->  checked against the inferred C
                             ->  "blue" not in union  ->  ERROR
```
Without `NoInfer`, `"blue"` would *widen* C to include `"blue"` and the bug would slip through.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Inference is a **waiter guessing your order's size from what you point at**. Usually helpful. But imagine the *default side dish* could also vote on the meal's "theme" — order a salad as your fallback and suddenly the kitchen decides the whole meal is "salads", letting through dishes you never wanted. `NoInfer` is the rule "the fallback side **doesn't get a vote** on the theme — it just has to *fit* the theme already decided by the mains."

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// WITHOUT NoInfer: the default value widens C and hides the bug.
function bad<C extends string>(colors: C[], fallback: C) {}
bad(["red", "green"], "blue");  // NO error — C widened to include "blue" 😬

// WITH NoInfer: fallback must be one of the inferred colors.
function streetLight<C extends string>(
  colors: C[],
  fallback: NoInfer<C>,
) {}
streetLight(["red", "yellow", "green"], "red");   // OK
// streetLight(["red", "yellow", "green"], "blue");
//   ERROR TS2345: Argument of type '"blue"' is not assignable to
//   parameter of type '"red" | "green" | "yellow"'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What problem does `NoInfer<T>` solve?**
A: It blocks a parameter position from contributing **candidates** to a generic's inference, while still type-checking against it. The classic case is a default/fallback argument that would otherwise *widen* the inferred type to include itself — `NoInfer` makes the fallback merely have to *fit* the type inferred elsewhere, so an out-of-range value is correctly rejected instead of silently expanding the type.

---

### 🧠 Variance — Which Way Types Are Allowed to Flex

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Variance** is the rule for *substitutability*: if `Dog` is a subtype of `Animal` (every Dog is an Animal), when is `Container<Dog>` usable where `Container<Animal>` is wanted — and when is the reverse true? The directions have names:
- **Covariant** — flexes the **same** way: `Dog` fits where `Animal` is wanted (outputs, return types, arrays).
- **Contravariant** — flexes the **opposite** way: an `Animal`-handler fits where a `Dog`-handler is wanted (function *parameters*).
- **Bivariant** — flexes **both** ways (unsoundly): the rule TS keeps for **method** parameters.

*(Subtype = a more specific type; supertype = a more general one. "Sound" = never lets a real runtime type error slip past.)*

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Substituting functions is everywhere — passing a callback, assigning a handler, storing functions in an array. Get the direction wrong and you either reject perfectly safe code, or accept code that crashes at runtime. Variance is the *exact* law for "is this swap safe?".

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
**Return types are covariant; parameter types are contravariant.** Intuition: a function you call is safe to replace if it **demands no more and promises no less**. It may accept a *wider* input (so it copes with anything you throw at it) and return a *narrower* output (so it gives you at least what you expected.)

```
   Want: (d: Dog) => Animal        wider IN, narrower OUT is safe
          ▲ param                  ───────────────────────────────
          │ CONTRAVARIANT          A replacement may take a SUPERtype
   Give:  (a: Animal) => Dog       in the param (Animal ⊇ Dog) ...
                    │ return
                    ▼ COVARIANT    ... and return a SUBtype (Dog ⊆ Animal)
```

**`strictFunctionTypes`** (a strict-mode flag) is what *enforces* the contravariant rule for parameters written as **function-type** properties. There is a deliberate hole:

> **Note (simplification):** With `strictFunctionTypes` ON, parameters are checked **contravariantly** — but **only** for params written in *function-type* position (a standalone `` `(x) => y` `` value or property). Parameters of a **method** written with method syntax (`` `compare(x): void` `` inside an interface) stay **bivariant** even under strict mode. TS keeps this hole on purpose so that, for example, `` `Array<Dog>` `` stays assignable to `` `Array<Animal>` `` (their methods like `push` take the element type as a method param).

```
   strictFunctionTypes: ON
   ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
   │ param in (x: T) => ...  prop   │ CONTRAVARIANT │
   │ param in  method(x: T): ...    │ BIVARIANT  ⚠  │
   └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Hiring a stand-in for a job. The job is "groom **Dogs**, hand back a **clean Animal**". A safe stand-in may be **over-qualified on intake** — willing to groom *any Animal* (wider input, contravariant) — and **more specific on output** — always hands back a *clean Dog* (narrower output, covariant). A stand-in who *only* grooms Chihuahuas (narrower input) is **unsafe**: the day a regular dog shows up, they choke. That's why parameters flex the opposite way to returns.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Animal = { name: string };
type Dog = { name: string; bark(): void };

// --- PARAMETERS: contravariant (verified, strictFunctionTypes ON) ---
type AnimalFn = (a: Animal) => void; // accepts ANY animal (wider)
type DogFn    = (d: Dog) => void;    // demands a Dog (narrower)
let df: DogFn = (d) => d.bark();
let af: AnimalFn = (a) => {};
df = af;  // OK: an Animal-handler copes with a Dog (wider input is safe)
// af = df;
//   ERROR TS2322: 'DogFn' is not assignable to 'AnimalFn' —
//   it would call .bark() on a plain Animal. (contravariance!)

// --- RETURNS: covariant (verified) ---
type GetAnimal = () => Animal;
type GetDog    = () => Dog;
let gd: GetDog = () => ({ name: "Rex", bark() {} });
let ga: GetAnimal = gd; // OK: a Dog IS an Animal (narrower output is safe)
// gd = ga;  ERROR: a GetAnimal might not return something with .bark()

// --- METHOD params stay BIVARIANT even under strict (verified) ---
interface AnimalCmp { compare(a: Animal): void; } // method syntax
interface DogCmp    { compare(d: Dog): void; }
let ac: AnimalCmp = { compare() {} };
let dc: DogCmp = { compare() {} };
ac = dc; // OK (bivariant hole) — would be an ERROR if written as a
dc = ac; // OK     function-type property instead of a method.

// --- ARRAYS: covariant but UNSOUND (verified) ---
const dogs: Dog[] = [{ name: "Rex", bark() {} }];
const animals: Animal[] = dogs;   // OK: Array is covariant
animals.push({ name: "Cat" });    // TS allows it — but now dogs[1]
// dogs[1].bark();                 // 💥 runtime crash: no bark()! (the hole)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Explain covariance vs contravariance with a function, and what `strictFunctionTypes` changes.**
A: For a function `` `(p: P) => R` ``, the **return** `R` is **covariant** (a replacement may return a *subtype* — "promises no less") and the **parameter** `P` is **contravariant** (a replacement may take a *supertype* — "demands no more"). `strictFunctionTypes` turns ON proper **contravariant** checking for parameters written in **function-type** position; before it (or for parameters declared with **method** syntax), params are checked **bivariantly** — accepted in either direction, which is unsound. TS deliberately keeps the method-param bivariance so that `` `Array<Dog>` `` stays assignable to `` `Array<Animal>` `` despite methods like `push` — the same convenient-but-unsound hole that lets you `push` a non-Dog into an array seen as `Animal[]`.

---

<h2 id="module-4">Module 4 — Objects, Interfaces & Classes</h2>

Most real-world data is shaped like an object — a user, an order, a config. This module is about *describing those shapes* precisely: which fields are optional, which can never change, which keys you don't even know yet. Then we climb up to classes — and discover the surprising truth that in TypeScript a class is **just its shape too**, with one carefully-guarded exception.

---

### 🧩 Object Types — Optional (`?`), `readonly` & the Shape of Data

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **object type** is a description of the keys an object has and the type of each value. TypeScript gives you two key modifiers: `?` marks a property **optional** (it may be missing), and `readonly` marks a property **unchangeable** after the object is built.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Real objects aren't all-or-nothing. A user *must* have an `id` but *might* have a `nickname`. And some fields — a database `id`, a frozen config — should never be reassigned after creation. Without these modifiers you'd be forced to either lie (mark everything required) or give up safety (mark everything `any`). `?` and `readonly` let the type say exactly what's true.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- `name: string` — **required**. The object is invalid without it.
- `nickname?: string` — **optional**. Its type becomes `string | undefined`, and you must check before using it.
- `readonly id: number` — assignable once (when the object is created), then locked. Reassigning is a **compile-time** error.

```
   type User = {
     name: string        ← must be present
     nickname?: string    ← may be missing  (string | undefined)
     readonly id: number  ← set once, then frozen
   }
```

> **Note (simplification):** `readonly` is enforced only by the compiler. At runtime the property is a normal, writable field — nothing stops plain JavaScript from changing it. For true runtime immutability you need `Object.freeze`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **paper form** at a clinic. "Full name" is a required box (`name`). "Preferred name" is an optional box you can leave blank (`nickname?`). Your "patient ID", once stamped on top, is printed in permanent ink — you can read it forever but never rewrite it (`readonly id`).

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type User = {
  name: string;
  nickname?: string;       // optional → type is string | undefined
  readonly id: number;     // can't be reassigned later
};

const u: User = { name: "Ada", id: 1 }; // OK — nickname omitted
console.log(u.nickname);                 // undefined

// u.id = 2;
// Error TS2540: Cannot assign to 'id' because it is a read-only property.

// Using an optional safely:
const label = u.nickname ?? u.name;      // "Ada"  (?? falls back when undefined)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What type does an optional property actually have, and what does `readonly` protect against?**
A: An optional property `nickname?: string` has the type `string | undefined`, so the compiler forces you to handle the "missing" case before using it. `readonly` blocks **reassignment at compile time** — but note it's *shallow* (the value it points to can still be mutated) and erased at runtime, so it's a type-checker guard, not a runtime lock.

---

### 🗂️ Index Signatures — Typing Objects With Unknown Keys

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **index signature** lets you describe an object whose **keys you don't know in advance**, but whose values all share a type. You write it as `{ [key: string]: ValueType }`, meaning "any string key maps to a `ValueType`".

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes the keys are *data*, not fixed field names — a dictionary of scores keyed by student name, a cache keyed by URL, a translations map keyed by language code. You can't list those keys ahead of time. An index signature says "I don't know the exact keys, but every value is a `number`."

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The key type can be `string`, `number`, or `symbol`. Every property access is then assumed to return the value type — even for a key that isn't actually there.

```
   type Scores = { [studentName: string]: number }

   scores["alice"]  ──► number   (key exists)
   scores["ghost"]  ──► number   (key MISSING — but TS still says number!)
```

> **Note (simplification):** By default TypeScript *trusts* the index signature and reports `number` even for absent keys — so `scores.ghost` is typed `number` but is actually `undefined` at runtime. The `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` strict flag fixes this by adding `| undefined` to every indexed read, forcing you to check.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
An index signature is a **mailbox wall in an apartment building**. You don't know every tenant's name in advance, but you know the rule: *each labelled slot holds letters*. Ask for a slot that doesn't exist and the basic system still hands you "a slot" — only a stricter front desk (`noUncheckedIndexedAccess`) warns "that tenant may not live here."

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Scores = { [studentName: string]: number };

const scores: Scores = { alice: 90, bob: 75 };
console.log(scores.alice);   // 90

const ghost = scores.charlie;
// Default strict: `ghost` is typed `number`, but its real value is undefined.
// console.log(ghost.toFixed()); // compiles, but CRASHES at runtime:
//   TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'toFixed')

// With --noUncheckedIndexedAccess, the same read is typed number | undefined:
//   error TS18048: 'ghost' is possibly 'undefined'.  ← now TS forces a check
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the danger of an index signature, and how do you make it safe?**
A: By default TypeScript assumes every key is present, so reading a missing key is typed as the value type but is `undefined` at runtime — a classic crash. Enable `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, which makes every indexed access `Value | undefined`, forcing you to narrow (check for `undefined`) before use.

---

### 🚧 Excess Property Checks — The "Fresh Object Literal" Rule

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **excess property check** is a special, extra-strict check TypeScript runs **only** when you assign a brand-new object literal (a `{ ... }` written right there) directly to a typed slot. It flags any property that isn't part of the target type — even though structural typing would normally allow "extra" fields.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Structural typing says "more is fine" — an object with extra fields still *fits* a smaller type. But that's dangerous for **typos in literals**: if you write `{ color: "red", colour: "blue" }` for a type that wants `color`, the `colour` typo would silently be ignored. Excess property checks catch exactly this: a freshly-written literal shouldn't contain fields the type never mentioned, because that almost always means a mistake.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The key word is **freshness**. A literal is "fresh" the moment you write it. Assign it *directly* and TypeScript checks for excess keys. But the moment you store it in a variable first, the object **loses its freshness** — now it's "just some object," and ordinary structural rules (extra props allowed) apply.

```
   FRESH literal assigned directly      → excess-property check RUNS
   ┌────────────────────────────┐
   │ const b: Btn =             │
   │   { color:"red", shadow:1 }│ ──► ERROR: 'shadow' not in Btn
   └────────────────────────────┘

   Same object via a variable           → check is SKIPPED
   ┌────────────────────────────┐
   │ const raw = {color,shadow} │  ← freshness lost here
   │ const b: Btn = raw         │ ──► OK (structural: extra props fine)
   └────────────────────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's airport security for **carry-on bags you packed yourself at the desk**. Hand a freshly-packed bag straight to the agent (fresh literal) and they inspect every item — an unexpected object gets flagged. But if you'd already checked the bag into the system earlier (stored in a variable), it rides through on the conveyor without that face-to-face inspection.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Btn = { color: string };

// 1) FRESH literal, assigned directly → checked:
// const a: Btn = { color: "red", shadow: true };
// error TS2353: Object literal may only specify known properties,
//   and 'shadow' does not exist in type 'Btn'.

// 2) Same shape, but through a variable → freshness lost → allowed:
const raw = { color: "red", shadow: true };
const b: Btn = raw;   // OK — `raw` is no longer a fresh literal

// 3) Passing a fresh literal as an argument is ALSO checked:
function paint(btn: Btn) {}
// paint({ color: "red", shadow: true });
// error TS2353: ... 'shadow' does not exist in type 'Btn'.
paint(raw);           // OK — variable, not fresh
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is an excess property check, and why does assigning through a variable bypass it?**
A: It's an extra check TypeScript runs only on **fresh object literals** (a `{ ... }` written at the assignment/argument site) to catch typos and stray fields the target type doesn't declare. Assigning the literal to a variable first **strips its "freshness"**, after which normal structural typing applies — and structural typing happily allows an object with *extra* properties to satisfy a type that needs fewer. So the variable path is allowed by design, not by accident.

---

### 🧬 `interface extends` vs Intersection (`&`) — Two Ways to Combine Shapes

#### 🧩 What is it?
Both let you build a bigger type out of smaller ones. `interface B extends A` declares a new interface that **inherits** everything in `A` and adds more. An **intersection** `A & B` is a type-alias operator meaning "has all properties of `A` *and* all of `B`."

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You constantly want to reuse a base shape: every `Dog` is an `Animal` plus a `breed`. Re-typing the base fields everywhere is error-prone. Composition (`extends` or `&`) keeps one source of truth and layers extras on top.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
They mostly produce the same result, but differ on the edges:
- `extends` only works on **interfaces** (and TypeScript checks at declaration time that the child is compatible with the parent).
- `&` works on **any types** (unions, primitives, objects) and is how you compose `type` aliases.
- **Conflict handling differs.** If two parents declare the same key with *incompatible* types, `extends` reports an **error at the declaration**, while `&` silently combines them into the impossible type `never`.

```
   interface Dog extends Animal { breed: string }
   type    DogT  = Animal & { breed: string }
            both ──►  { name: string; breed: string }

   conflict on `x`:
   { x: string } & { x: number }  ──►  x: never   (silent, unbuildable)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`extends` is **filling out an upgraded membership form** that already inherits all the base-membership fields — the clerk checks your new form is consistent with the base before issuing it. `&` is **stapling two forms together** and declaring "this person satisfies both" — staple two forms that demand contradictory things (age 30 *and* age 40 for the same box) and you've created a card no real person can ever hold (`never`).

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// extends — interfaces only
interface Animal { name: string; }
interface Dog extends Animal { breed: string; }
const d: Dog = { name: "Rex", breed: "Lab" }; // OK

// intersection — works on any types
type AnimalT = { name: string };
type DogT = AnimalT & { breed: string };
const d2: DogT = { name: "Rex", breed: "Lab" }; // OK

// Conflict: intersection produces an impossible `never`-typed property
type Conflict = { x: string } & { x: number };
// `x` is now `string & number` = never; you can never supply a valid value.
// const bad: Conflict = { x: "hi" }; // error: string not assignable to never
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: `interface extends` vs `&` — when do they behave differently?**
A: For combining plain object shapes they're nearly identical. Differences: `extends` is interface-only and verifies compatibility *at the declaration* (a conflicting override is a clear error), while `&` works on any types and, on a property conflict, silently yields an impossible `never` type. Interfaces also support **declaration merging** (re-opening to add fields); `type` aliases don't.

---

### 🔐 Access Modifiers (`public`/`protected`/`private`) vs Runtime `#private`

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Access modifiers** are TypeScript keywords that control who can touch a class member: `public` (anyone), `protected` (this class and subclasses), `private` (this class only). Separately, JavaScript itself has `#private` fields — names starting with `#` — that are truly hidden at **runtime**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You want to hide internals so callers don't depend on them and so invariants stay safe. But there's a crucial catch: TypeScript's `private` is **a compile-time fiction** — it vanishes after compilation, so plain JavaScript (or `(obj as any)`) can still reach the field. `#private` was added to JavaScript to give *real*, unbreakable privacy.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- `public` / `protected` / `private` are **erased** during compilation. They guard your *TypeScript* code only. The emitted JS field is a normal, fully accessible property.
- `#field` is part of the **JavaScript language**. It survives compilation and is enforced by the engine — accessing it from outside the class is a hard error, even at runtime.

```
   TS `private pin`            JS `#secret`
   ───────────────            ───────────────
   compile-time only          runtime-enforced
   erased after build         survives into the build
   (obj as any).pin works ✔   obj.#secret → throws ✗
```

> **Note (simplification):** "TS `private` does nothing at runtime" is the headline. In emitted JS the field is a regular property — reachable via bracket access or a cast. Only `#private` is genuinely inaccessible from outside.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
TS `private` is a **"Staff Only" sign on an unlocked door** — it tells honest people to keep out, but the door still opens if someone ignores it. `#private` is **a door with an actual deadbolt** — the sign is irrelevant; you physically cannot get in.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
class Account {
  public owner: string;
  protected balance: number;   // this class + subclasses
  private pin: string;         // this class only (compile-time)
  constructor(owner: string) {
    this.owner = owner; this.balance = 0; this.pin = "0000";
  }
}
const a = new Account("Ada");
console.log(a.owner);          // "Ada"
// console.log(a.balance);
//   error TS2445: Property 'balance' is protected and only accessible
//   within class 'Account' and its subclasses.
// console.log(a.pin);
//   error TS2341: Property 'pin' is private and only accessible within class 'Account'.
console.log((a as any).pin);   // "0000"  ← TS private is reachable at runtime!

class Vault {
  #secret = 42;                // real, runtime-enforced privacy
  reveal() { return this.#secret; }
}
const v = new Vault();
console.log(v.reveal());       // 42
// console.log(v.#secret);
//   error TS18013: Property '#secret' is not accessible outside class 'Vault'
//   because it has a private identifier.   (and it throws at runtime too)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: TS `private` vs JS `#private` — what's the runtime difference?**
A: TypeScript's `private` is **compile-time only**; it's erased on build, so the field is a normal JS property reachable via a cast like `(obj as any).pin` or bracket access. JavaScript's `#private` is **runtime-enforced** by the engine — it survives compilation and throws if accessed from outside the class. Use `#` when you need *real* encapsulation (e.g. protecting an invariant from untrusted callers).

---

### 🏛️ Class Machinery — `abstract`, `implements`, Parameter Properties, `override`

#### 🧩 What is it?
A toolkit of class features: `abstract` (a base class/method that *must* be completed by a subclass and can't be instantiated directly), `implements` (a promise that a class satisfies an interface), **parameter properties** (a shorthand that declares and assigns a field straight from the constructor), and `override` (an explicit marker that a method replaces a base-class one).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
- `abstract` defines a contract with a partial implementation — "every `Shape` must provide `area()`, but I'll give you `describe()` for free."
- `implements` checks a class against an interface so you catch missing methods early.
- Parameter properties kill the boilerplate of "declare a field, then copy the constructor arg into it."
- `override` (with `noImplicitOverride`) prevents two bugs: silently shadowing a base method, and a typo that *fails* to override the method you meant to.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
   abstract class Shape { abstract area(): number }  ← can't `new Shape()`
        ▲
        │ extends + must implement area()
   class Circle extends Shape {
     constructor(private radius: number) {...}  ← parameter property:
                                                   declares + sets this.radius
     area() { ... }
     override describe() { ... }  ← explicit: I'm replacing Shape.describe
   }
```
Also relevant: **`strictPropertyInitialization`** (part of `strict`) requires every declared field to be assigned in the constructor (or have an initializer / `?`), so you never read an `undefined` field you thought was set.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`abstract` is a **recipe template** that says "every dessert must define a `bake()` step" but refuses to be served as-is — you can't eat "Dessert," only a concrete `Cake`. `implements` is a **job description** the class signs ("I will provide `log()`"). A parameter property is the **express checkout** that registers and shelves an item in one motion. `override` is **initialling the exact clause you're changing** in a contract so nobody mistakes which line you meant.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
abstract class Shape {
  abstract area(): number;                  // subclasses MUST provide this
  describe() { return `area=${this.area()}`; }
}
// new Shape();
//   error TS2511: Cannot create an instance of an abstract class.

class Circle extends Shape {
  constructor(private radius: number) { super(); } // parameter property
  area() { return Math.PI * this.radius ** 2; }
}
console.log(new Circle(2).area());          // 12.566370614359172

interface Logger { log(msg: string): void; }
class ConsoleLogger implements Logger {     // checked against Logger
  log(msg: string) { console.log(msg); }
}

class Base { greet() { return "base"; } }
class Derived extends Base {
  override greet() { return "derived"; }    // explicit override
}
// With noImplicitOverride, overriding WITHOUT `override` errors:
//   error TS4114: This member must have an 'override' modifier ...
// A typo'd `override gret()` errors too:
//   error TS4117: ... not declared in the base class 'Base'. Did you mean 'greet'?

class User { name: string; }                // (strictPropertyInitialization)
//   error TS2564: Property 'name' has no initializer and is not
//   definitely assigned in the constructor.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `strictPropertyInitialization` guard against, and how do you satisfy it?**
A: It ensures every declared class field is actually assigned — either by an initializer (`name = ""`), in the constructor, or marked optional (`name?`). Without it you can declare `name: string` yet leave it `undefined`, then crash reading it. Satisfy it by initializing the field, assigning it in the constructor, or — if you *know* it's set elsewhere — using the definite-assignment assertion `name!: string` (a deliberate opt-out you should use sparingly).

---

### 🧩 Structural Compatibility of Classes — A Class Is Just Its Shape

#### 🧩 What is it?
TypeScript is **structural**: two types are compatible if their *shapes* match, regardless of their names or where they came from. This applies to classes too — two completely unrelated classes with the same public members are assignable to each other. The one exception: classes that declare `private` or `#private` members.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
In nominal languages (Java, C#), a `Vector2D` is never a `Point2D` unless it explicitly says so. TypeScript instead asks "does it have the right shape?" — which makes types flow freely and makes mocking/duck-typing easy. But you sometimes *want* two look-alike classes to stay distinct; that's what the `private` exception is for.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- For public members, only the **shape** matters. `Vector2D` with `{ x, y }` satisfies `Point2D` with `{ x, y }` even though neither extends the other.
- The moment a class has a `private` (or `#private`) member, that member is tagged to **its specific declaration**. Another class's identical-looking private member is considered a *different* member — so the two classes are **not** compatible. This is TypeScript's one nominal escape hatch.

```
   class Point2D  { x; y }            ← same public shape
   class Vector2D { x; y }            → assignable BOTH ways ✔

   class Secret { private token }     ← private tied to THIS class
   class Other  { private token }     → NOT assignable ✗
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A house key works by **the shape of its teeth**, not the brand stamped on it — any key cut to the same profile opens the lock (structural). But a `private` field is like a **serial-numbered key registered to one specific lock**: even an identical-looking copy from a different registry is rejected, because the lock checks *which* registry it came from.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
class Point2D  { constructor(public x: number, public y: number) {} }
class Vector2D { constructor(public x: number, public y: number) {} }

let p: Point2D = new Vector2D(1, 2);  // OK! identical public shape
console.log(p.x, p.y);                // 1 2

class Secret { private token = "x"; }
class Other  { private token = "x"; } // looks identical...
// let sec: Secret = new Other();
//   error TS2322: Type 'Other' is not assignable to type 'Secret'.
//     Types have separate declarations of a private property 'token'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Are two unrelated classes with the same shape assignable in TypeScript?**
A: Yes — TypeScript is structural, so if their **public** members match they're mutually assignable even with no `extends` relationship. The exception is **`private`/`#private` members**: those are tied to their declaring class, so two classes with identically-named private fields are treated as distinct and are *not* assignable. That private-field rule is TypeScript's one bit of nominal typing.

---

### 🔗 `this` Types — Fluent Builders That Keep Their Type

#### 🧩 What is it?
The polymorphic **`this` type** is a special return type meaning "the actual type of the object the method was called on." Returning `this` from a method lets you **chain calls** (a fluent / builder API) while preserving the precise subclass type the whole way down the chain.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Builder APIs chain: `query.select(...).from(...).build()`. For chaining to work, each step must return the object itself. If you annotate the return as the base class, calling a *subclass* method later in the chain breaks — the type was downgraded. Returning `this` instead keeps the real type, so subclass methods stay available through the whole chain.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A method typed `(): this` returns "whatever type `this` is at the call site." On a base instance it's the base; on a subclass instance it's the subclass — automatically, with no extra generics.

```
   select(): this   from(): this   build(): string
        │ returns the SAME concrete type each hop
        ▼
   new AdminQuery().select("*").whereAdmin().build()
                    └─ still typed AdminQuery, so whereAdmin() exists ✔
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's an **assembly line that hands the same car back to you after each station** — paint, then wheels, then trim. Because you always get *your* car back (not "a generic vehicle"), a custom station only your model needs is still reachable at the end of the line.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
class QueryBuilder {
  private parts: string[] = [];
  select(col: string): this { this.parts.push(`SELECT ${col}`); return this; }
  from(tbl: string): this   { this.parts.push(`FROM ${tbl}`);   return this; }
  build(): string           { return this.parts.join(" "); }
}

const sql = new QueryBuilder().select("*").from("users").build();
console.log(sql);  // "SELECT * FROM users"

class AdminQuery extends QueryBuilder {
  whereAdmin(): this { return this; }
}
// Because select() returns `this`, the chain stays typed as AdminQuery,
// so the subclass-only whereAdmin() is still callable:
new AdminQuery().select("*").whereAdmin().from("users").build(); // OK
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you type a fluent builder so subclass methods survive the chain?**
A: Make each chainable method return the **polymorphic `this` type** (`method(): this { ...; return this; }`) instead of the explicit class name. `this` resolves to the concrete type at the call site, so on a subclass the chain stays typed as the subclass and its extra methods remain available — something a hardcoded base-class return type would lose.

---

<h2 id="module-5">Module 5 — Type-Level Programming: Conditional & Mapped Types</h2>

Up to now you've *described* shapes with types. In this module you start **computing** with them. TypeScript's type system is a tiny programming language of its own: it has `if/else` (conditional types), loops over keys (mapped types), pattern-matching (`infer`), and even string manipulation (template literal types). The payoff is huge — one generic type can derive dozens of related types automatically, so they never drift apart. The danger is also real — go too clever and your types become unreadable and slow to compile. This module teaches both the power and the restraint.

> A quick word on jargon you'll see a lot: a **type parameter** is a placeholder like the `T` in `Box<T>` — it's a "blank" you fill in later. A **union** (written `A | B`) means "either A or B". Keep those two in mind; everything below builds on them.

---

### 🔀 Conditional Types & `infer` — `if/else` for Types

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **conditional type** is an `if/else` that runs in *type space* (at compile time, before any code runs). It has the shape `T extends U ? X : Y`, read as: "if type `T` is assignable to type `U`, the result is `X`, otherwise `Y`". The **`infer`** keyword is a companion that lets you say "match this shape and **capture** a piece of it into a new type variable".

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes the *right* type depends on the *input* type. Imagine a helper that returns a value if you pass one, or `null` if you pass `null`. Without conditionals you'd have to write two separate types and hope callers pick the right one. And often you have a *wrapped* type — like `Promise<User>` — and you just want the thing **inside** the wrapper (`User`). You can't reach inside a generic by hand; `infer` reaches in for you.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The compiler checks the `extends` test and picks a branch. `infer` works only inside the `extends` clause: it introduces a fresh placeholder, matches the input against a pattern, and binds whatever fills that slot.

```
   ElementType<T> = T extends (infer U)[] ? U : never
                              └──┬──┘
        "match an array of SOMETHING, call that
         something U, and give it back as the result"

   ElementType<number[]>
        │  T is number[], pattern is (infer U)[]
        ▼  so U is bound to number
      number
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **vending machine slot** shaped exactly like a can. When you push a packet of crisps through, it doesn't fit — the test fails (`else` branch). When you push a can through, it fits *and* the machine now "knows" the can's size. `infer` is that moment of the can sliding in and the machine reading its label.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Basic conditional: pick a result based on the input type.
type IsString<T> = T extends string ? "yes" : "no";

type A = IsString<string>;   // "yes"
type B = IsString<number>;   // "no"

// `infer` captures the element type of an array.
type ElementType<T> = T extends (infer U)[] ? U : never;

type C = ElementType<number[]>;   // number
type D = ElementType<string[]>;   // string

// The classic: unwrap whatever a Promise resolves to.
type UnwrapPromise<T> = T extends Promise<infer U> ? U : T;

type E = UnwrapPromise<Promise<number>>;  // number
type F = UnwrapPromise<string>;           // string  (not a Promise -> returned as-is)

// `infer` can grab a function's return type.
type ReturnOf<T> = T extends (...args: any[]) => infer R ? R : never;
type G = ReturnOf<() => boolean>;   // boolean
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Implement a conditional type using `infer` that extracts the element type of an array.**
A:
```typescript
type ElementType<T> = T extends (infer U)[] ? U : never;
```
`infer U` pattern-matches the array's element type and binds it to `U`, which you return in the "true" branch. If `T` isn't an array, fall through to `never` (the empty type, meaning "no value"). `never` is the conventional "no match" result.

---

### 🌿 Distributive Conditional Types — One Check, Applied to Each Union Member

#### 🧩 What is it?
When the type you test in a conditional is a **naked type parameter** (the bare `T` itself, not wrapped in anything) and you feed it a **union**, TypeScript doesn't test the whole union at once. It **distributes**: it runs the conditional *separately on each member* of the union, then unions the results back together. This is called a **distributive conditional type**.

> The word "naked" is the official term: `T` is naked in `T extends U ? ...`. It is **not** naked in `[T] extends [U] ? ...` because the `[ ]` wraps it. Naked = distribution on; wrapped = distribution off.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Most of the time distribution is exactly what you want — it's how filtering union members works (e.g. "remove all the strings from this union"). But sometimes it's a *trap*: you want to ask one question about the union *as a whole*, and distribution silently asks it of each part instead, giving a surprising answer. You need to know how to **turn it off** — by wrapping both sides in a one-element tuple `[ ]`.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  ToArray<T> = T extends any ? T[] : never
  ToArray<string | number>      // T is a naked param

        distributes over each member:
   ┌──────────────┬──────────────┐
   │ string -> string[]          │
   │ number -> number[]          │
   └──────┬───────┴──────┬───────┘
          ▼  union the results
       string[] | number[]      <-- NOT (string | number)[] !

  Wrap it to STOP distribution:
  ToArrayN<T> = [T] extends [any] ? T[] : never
  ToArrayN<string | number>  ->  (string | number)[]
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Distribution is like a **mail-merge letter**. You write one template ("Dear `NAME`,") and a list of recipients (the union). The merge runs the template *once per recipient* and you get a stack of personalised letters — not one letter addressed to the whole list at once. Wrapping in `[T]` is like sealing the whole list in a single envelope so it's treated as one bundle.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Naked T + union -> distributes over each member.
type ToArray<T> = T extends any ? T[] : never;
type A = ToArray<string | number>;   // string[] | number[]

// Wrap both sides in a tuple -> distribution is OFF.
type ToArrayNonDist<T> = [T] extends [any] ? T[] : never;
type B = ToArrayNonDist<string | number>;  // (string | number)[]

// Distribution is what makes filtering work (this is how `Exclude` is built):
type MyExclude<T, U> = T extends U ? never : T;
type C = MyExclude<"a" | "b" | "c", "a">;   // "b" | "c"
// each member is tested: "a" -> never (dropped), "b" -> "b", "c" -> "c"

// Edge case to know: distributing over `never` (the empty union)
// produces `never`, because there are zero members to map over.
type Dist<T> = T extends any ? T[] : never;
type D = Dist<never>;    // never   <-- surprising! nothing to distribute over
type NonDist<T> = [T] extends [any] ? T[] : never;
type E = NonDist<never>; // never[] <-- wrapped, so `never` is treated as a value
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is a distributive conditional type, and how do you switch the behaviour off?**
A: When a conditional type tests a **naked type parameter** (`T extends ...`) and `T` is a **union**, TypeScript applies the conditional to *each union member individually* and unions the results — that's distribution. So `ToArray<string | number>` becomes `string[] | number[]`, not `(string | number)[]`. To switch it off, wrap both the checked type and the extends type in a single-element tuple: `[T] extends [U] ? ... : ...`. The tuple makes `T` non-naked, so the union is evaluated as a whole.

---

### 🗺️ Mapped Types & Modifiers — Loop Over Every Key

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **mapped type** builds a new object type by walking over the keys of an existing one and producing a property for each. The syntax is `{ [K in keyof T]: <new type> }`, read as: "for each key `K` in `T`, create a property whose type is `<new type>`". You can also tweak the **modifiers** on each property — the `readonly` flag and the optional `?` flag — by prefixing them with `+` (add) or `-` (remove).

> `keyof T` means "the union of all property names of `T`". For `{ a: number; b: string }`, `keyof T` is `"a" | "b"`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You constantly need *variations* of an existing type: a version where everything is optional (for a PATCH update), a fully `readonly` version (for immutable data), a version where nothing is optional anymore. Writing each variation by hand means copy-pasting every field — and the moment someone adds a field to the original, every copy silently goes stale. A mapped type derives the variation **automatically**, so it's always in sync.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  T = { a: number; readonly b: string }
                │
   { [K in keyof T]: ... }   // K iterates: "a", then "b"
                │
       ┌────────┴─────────┐
       ▼                  ▼
   K = "a"            K = "b"
   T["a"] = number    T["b"] = string

  Modifiers (applied per property):
    readonly [K in keyof T]   -> make every prop readonly
   -readonly [K in keyof T]   -> strip readonly
            [K in keyof T]?   -> make every prop optional
            [K in keyof T]-?  -> make every prop required
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A mapped type is a **rubber stamp run down a checklist**. You have a list of items (the keys). The stamp applies the *same rule* to every line — "mark every item optional", "mark every item read-only". You don't re-type the list; you stamp it. Add a new line to the checklist later and the stamp covers it too.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Make every property readonly (this is basically the built-in `Readonly<T>`).
type AllReadonly<T> = { readonly [K in keyof T]: T[K] };
type A = AllReadonly<{ a: number }>;   // { readonly a: number }

// Make every property optional (basically `Partial<T>`).
type AllOptional<T> = { [K in keyof T]?: T[K] };
type B = AllOptional<{ a: number }>;   // { a?: number }

// Strip readonly with the `-` modifier.
type Mutable<T> = { -readonly [K in keyof T]: T[K] };
type C = Mutable<{ readonly a: number }>;   // { a: number }

// Strip optionality with `-?`. IMPORTANT: -? also removes `undefined`
// from the property's type, not just the `?` flag.
type Concrete<T> = { [K in keyof T]-?: T[K] };
type D = Concrete<{ a?: number }>;   // { a: number }   (not number | undefined)
```

> **Note (simplification):** `{ [K in keyof T]: T[K] }` is a **homomorphic** mapped type ("homomorphic" just means "same shape in, same shape out"). Because it iterates `keyof T` directly, it automatically *preserves* the source's existing `readonly` and `?` modifiers unless you explicitly change them. Mapped types that iterate some other union of keys don't get this automatic preservation.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Build a mapped type that makes every key optional and readonly.**
A:
```typescript
type ReadonlyPartial<T> = { readonly [K in keyof T]?: T[K] };
// { a: number; b: string } -> { readonly a?: number; readonly b?: string }
```
The `readonly` prefix adds the read-only modifier and the `?` after the key adds optionality, both applied to every property. (Equivalently you could compose the built-ins as `Readonly<Partial<T>>`.)

---

### 🔑 Key Remapping with `as` — Rename and Filter Keys

#### 🧩 What is it?
Inside a mapped type you can add an `as` clause — `[K in keyof T as <new key>]` — to **rename** each key as you go. The crucial trick: if the new key evaluates to `never`, that property is **dropped entirely**. So `as` gives you both **renaming** and **filtering** of keys.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Plain mapped types keep the same key names. But real transformations often need *different* names (turn `name` into `getName`) or need to **remove** some keys (keep only the string-valued fields, drop the rest). Before key remapping existed you had to chain awkward helper types. With `as` it's one clean clause: map a key to `never` and it vanishes from the result.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  { [K in keyof T as NewKey]: T[K] }

  For each key K, compute NewKey:
    - a normal string  -> property is renamed to that
    - `never`          -> property is DROPPED

  RemoveKey<T, "b">  where the `as` maps "b" -> never:
    { a: number; b: string }
        a -> kept as "a"
        b -> never -> removed
    = { a: number }
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's an **airport security checkpoint with a relabeling desk**. Each passenger (key) walks through. Some get a new boarding-pass name printed (renamed). Anyone on the no-fly list (maps to `never`) is turned away and never boards (dropped). One desk, two jobs: rename and reject.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Filter OUT a specific key by mapping it to `never`.
type RemoveKey<T, ToRemove> = {
  [K in keyof T as K extends ToRemove ? never : K]: T[K];
};
type A = RemoveKey<{ a: number; b: string }, "b">;   // { a: number }

// Filter by VALUE type: keep only properties whose value is a string.
type StringProps<T> = {
  [K in keyof T as T[K] extends string ? K : never]: T[K];
};
type B = StringProps<{ a: number; b: string; c: string }>;
// { b: string; c: string }   (the number-valued `a` is dropped)
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you remove a key from an object type using a mapped type?**
A: Use key remapping with `as` and map the unwanted key to `never`:
```typescript
type Without<T, K extends keyof T> = {
  [P in keyof T as P extends K ? never : P]: T[P];
};
```
Any key whose remapped value is `never` is omitted from the resulting type. (This is the core mechanism behind the built-in `Omit`.)

---

### 🧵 Template Literal Types — String Building in the Type System

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **template literal type** looks just like a JavaScript template string, but it operates on *types*. Using backticks and `${ }` slots, you can stitch string literal types together at compile time — e.g. turn the key `"name"` into the type `"getName"`. TypeScript also ships four built-in helpers that change casing: `Uppercase`, `Lowercase`, `Capitalize`, and `Uncapitalize`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
APIs are full of *name patterns*: a model with fields `name` and `age` often wants getter methods `getName` and `getAge`; event systems want `"click"` to map to `"onClick"`; route builders want `"/users/" + id`. Without template literal types you'd type those derived names by hand and they'd drift the moment a field is renamed. Template literal types **compute** the names from the source, so they stay correct automatically.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  `get${Capitalize<K>}`   where K = "name"

   Capitalize<"name">  ->  "Name"
   "get" + "Name"      ->  "getName"

  Combined with key remapping over an object:
   { name: string; age: number }
        name -> getName: () => string
        age  -> getAge:  () => number
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's a **label-maker with a prefix dial**. You type the core word, the machine auto-capitalises the first letter and prints "get" in front. Feed it your list of field names and out comes a perfectly consistent set of method labels — no hand-printing, no typos.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Build one derived name from a string literal type.
type Getter<K extends string> = `get${Capitalize<K>}`;
type A = Getter<"name">;   // "getName"

// The built-in casing intrinsics:
type B = Uppercase<"abc">;       // "ABC"
type C = Uncapitalize<"Hello">;  // "hello"

// Combine template literals + key remapping to generate a getters interface.
// `K & string` narrows the key to a string before Capitalize (keys could
// theoretically be number/symbol, which Capitalize can't take).
type Getters<T> = {
  [K in keyof T as `get${Capitalize<K & string>}`]: () => T[K];
};
type D = Getters<{ name: string; age: number }>;
// {
//   getName: () => string;
//   getAge:  () => number;
// }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Write a type that turns an object's keys into getter method names like `getName`.**
A:
```typescript
type Getters<T> = {
  [K in keyof T as `get${Capitalize<K & string>}`]: () => T[K];
};
```
A template literal type `` `get${Capitalize<K & string>}` `` builds each new key. `Capitalize` upper-cases the first letter; `K & string` guarantees `K` is a string (object keys can also be `number` or `symbol`, which the casing helpers reject). The value `() => T[K]` types each getter to return the original property's type.

---

### 🌀 Recursion & Depth Limits — When Types Call Themselves

#### 🧩 What is it?
A type can refer to **itself** to handle nested structures — a `DeepReadonly` that makes every property read-only, then recurses into nested objects to do the same all the way down. But the compiler caps how deep this recursion and type "instantiation" can go, to protect itself from infinite or explosive computation. Hit the cap and you get the famous error: *"Type instantiation is excessively deep and possibly infinite."*

> "Instantiation" here means each time the compiler expands a generic with concrete arguments — like a function call, but for types. Deeply nested or self-referential types can trigger thousands of these.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Real data nests: config objects, JSON trees, deeply nested state. A non-recursive type only transforms the top layer and leaves nested objects untouched — a `Readonly` that isn't actually deep. Recursion lets one type reach all the way down. The depth **limit** matters because it's the wall you hit when a clever type goes too far — and knowing it exists turns a baffling error into an expected boundary.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  DeepReadonly<{ a: { b: number } }>
        │  map keys, recurse when value is an object
        ▼
  { readonly a: DeepReadonly<{ b: number }> }
        │  recurse again
        ▼
  { readonly a: { readonly b: number } }
        │  b's value is `number` (not an object) -> stop
        ▼  base case reached, recursion ends
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's a set of **Russian nesting dolls**. To paint every doll you open the outer one, paint it, open the next, paint it — repeating until you reach the tiny solid doll that doesn't open (the base case). The compiler's depth limit is a rule that says "if you've opened ~50 dolls and still aren't done, stop — this is probably a doll that contains itself forever."

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Recursive mapped type: readonly all the way down.
type DeepReadonly<T> = {
  readonly [K in keyof T]: T[K] extends object
    ? DeepReadonly<T[K]>   // value is an object -> recurse
    : T[K];                // primitive -> base case, stop
};

type Config = { server: { port: number }; debug: boolean };
type Frozen = DeepReadonly<Config>;
// {
//   readonly server: { readonly port: number };
//   readonly debug: boolean;
// }

// If you write a type with no shrinking base case, the compiler bails:
// type Infinite<T> = { next: Infinite<T> };  // works lazily, but eager
// recursion that grows each step eventually errors:
//   "Type instantiation is excessively deep and possibly infinite."
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What happens if a recursive type has no base case, and what error do you see?**
A: The compiler keeps instantiating the type deeper and deeper until it crosses an internal limit (historically around 50 levels of instantiation depth), then aborts with *"Type instantiation is excessively deep and possibly infinite."* The fix is to ensure every recursion shrinks toward a **base case** — for `DeepReadonly`, the base case is "stop recursing once the value is a primitive (not an `object`)".

---

### ⚖️ When Type-Level Cleverness Becomes a Liability — Knowing When to Stop

#### 🧩 What is it?
Everything in this module is powerful enough to encode astonishing logic in types — entire parsers and state machines have been written purely in the type system. This final topic is the **judgement call**: recognising the point where a clever type stops paying for itself and starts costing your team in readability, compile time, and error legibility.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A type that's a marvel of cleverness can be a nightmare to maintain: nobody else on the team can read it, the IDE error it produces is forty lines of nested generics, and it quietly adds seconds to every type-check. Type-level code has **no runtime tests** and no debugger — if it's wrong, you find out by squinting at hover tooltips. The skill an architect brings is knowing when a plain, slightly-repetitive type is the *better* engineering choice.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? (the trade-off checklist)
```
  Reach for type-level magic when:
    + it removes a whole class of runtime bugs
    + it stays in sync automatically with a source type
    + a teammate can still read it in under a minute

  Step back to something simpler when:
    - the error messages become unreadable
    - tsc / the editor noticeably slows down
    - only the author understands it
    - a plain hand-written type would do
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like **seasoning a dish**. A pinch of spice transforms the meal; dumping in the whole rack makes it inedible. The mark of a good cook isn't using *every* spice — it's knowing exactly when to stop. Clever types are the same: restraint is the senior skill, not raw firepower.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// CLEVER: a deeply generic, "do everything" transform that few can read.
type DeepCamelKeys<T> = T extends object
  ? { [K in keyof T as CamelCase<K & string>]: DeepCamelKeys<T[K]> }
  : T;
// ...where CamelCase is itself a recursive template-literal parser.
// Powerful, but a wall of generics in every error message and tooltip.

// SIMPLER: when the shape is fixed and small, just write it out.
type UserDto = { firstName: string; lastName: string };
// Three readable lines beat a 40-line generic nobody can debug.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When is a clever type *not* worth it?**
A: When its cost outweighs its benefit. Concretely: when the resulting error messages become unreadable, when it measurably slows `tsc` or the editor, when no one but the author can maintain it, or when a simple hand-written type would do the same job. Type-level code has no runtime tests and no debugger, so favour clarity. Reach for the advanced machinery when it eliminates a real class of bugs *and* stays readable; otherwise prefer the boring, explicit type.

---

<h2 id="module-6">Module 6 — keyof, Indexed Access & Utility Types</h2>

You already have a perfectly good `User` type. Now someone asks for "the same type but every field optional", or "the same type minus the password", or "just the return value of this function". Re-typing all of that by hand is busywork — and it goes stale the moment the original changes. This module is about **deriving** new types from existing ones, so one source of truth drives them all. By the end you'll be able to read `keyof`, index into a type, use every built-in utility type with confidence, and **rebuild those utilities from scratch** — the single most common TypeScript whiteboard drill.

---

### 🔑 `keyof` — Turn an Object Type Into Its Keys

#### 🧩 What is it?
`keyof T` is an operator that **reads the property names of a type `T` and gives you back a union of those names as string literals**. (A **union** — written `A | B` — means "one of these"; a **string literal type** like `"id"` is a type that allows only that exact string.)

If `T` is an object type with keys `id`, `name`, and `email`, then `keyof T` is the type `"id" | "name" | "email"`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Imagine a function that reads one property off an object: `getField(user, "name")`. Without `keyof`, the key parameter would be a plain `string`, so `getField(user, "naem")` (a typo) compiles fine and blows up at runtime. `keyof` lets you say "the key must be one of the *actual* keys of this object" — the typo becomes a compile error.

It also keeps derived types **in sync**. If you build something from `keyof User` and later add a field to `User`, the derived thing updates automatically. No manual edit, no drift.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`keyof` looks at every property name on the type and unions them into a single type:

```
   type User = {
     id: number          ──┐
     name: string        ──┤  keyof User
     email: string       ──┘     │
   }                             ▼
              "id" | "name" | "email"
```

A few precise points (each glossed):
- Keys can be `string`, `number`, or `symbol`, so `keyof` can yield those too.
- `keyof` an array type gives `number` plus all the array method names (`"length" | "push" | ... | number`).
- `keyof {}` (an empty object type) is `never` — the **empty union**, a type with no values.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`keyof` is the **table of contents** of a book. You don't copy out the whole chapter — you just list the chapter titles. If the author adds a chapter, the table of contents grows on its own. `keyof User` is the table of contents for the `User` type: the list of valid "places you can look things up".

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface User {
  id: number;
  name: string;
  email: string;
}

type UserKeys = keyof User;
// UserKeys = "id" | "name" | "email"

let k: UserKeys;
k = "id";    // ok
k = "name";  // ok
// k = "naem";  // ❌ Type '"naem"' is not assignable to 'UserKeys'

// keyof shines in generic accessors (see indexed access, next topic):
function getKey<T, K extends keyof T>(obj: T, key: K) {
  return obj[key];
}
const u: User = { id: 1, name: "Ada", email: "ada@x.dev" };
getKey(u, "name"); // ok, key must be a real key of User
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `keyof` produce, and what is `keyof` of an array type?**
A: `keyof T` produces a **union of `T`'s property names** as literal types. For an object type `{ a: 1; b: 2 }` it's `"a" | "b"`. For an **array** type it's `number` (the numeric index) **plus** the names of all array methods/properties (`"length" | "push" | "map" | ... | number`) — because an array is just an object whose keys include numeric indices and method names.

---

### 📇 Indexed Access — Look Up a Type By Its Key

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Indexed access** (also called a "lookup type") is `T[K]`: it gives you **the type of property `K` inside type `T`**. It's exactly like reading `obj[key]` in JavaScript — but it happens in the type world and returns a *type*, not a value.

`User["name"]` is `string`. `User["id"]` is `number`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You constantly need to refer to "whatever type that field is" without hard-coding it. Say the `id` field is `number` today but becomes a branded `UserId` next month. If you wrote `function setId(id: number)`, you must hunt down every copy. If you wrote `function setId(id: User["id"])`, it updates itself. Indexed access lets types **point at** other types instead of duplicating them.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You index a type with a **key type** (or a *union* of key types):

```
   T[K]  =  "give me the type stored at key K in T"

   User["name"]            -> string
   User["id"]              -> number
   User["id" | "name"]     -> number | string   (union in → union out)
   User[keyof User]        -> number | string    (every value type)
```

The killer combination is `T[keyof T]`: index a type with **all** its keys at once, and you get the **union of all its value types**. And for arrays/tuples, `T[number]` gives "the type of any element":

```
   const xs = [1, 2, 3]
   typeof xs            -> number[]
   (typeof xs)[number]  -> number   ← element type
```

> **Note (simplification):** `typeof xs` here is the **type-level** `typeof` (it reads a value's type), which is different from JavaScript's runtime `typeof` that returns a string like `"object"`. Same keyword, two different worlds.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Indexed access is **looking up a word in a dictionary**. `keyof` gave you the list of words (the headwords); `T[K]` is flipping to one word and reading its *definition*. Index with several words at once (a union) and you get all their definitions bundled together.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface User {
  id: number;
  name: string;
  email: string;
}

type NameType = User["name"];        // string
type IdType = User["id"];            // number
type IdOrName = User["id" | "name"]; // number | string  (union index)
type AnyValue = User[keyof User];    // number | string  (all value types)

// Element type of an array, derived — not hard-coded:
const roles = ["admin", "editor", "viewer"] as const;
type Role = (typeof roles)[number];
// Role = "admin" | "editor" | "viewer"

// Why it beats hard-coding — id's type can change in ONE place:
function setId(id: User["id"]) { /* ... */ }
setId(42); // tracks User["id"] automatically
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `T[keyof T]` give you, and how is indexing with a union different from indexing with one key?**
A: `T[keyof T]` indexes a type with **all of its keys at once**, producing the **union of all its property value types**. Indexing with a single literal key (`User["id"]`) gives one type (`number`); indexing with a **union** of keys (`User["id" | "name"]`) distributes over each key and **unions the results** (`number | string`). So "one key → one type, union of keys → union of types."

---

### 🧰 Built-in Utility Types — The Standard Toolbox

#### 🧩 What is it?
TypeScript ships a set of **utility types**: generic types built into the standard library that transform an existing type into a related one. You write `Partial<User>` and get a version of `User` with every field optional — no manual rewrite.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
The same handful of transformations come up endlessly: "make this optional for a PATCH endpoint", "pick only these fields for a form", "drop the secret field before sending to the client". Hand-writing each variant duplicates the shape and rots when the original changes. Utility types do the transformation **once, derived from the source**, so there's a single source of truth.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Here's the toolbox, grouped by what each one is *made of* (these mechanisms — **mapped types** that loop over keys, and **conditional types** that branch with `extends` — are Module 5's machinery; here we just use them):

```
   SHAPE-TRANSFORMERS (loop over keys = "mapped types")
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
   Partial<T>    every field optional        ( ?  added )
   Required<T>   every field required        ( ?  removed )
   Readonly<T>   every field read-only       ( readonly added )
   Pick<T,K>     keep only keys K
   Omit<T,K>     keep all keys EXCEPT K
   Record<K,V>   build { [each K]: V } from scratch

   UNION-FILTERS (branch with extends = "conditional types")
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
   Exclude<T,U>      remove members of T that are in U
   Extract<T,U>      keep members of T that are in U
   NonNullable<T>    remove null and undefined

   FUNCTION/PROMISE EXTRACTORS (pull a type out with "infer")
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
   ReturnType<F>     the type F returns
   Parameters<F>     the tuple of F's argument types
   InstanceType<C>   the instance type a class C constructs
   Awaited<T>        the value a Promise resolves to (recursively)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
They're the **preset filters** in a photo app. The original photo (your base type) stays untouched; "B&W", "Crop", "Brighten" each hand you a *derived* image. Edit the original and re-apply the preset — the derived photo updates. You rarely build a filter by hand because the presets cover the common edits.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface User {
  id: number;
  name: string;
  email?: string;
}

type T1 = Partial<User>;
// { id?: number; name?: string; email?: string }

type T2 = Required<User>;
// { id: number; name: string; email: string }   (email no longer optional)

type T3 = Readonly<User>;
// { readonly id: number; readonly name: string; readonly email?: string }

type T4 = Pick<User, "id" | "name">;
// { id: number; name: string }

type T5 = Omit<User, "email">;
// { id: number; name: string }

type T6 = Record<"admin" | "guest", boolean>;
// { admin: boolean; guest: boolean }

type T7 = Exclude<"a" | "b" | "c", "a">;        // "b" | "c"
type T8 = Extract<"a" | "b" | "c", "a" | "x">;  // "a"
type T9 = NonNullable<string | null | undefined>; // string

function makeUser(name: string): User {
  return { id: 1, name };
}
type T10 = ReturnType<typeof makeUser>;  // User
type T11 = Parameters<typeof makeUser>;  // [name: string]

class Box { value = 0; }
type T12 = InstanceType<typeof Box>;     // Box
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `Pick`/`Omit` and `Exclude`/`Extract`?**
A: `Pick` and `Omit` operate on **object types** — they keep or drop whole **properties** by key (`Pick<User, "id">` keeps the `id` property). `Exclude` and `Extract` operate on **union types** — they keep or drop **members of a union** by assignability (`Exclude<"a" | "b", "a">` removes the `"a"` member). One filters the *keys of an object*, the other filters the *members of a union*. (Internally `Omit` is even built *on top of* `Exclude`, as we'll see next.)

---

### 🛠️ Build the Utilities From Scratch — The Classic Drill

#### 🧩 What is it?
Re-implementing the built-in utilities yourself using the raw machinery: **mapped types** (`{ [P in K]: ... }` — a loop over keys), **key remapping** (`as` inside a mapped type — rename/filter keys), and `infer` (a placeholder inside a conditional type that captures a type for you). This is *the* most-asked TypeScript interview exercise.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Two reasons. First, interviews ask for it directly — "implement `Pick`/`Omit`/`ReturnType`". Second, understanding the internals means you can build your *own* utilities (a `DeepPartial`, an `Update<T>`) when the built-ins don't quite fit. You can't extend a tool you treat as magic.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each utility is a small, readable formula. Read `[P in K]` as "for each key `P` in the set `K`", and `T[P]` as the indexed-access lookup from earlier:

```
   MyPick<T, K>      for each P in K:        keep   T[P]
   MyPartial<T>      for each P in keyof T:  make   T[P] optional  (?)
   MyRequired<T>     for each P in keyof T:  make   T[P] required  (-?)
   MyReadonly<T>     for each P in keyof T:  make   T[P] readonly
   MyRecord<K, V>    for each P in K:        set    V
   MyOmit<T, K>      for each P in keyof T,  drop P if P is in K   (key remap)
   MyReturnType<F>   F extends (..)=> infer R  ?  R : never
   MyParameters<F>   F extends (infer P)=>..   ?  P : never
```

The two clever bits:
- **`-?`** in `MyRequired` *removes* the optional flag (the `+`/`-` modifiers add or strip `?`/`readonly`).
- **`as P extends K ? never : P`** in `MyOmit` is **key remapping**: a key that should be dropped is remapped to `never`, and a mapped-type key of `never` simply disappears.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's the difference between **buying flat-pack furniture** (`Partial<T>`) and **owning the screwdriver and the diagram** (`{ [P in keyof T]?: T[P] }`). Once you've assembled it from parts a couple of times, you can build a custom shelf the catalogue doesn't sell.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface User {
  id: number;
  name: string;
  email: string;
}

// ── Pick: for each P in K, keep T[P] ──
type MyPick<T, K extends keyof T> = {
  [P in K]: T[P];
};
type R1 = MyPick<User, "id" | "name">;
// { id: number; name: string }   ✅ equals Pick<User,"id"|"name">

// ── Omit: drop keys in K via key remapping (dropped key -> never) ──
type MyOmit<T, K extends keyof T> = {
  [P in keyof T as P extends K ? never : P]: T[P];
};
type R2 = MyOmit<User, "email">;
// { id: number; name: string }   ✅ equals Omit<User,"email">

// ── Partial / Required / Readonly: toggle modifiers ──
type MyPartial<T> = { [P in keyof T]?: T[P] };
type MyRequired<T> = { [P in keyof T]-?: T[P] };   // -? strips optional
type MyReadonly<T> = { readonly [P in keyof T]: T[P] };

// ── Record: build a fresh object type ──
type MyRecord<K extends keyof never, V> = { [P in K]: V };
type R3 = MyRecord<"a" | "b", number>; // { a: number; b: number }

// ── ReturnType / Parameters: pull types out with infer ──
type MyReturnType<F extends (...args: never[]) => unknown> =
  F extends (...args: never[]) => infer R ? R : never;
type MyParameters<F extends (...args: never[]) => unknown> =
  F extends (...args: infer P) => unknown ? P : never;

function greet(name: string, times: number): string {
  return name.repeat(times);
}
type R4 = MyReturnType<typeof greet>;  // string
type R5 = MyParameters<typeof greet>;  // [name: string, times: number]
```

> **Note (simplification):** the built-in `ReturnType`/`Parameters` constrain `F` to `(...args: any) => any`. We use `(...args: never[]) => unknown` to avoid `any` (which the house rules forbid); `never[]` in the parameter position accepts any function for inference. The results are identical for the cases above.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Implement `Omit` from scratch and explain why a key "disappears".**
A:
```typescript
type MyOmit<T, K extends keyof T> = {
  [P in keyof T as P extends K ? never : P]: T[P];
};
```
The `as` clause is **key remapping**: for each property `P`, if `P` is one of the keys to remove (`P extends K`), we remap its key to `never`; otherwise we keep `P`. A mapped-type key that resolves to `never` is **omitted from the result entirely** — so the unwanted properties vanish, leaving exactly the others. (The real `Omit` is defined as `Pick<T, Exclude<keyof T, K>>`, which is the same idea expressed via `Exclude`.)

---

### ⏳ `Awaited<T>` — Unwrap Promises, Even Nested Ones

#### 🧩 What is it?
`Awaited<T>` gives you **the type you'd get if you `await`ed a value of type `T`**. `Awaited<Promise<string>>` is `string`. Crucially, if the promise resolves to *another* promise, `Awaited` keeps unwrapping until it reaches a plain value. (A **`Promise`** — glossing — is the type of an async result that will arrive later.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Async functions return promises, and sometimes promises of promises (e.g. a function returning `Promise<Promise<User>>`, or `ReturnType` of an `async` function). You want "the final resolved value type", not "a promise wrapping a promise wrapping the value". Before `Awaited` existed, people wrote buggy single-level unwrappers that broke on nesting. `Awaited` models exactly what `await` does at runtime — including how `await` flattens nested promises.
