#### ⚙️ How does it work?
It's a **recursive conditional type**: peel one `Promise` layer, then call itself on the inside, stopping when there's no promise left:

```
   Awaited<Promise<Promise<string>>>
        │ peel one Promise layer
        ▼
   Awaited<Promise<string>>
        │ peel one Promise layer
        ▼
   Awaited<string>
        │ no Promise to peel → done
        ▼
   string
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A **package inside a package inside a package** (those frustrating gift boxes). `await` is you opening box after box until you reach the actual gift. `Awaited<T>` is the label that tells you, in advance, *what the gift will be* — no matter how many boxes deep it's wrapped.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// A simplified version of the real Awaited, to show the recursion:
type MyAwaited<T> = T extends Promise<infer Inner> ? MyAwaited<Inner> : T;

type A1 = MyAwaited<Promise<string>>;                 // string
type A2 = MyAwaited<Promise<Promise<number>>>;        // number
type A3 = MyAwaited<Promise<Promise<Promise<User>>>>; // User
type A4 = MyAwaited<boolean>;                         // boolean (not a promise → unchanged)

// The built-in behaves the same for these cases:
type B1 = Awaited<Promise<Promise<number>>>;          // number

// Realistic use — the resolved type of an async function:
async function loadUser(): Promise<User> {
  return { id: 1, name: "Ada", email: "ada@x.dev" };
}
type Loaded = Awaited<ReturnType<typeof loadUser>>;   // User
//            ^ ReturnType gives Promise<User>, Awaited unwraps to User

interface User { id: number; name: string; email: string }
```

> **Note (simplification):** the real `Awaited` also handles **thenables** (objects with a `.then` method that aren't `Promise` instances) and `null`/`undefined` edge cases. The one-line `MyAwaited` above captures the essential recursion but skips those; it matches the built-in for ordinary `Promise` nesting.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How does `Awaited` unwrap a `Promise<Promise<string>>` down to `string`?**
A: It's a **recursive conditional type**. It checks `T extends Promise<infer Inner>`; if so, it doesn't return `Inner` directly — it returns `Awaited<Inner>`, applying itself again to the unwrapped type. So `Promise<Promise<string>>` → `Awaited<Promise<string>>` → `Awaited<string>` → (no promise left) → `string`. This mirrors runtime `await`, which also flattens nested promises rather than handing you a promise back.

---

### 🧱 Composing Utilities — Derive Real API & DB Types

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Combining utility types** to turn one base type (say, your database row) into the several related shapes a real app needs: a public response that hides secrets, an update payload where everything's optional, a create payload without server-managed fields. You stack `Omit`, `Partial`, `Pick`, etc. so all variants stay derived from one source.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A single `User` entity spawns many shapes across an app: the DB row, the create body, the PATCH body, the public JSON. Typing each by hand means four copies that silently drift apart — add a column and you must remember every copy. Composing utilities means you change the base type **once** and every derived shape updates, with the compiler catching anything that no longer fits.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You apply utilities outside-in, each one reshaping the result of the last:

```
   DbUser (the source of truth)
     │  Omit<…, "passwordHash">          ← hide the secret
     ▼
   PublicUser  (safe to send to client)

   DbUser
     │  Omit<…, "id" | "createdAt">      ← drop server-managed fields
     ▼  Partial<…>                       ← make the rest optional
   UpdateUser  (a PATCH body)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
One **master architectural blueprint** of a house, from which you print different overlays: the electrician's copy hides the plumbing, the client's brochure hides the load-bearing details. Every overlay is *derived* from the same master — fix a wall on the master and every overlay reflects it. You never redraw the house four times.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// The single source of truth — a database row:
interface DbUser {
  id: string;
  email: string;
  passwordHash: string;   // secret — never leaves the server
  name: string | null;
  createdAt: Date;        // set by the DB, not the client
}

// 1. Public shape: strip the secret before sending to the client.
type PublicUser = Omit<DbUser, "passwordHash">;
// { id: string; email: string; name: string | null; createdAt: Date }

// 2. Update (PATCH) payload: drop fields a client can't change,
//    then make every remaining field optional.
type UpdateUser = Partial<Omit<DbUser, "id" | "createdAt" | "passwordHash">>;
// { email?: string; name?: string | null }

// 3. Create payload: everything except DB-generated fields, all required.
type CreateUser = Omit<DbUser, "id" | "createdAt">;
// { email: string; passwordHash: string; name: string | null }

// They all type-check against the same source:
const patch: UpdateUser = { name: "Ada" };          // ok — partial
const safe: PublicUser = {
  id: "u1", email: "a@x.dev", name: null, createdAt: new Date(),
};
// const bad: PublicUser = { ...safe, passwordHash: "x" };
//   ❌ 'passwordHash' does not exist on PublicUser — secret can't leak
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Given an entity type, derive an `Update` type using utilities, and justify the choices.**
A:
```typescript
type Update<T> = Partial<Omit<T, "id" | "createdAt">>;
```
`Omit<T, "id" | "createdAt">` removes the fields a client must **not** change (the primary key and server-set timestamps); `Partial<…>` makes every remaining field **optional**, which is exactly the semantics of a PATCH — send only the fields you're changing. Because it's derived from `T`, adding a column to the entity automatically flows into the `Update` type, and any update handler that forgot to account for it will fail to compile.

---

<h2 id="module-7">Module 7 — Inference Control: satisfies, const Params & Branding</h2>

TypeScript is constantly *guessing* the type of your values — that guessing is called **inference** (the compiler working out a type you didn't write). Most of the time its guess is fine, but sometimes it guesses *too wide* (loses detail you wanted to keep) or *too narrow* (won't let you do something safe). This module is about taking the steering wheel: telling the compiler "check this, but don't change what you inferred", pinning exact literal values, and even inventing brand-new distinctions (a "user id" that can never be mixed up with a "post id") that the type system doesn't give you for free.

---

### ✅ The `satisfies` Operator — Check It, But Don't Touch My Type

#### 🧩 What is it?
`satisfies` is a keyword you put *after* a value: `value satisfies SomeType`. It tells the compiler: **"Verify that this value matches `SomeType`. If it doesn't, error. But leave the type you already inferred for the value exactly as it was — don't replace it."** The word "inferred" here just means the type TypeScript worked out on its own.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
There are normally two ways to attach a type to a value, and both have a downside:

- A **type annotation** (`const x: T = ...`) checks the value *and then* forces the variable's type to be `T` — even if that throws away useful detail. This is called **widening** (replacing a specific type with a more general one).
- A **type assertion** (`x as T`) does the opposite: it forces the type to `T` with **no checking at all**. If you lie, the compiler believes you.

So you're stuck choosing between "checked but blurry" and "precise but unchecked". `satisfies` gives you the third option you actually wanted: **checked AND precise**.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 const x: T   =  value   →  checks ✅   type becomes T (widened) ❌
 value as T              →  type becomes T   NO check 😱
 value satisfies T       →  checks ✅   type stays the narrow inferred one ✅
```

Think of three gates the value can pass through. Only `satisfies` both inspects the value *and* hands it back unchanged.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine handing a passport to three different officers.
- The **annotation** officer checks it, then makes you wear a generic "Traveler" badge — your name and nationality are now hidden behind the label.
- The **`as` (assertion)** officer waves you through without looking — you could be holding a library card.
- The **`satisfies`** officer actually inspects the passport *and* lets you keep it in your hand, name and all. Verified, and nothing lost.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type RGB = [number, number, number];
type Color = RGB | string;

// satisfies: CHECKED, and `primary` keeps its narrow inferred type
// (the tuple `[number, number, number]`, not the wide `number[]`)
const palette = {
  primary: [10, 200, 100],
  secondary: "#ffffff",
} satisfies Record<string, Color>;

palette.primary.map((c) => c * 2);  // ✅ works — primary is the RGB tuple

// Plain annotation WIDENS primary to the whole `Color` type (RGB | string):
const palette2: Record<string, Color> = {
  primary: [10, 200, 100],
  secondary: "#ffffff",
};
palette2.primary.map((c) => c);     // ❌ Error: .map may not exist on a string

// `as` does NO checking — it forces the type even on a broken shape:
type Point = { x: number; y: number };
const half = { x: 1 } as Point;     // ✅ no error... but half.y is undefined!

// `satisfies` still REJECTS genuinely wrong values:
const bad = { primary: 123 } satisfies Record<string, Color>;
//            ~~~~~~~ Error: number is not assignable to Color
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: `as` vs `satisfies` — what's the difference?**
A: `as` is a **type assertion**: it forces a type onto a value with **no checking**, so you can silently lie (e.g. `{} as User`). `satisfies` is a **constraint check**: it verifies the value is assignable to the type but **does not change the value's inferred type**. So `satisfies` catches mistakes *and* preserves the narrow, specific type you inferred — `as` does neither.

---

### 🗂️ `as const` + `satisfies` — Bulletproof Config & Lookup Tables

#### 🧩 What is it?
A pattern that combines two tools. **`as const`** freezes a value to its most specific, read-only form — every property becomes a literal (the exact value `"/"`, not the broad type `string`) and nothing can be reassigned. **`satisfies`** then validates that frozen object against a shape you expect. Together they give you a config object that is both **exactly typed** and **guaranteed correct**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Config objects, route maps, and lookup tables (a small dictionary mapping keys to values) are everywhere. You want two things at once:
1. **Validation:** every entry follows the rules (e.g. every route starts with `/`).
2. **Precision:** when you read `routes.home` you get the literal `"/"`, and the *keys* are the exact set `"home" | "profile"`, so autocomplete and exhaustiveness work.

A plain annotation gives you (1) but destroys (2). `as const` alone gives you (2) but no validation. You need both, so you stack them.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 { home: "/" }
      │  as const     → keys + values become exact literals,
      ▼                 object is deeply readonly
 { readonly home: "/" }
      │  satisfies Record<string, `/${string}`>
      ▼                 checks every value starts with "/"
 verified ✅  AND  routes.home is the literal "/"  ✅
```

The `` `/${string}` `` is a **template literal type** — it means "any string that starts with a slash". Order matters: write `as const` *first*, then `satisfies`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`as const` is **laminating** a printed page — now it's fixed and you can read every exact word. `satisfies` is the **proofreader** who then checks every line follows the style guide. You end up with a sealed, error-checked document where every word is still legible — not blurred into "some text".

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
const routes = {
  home: "/",
  profile: "/users/:id",
} as const satisfies Record<string, `/${string}`>;

type RouteKey = keyof typeof routes;  // "home" | "profile"  (exact!)
const k: RouteKey = "home";           // ✅
const v = routes.home;                // type is "/"  (literal, not string)

// If a route forgot its leading slash, satisfies catches it:
const broken = {
  home: "oops-no-slash",
} as const satisfies Record<string, `/${string}`>;
//  ~~~~ Error: "oops-no-slash" is not assignable to `/${string}`
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why combine `as const` with `satisfies` for a config object?**
A: `as const` preserves the **exact literal types and read-only-ness** (so keys and values stay specific, enabling precise autocomplete and exhaustiveness checks), while `satisfies` **validates** the whole object against an expected shape **without widening** it. A plain `: Record<...>` annotation would validate but blur every value back to the general type, losing the literals.

---

### 📌 `const` Type Parameters — Pin the Exact Literals

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **type parameter** is the `<T>` placeholder a generic function uses to capture the type of an argument. Writing it as **`<const T>`** tells TypeScript: "when you infer `T` from the argument, infer it as narrowly as if the caller had written `as const`." So instead of guessing the broad type, the compiler pins the exact literal values.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
By default, when you pass a value to a generic function, TypeScript **widens** the inferred type for convenience. Pass `[1, "a", true]` and it guesses `(string | number | boolean)[]` — a loose array — because it assumes you might want to add more items later. But for helper functions that *capture* data (a tuple builder, a state-machine definer, a router), you want the **exact** values preserved. Before `const` type parameters (added in TS 5.0), the only fix was forcing every *caller* to write `as const` — easy to forget. `<const T>` moves that responsibility into the function once.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 asTuple([1, "a", true])

   <T>        infers  (string | number | boolean)[]   ← widened, lost
   <const T>  infers  readonly [1, "a", true]          ← exact literals kept
```

`<const T>` makes the inference behave as if the argument were written with `as const` — literal values, and a `readonly` tuple instead of a mutable array.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Picture dictating a shopping list to an assistant. A normal assistant rounds you off — "some fruit, a couple of drinks" (widened). A **`const`** assistant writes down your *exact* words in order: "2 apples, 1 oat milk, then bread" — nothing generalized, nothing reordered. Same request, far more faithful record.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
function asTuple<const T extends readonly unknown[]>(t: T): T {
  return t;
}
const t1 = asTuple([1, "a", true]);
// t1 is readonly [1, "a", true]  — exact literals, in order
const ok: readonly [1, "a", true] = t1;   // ✅

// Without `const`, the same call widens:
function noConst<T extends readonly unknown[]>(t: T): T {
  return t;
}
const t2 = noConst([1, "a", true]);
// t2 is (string | number | boolean)[]  — literals lost
const ok2: readonly [1, "a", true] = t2;
//    ~~~ Error: (string | number | boolean)[] is not that tuple
```

> **Note (simplification):** `const` here controls how the *type* is inferred — it does **not** call `Object.freeze` or make the value read-only at runtime. The argument object is still mutable in plain JavaScript; only its compile-time type becomes `readonly`.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does a `const` type parameter (`<const T>`) do?**
A: It makes the compiler infer `T` as narrowly as if the caller had written `as const` — preserving literal values and producing `readonly` tuples instead of widened arrays. It's useful for helper functions that capture data precisely (tuple builders, config/route definers) without forcing every caller to remember to add `as const` themselves.

---

### 🏷️ Branded Types — Make Two `string`s Refuse to Mix

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **branded type** (also called a **nominal type**) is a trick to make two values that are *physically* the same type — say two `string`s — be treated as **different, incompatible types** by the compiler. You "brand" each one with a hidden marker so a `UserId` can never be accidentally passed where a `PostId` is expected.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
TypeScript is **structurally typed**: two types are compatible if they have the same *shape*. Since `UserId` and `PostId` are both just `string`, TypeScript happily lets you swap them — even though mixing a user id and a post id is a real, dangerous bug. Languages like Java use **nominal typing** (compatibility by *name*), which would catch this. Branding *simulates* nominal typing inside TypeScript's structural system so these IDs stop being interchangeable.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You intersect (`&`) the real type with a fake, unique marker property that exists **only in the type system** — it's never actually present at runtime. That extra marker makes the shapes differ, so the two types stop being compatible.

```
   string                 string
     &                      &
 { __brand: "UserId" }   { __brand: "PostId" }
        │                      │
        ▼                      ▼
     UserId   ✗ not assignable to ✗   PostId
   (both are just strings at runtime, but the
    fake brand makes the COMPILER keep them apart)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A €20 note and a £20 note are both "rectangles of printed paper" — structurally identical, like two `string`s. But a cash register won't accept one for the other, because each carries a **currency mark**. The brand is that currency mark: invisible to the paper's physical shape, but enough to stop a mix-up.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// The `__brand` field is phantom — it never exists at runtime,
// it only lives in the type to keep these apart.
type UserId = string & { readonly __brand: "UserId" };
type PostId = string & { readonly __brand: "PostId" };

// A "constructor" is the one allowed place to mint a UserId:
function makeUserId(s: string): UserId {
  return s as UserId;   // the single, intentional assertion
}

function getUser(id: UserId) { /* ... */ }

const uid = makeUserId("u_123");
getUser(uid);            // ✅ correct brand

getUser("u_123");        // ❌ Error: plain string is not a UserId
const pid = "p_9" as PostId;
getUser(pid);            // ❌ Error: PostId is not a UserId
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you make two `string` IDs non-interchangeable, and why would you?**
A: Use **branded types**: intersect each with a unique phantom marker, e.g. `type UserId = string & { __brand: "UserId" }`. TypeScript is structural, so two plain `string` ids are freely swappable — a real source of bugs. Branding makes their *shapes* differ so the compiler treats them as distinct, simulating **nominal** typing. You'd reach for it for ID types, validated strings (email, URL), and units (meters vs feet).

---

### 🔒 Opaque Types & "Parse, Don't Validate"

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **opaque type** is a branded type used so that the *only* way to obtain a value of that type is to pass through a function that proves it's valid. "Opaque" means outsiders can't see or fake the brand — they must go through your front door. The slogan **"parse, don't validate"** means: instead of repeatedly *checking* a raw value everywhere ("is this a valid email?"), you **parse it once** at the boundary into a type that, by its very existence, *guarantees* it's valid.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The common pattern is to validate the same raw `string` over and over — `if (isEmail(x))` here, `if (isEmail(x))` again there. It's repetitive and easy to forget one spot, letting bad data slip through. With "parse, don't validate", you convert `string → Email` exactly once. After that, anything typed `Email` is *provably* a valid email — the compiler now enforces "this was checked" for you, so you never re-check and never forget.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
   raw string  ──parse once──▶  Email | null
                                   │
                          if not null, it's PROVEN valid
                                   ▼
        every later function takes `Email`, never re-checks
```

The type itself becomes the proof. Once you hold an `Email`, "is it valid?" is already answered — by construction.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of airport security. You're checked **once** at the gate, then handed a wristband (the `Email` type). Past that point, no café or lounge re-screens you — the wristband *is* the proof you were cleared. "Parse, don't validate" is issuing the wristband instead of patting everyone down at every door.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
type Email = string & { readonly __brand: "Email" };

// The ONE checkpoint. Returns null if invalid — you must handle it.
function parseEmail(s: string): Email | null {
  return s.includes("@") ? (s as Email) : null;
}

function sendTo(addr: Email) { /* trusts it's valid */ }

const maybe = parseEmail("a@b.com");
if (maybe) {
  sendTo(maybe);     // ✅ proven valid once, used freely after
}

sendTo("a@b.com");   // ❌ Error: a raw string was never parsed/proven
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does "parse, don't validate" mean, and how do opaque types support it?**
A: Instead of re-checking a raw value at every use site (validate), you convert it **once** at the boundary into a type that *proves* it's valid (parse). Opaque/branded types make this enforceable: the only way to get an `Email` is through `parseEmail`, so any value typed `Email` is guaranteed already-checked. This removes repeated, forgettable validation and pushes the guarantee into the type system.

---

### 🎯 Controlling Inference with `NoInfer`

#### 🧩 What is it?
`NoInfer<T>` is a built-in utility type (added in TS 5.4) that tells the compiler: **"When you're working out what `T` is from the arguments, ignore *this* position."** It lets you choose *which* argument drives the inference and which argument merely gets checked against the result.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
When a generic function has the same type parameter in two arguments, TypeScript infers `T` from **all** of them and unions the results — which can be too permissive. Say a function takes a default colour and a list of allowed colours, both typed `C`. You *want* the list to define the allowed set and the default to be checked against it. But by default the compiler also infers `C` from the default, so a typo in the default silently *widens* the allowed set instead of being rejected. `NoInfer` lets you say "infer `C` from the list only; just *check* the default against it."

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 createState(initial: C,          colors: C[])
                ▲                     ▲
                └── both infer C ──── ┘   default widens the set 😞

 createState(initial: NoInfer<C>,  colors: C[])
                ▲                     ▲
            checked only         infers C here   ✅
```

Wrapping a position in `NoInfer<...>` removes it from the inference vote, so only the un-wrapped positions decide `T`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Picture a vote to pick the team colour. Normally *everyone* in the room votes, so one confused newcomer shouting "orange!" can sway the result. `NoInfer` is the rule "only the official members get a vote; guests may attend but can't change the outcome." The colours list votes; the default just has to abide by the decision.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Without NoInfer: the default ALSO votes on what C is.
function createState<C extends string>(initial: C, colors: C[]): C {
  return initial;
}
createState("orange", ["red", "blue"]);   // ✅ no error — C widened to include "orange" 😞

// With NoInfer: only `colors` decides C; `initial` is just checked.
function createState2<C extends string>(
  initial: NoInfer<C>,
  colors: C[],
): C {
  return initial;
}
createState2("red", ["red", "blue"]);      // ✅ "red" is in the list
createState2("orange", ["red", "blue"]);   // ❌ Error: "orange" not in C ("red" | "blue")
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What problem does `NoInfer<T>` solve?**
A: When a type parameter appears in multiple argument positions, TypeScript infers it from *all* of them, which can widen the type and let mistakes through. `NoInfer<T>` excludes a position from inference so it's only **checked** against the type inferred elsewhere. Classic case: a default value that should be validated against a list of allowed options, rather than expanding that list.

---

<h2 id="module-8">Module 8 — Declaration Files & Ambient Types</h2>

You install a package, import it, and your editor lights up with red squiggles: *"Could not find a declaration file for module..."*. The code runs fine — JavaScript doesn't care about types — but TypeScript is blind. This module is about the files that give TypeScript its eyes: **declaration files** (`.d.ts`). They describe the *shape* of code without containing the code itself, and they're how every typed library, every `@types/*` package, and every "make this untyped thing typed" hack actually works.

---

### 📄 The `.d.ts` File — Types Without a Body

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **declaration file** (a file ending in `.d.ts`) is a file that describes *types only* — the shapes, function signatures, and names that exist — but contains **no runnable code**. Think of it as a label that says "here's what this thing looks like" without being the thing.

A regular `.ts` file has both: the types *and* the implementation (the actual logic). A `.d.ts` file has the types and *nothing else*.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The code that actually runs in production is **JavaScript** — types are erased ("**type erasure**" — TypeScript strips all type information when it compiles to JS). So when you ship a library, the published files are `.js`. But your *users* write TypeScript and want autocomplete and type-checking.

How do you give them types for code that is plain JavaScript at runtime? You ship a **separate `.d.ts` file** alongside the `.js`. The `.js` runs; the `.d.ts` describes it to the type-checker. The two travel together.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
When you compile a `.ts` file with `declaration: true`, TypeScript emits **two** outputs: the `.js` (what runs) and the `.d.ts` (what describes). A `.d.ts` file by itself emits **no JavaScript at all** — it's input to the type-checker only.

```
   greet.ts  (types + implementation)
        │
        │  tsc with "declaration": true
        ▼
   ┌──────────────┬──────────────┐
   ▼              ▼
 greet.js      greet.d.ts
 (runs)        (types only,
               no runtime code)
```

> **Note (simplification):** A `.d.ts` *can* technically still cause a few things via ambient declarations and triple-slash references, but it never emits its own `.js` file. Verified: running `tsc` on a `.d.ts` produces no `.js` output.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `.d.ts` file is like the **nutrition label on a food package**. The label tells you exactly what's inside — calories, ingredients, allergens — without being the food. You read the label to decide how to use the product; you eat the actual food. TypeScript "reads the label" (`.d.ts`) to check your code, while the runtime "eats the food" (the `.js`).

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// ---- greet.d.ts (types only — NO function body allowed) ----
export declare function greet(name: string): string;
// "declare" = "this exists somewhere; trust me, here's its shape."
// There is NO { ... } body. A body would be an error in a .d.ts.

// ---- greet.js (the real runtime code, plain JavaScript) ----
// export function greet(name) { return "Hi " + name; }

// ---- consumer.ts (your user's code) ----
import { greet } from "./greet";
const msg: string = greet("Ada"); // TS checks the call against greet.d.ts
// greet(123); // ❌ Error: number is not assignable to string
//             // TS knew this WITHOUT ever seeing greet.js's body.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What is a `.d.ts` file and does it produce any JavaScript when compiled?**
A: It's a declaration file containing *type information only* — no implementation. Compiling it produces **no `.js`**; it's consumed solely by the type-checker. Libraries ship `.d.ts` files next to their `.js` so TypeScript users get types for code that is plain JavaScript at runtime.

---

### 🗣️ `declare` — Promising That Something Exists

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **`declare`** keyword tells TypeScript: *"a value or function with this name and shape exists at runtime — I'm not defining it here, just describing it."* This is called an **ambient declaration** ("ambient" = "already present in the surrounding environment, not created by this file").

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes a value really does exist at runtime, but TypeScript can't see where it came from. Classic cases: a global variable injected by a `<script>` tag, a build-time constant your bundler swaps in (like `__VERSION__`), or something defined in a different language entirely. Without `declare`, TypeScript flags it as `Cannot find name '...'`. With `declare`, you give it a typed promise.

The key rule: **`declare` describes, it never creates.** There is no body, no value — just a shape.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`declare` flips a declaration from "I'm defining this" to "I'm only typing this." Because it produces no runtime code, you can write `declare` declarations in either a `.d.ts` file or inside a regular `.ts` file as an ambient block.

```
  Normal:   const x = 5;        → emits JS, x really created
  Ambient:  declare const x: number;
                                → emits NOTHING; just a typed
                                  promise that x exists already
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`declare` is like a **name tag at a conference** for a guest who's arriving separately. The tag says "Dr. Lopez, Cardiologist" — it doesn't *create* Dr. Lopez, it just tells everyone what to expect when she shows up. If she never arrives, you've got a tag pointing at empty air (a runtime crash). `declare` is a promise *you* are responsible for keeping.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Imagine a <script> tag already put `APP_CONFIG` on the page,
// and your bundler replaces __BUILD_ID__ at build time.

declare const APP_CONFIG: { apiUrl: string; debug: boolean };
declare const __BUILD_ID__: string;
declare function gtag(event: string, data: object): void; // analytics global

// Now TypeScript trusts these exist — full autocomplete, full checking:
console.log(APP_CONFIG.apiUrl);   // ✅ typed as string
gtag("page_view", { id: __BUILD_ID__ }); // ✅ typed call
// APP_CONFIG.nope;               // ❌ Error: property 'nope' doesn't exist

// ⚠️ Reality check: declare emits NO code. If APP_CONFIG isn't actually
// on the page at runtime, this throws ReferenceError. TS won't warn you —
// the promise is yours to keep.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `declare` do, and what's the risk of using it?**
A: It creates an *ambient declaration* — it tells TypeScript a value/function exists with a given type, without defining it (no body, no emitted code). The risk: it's an unchecked promise. If the thing doesn't actually exist at runtime, you get a `ReferenceError` and TypeScript gave you no warning, because types are erased and it trusted your `declare`.

---

### 🌐 `declare global` — Reaching Into the Global Scope

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`declare global`** lets you add or extend types in the **global scope** — the shared namespace available everywhere without importing, like `window`, `globalThis`, or `process`. It's how you say "I'm attaching a new property to a global object, and here's its type."

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Libraries and apps sometimes bolt things onto globals: `window.dataLayer` for analytics, `globalThis.__myCache`, a polyfill adding a method to `Array.prototype`, or extra fields on Node's `process.env`. TypeScript ships built-in types for the standard globals, but it doesn't know about *your* additions. Accessing `window.myAppVersion` without telling TS gives `Property 'myAppVersion' does not exist on type 'Window'`.

`declare global` patches the global types so your additions are recognized everywhere.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
There's a crucial rule: `declare global` only works **inside a module** — a file that has at least one top-level `import` or `export`. If your file has neither, TypeScript treats the whole file as *already global*, and `declare global` is redundant/an error. The common fix is to add an empty `export {}` to force the file to be a module.

```
  File is a MODULE (has import/export)
        │
        ▼
   declare global { ... }   ← "step OUT into global scope
        │                      and add this"
        ▼
   window.myAppVersion now typed everywhere
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A module is your **own private office**. Normally what you write stays inside it. `declare global` is like walking out to the **building's shared lobby** and pinning a notice on the community board — now *everyone* in the building sees it. But (the `export {}` rule) you can only "walk out to the lobby" if you actually *have* a private office to walk out of.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// globals.d.ts (or any module-scoped .ts file)

export {}; // ← makes this file a MODULE, so `declare global` is allowed

declare global {
  interface Window {
    myAppVersion: string;       // add a field to the existing Window type
  }
  // Add to Node's process.env, etc., the same way.
}

// Anywhere in the app now:
window.myAppVersion = "1.0.0";          // ✅ typed
const v: string = window.myAppVersion;  // ✅ string

// ✅ Verified with `tsc --strict --lib dom`: passes only WITH `export {}`.
// Remove the export and `declare global` becomes an error / is ignored,
// because the file is then already global.
```

> **Why it's dangerous (the interview trap):** `declare global` is *project-wide* and *unconditional*. Once any file declares it, every file sees it — even files that don't import yours. A library that does this silently pollutes its consumers' global types and can collide with another library's globals. Prefer module augmentation (next topic) or namespacing your globals.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `declare global` do, and when is it dangerous?**
A: It adds/extends types in the global scope (e.g. a new property on `Window` or `process.env`) and must live inside a module (often forced with `export {}`). It's dangerous because it's global and unconditional — it leaks into every file in the project, including consumers of a library, risking name collisions and surprising "where did this type come from?" pollution.

---

### 🔌 Module Augmentation — Adding to Someone Else's Types

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Module augmentation** is using `declare module "the-module-name"` to **add new members to types that already belong to another module** — typically a third-party package. Instead of replacing its types, you *merge* extra fields into them.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You use a library and a plugin extends it at runtime — for example, an Express middleware that attaches `req.user`, or a `styled-components` theme with your custom colors. The runtime gains a property, but the library's published types don't know about *your* addition. Accessing `req.user` errors. You don't own the library's source, so you can't edit its `.d.ts`. Module augmentation lets you *merge* your additions into its existing types from the outside.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You write `declare module "lib"` and re-declare an `interface` from it; TypeScript **merges** your members into the original interface (this is "declaration merging"). The non-negotiable rule:

**The module being augmented must already be visible — you have to *import* it.** Augmentation merges into an *existing* module's types. If TypeScript can't resolve the module (no import, or you're in a context where it isn't loaded), it errors with *"Invalid module name in augmentation."* Also: the augmenting file must itself be a module (have an `import`/`export`).

```
  import "express"   ← loads the real types
        │
        ▼
  declare module "express-serve-static-core" {
    interface Request { user?: User }  ← MERGED in
  }
        │
        ▼
  req.user now typed on EVERY Express Request
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Module augmentation is like **adding a sticky note to a page in a library book you borrowed**. You can't reprint the book, but you can attach your note so anyone reading *your copy* sees the extra info merged in. And you can only stick the note on a page that **exists** — you must have the book open first (the import). No book, nowhere to stick the note.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// augment.ts

import "./lib"; // ⬅️ REQUIRED: the module must be visible to augment it.
                //    Without this import → "Invalid module name in augmentation".

declare module "./lib" {
  interface Config {
    version: number; // MERGED into lib's existing Config { name: string }
  }
}

import { Config } from "./lib";

// Both the original `name` AND our added `version` are now required:
const c: Config = { name: "x", version: 1 }; // ✅

// const bad: Config = { name: "x" };
// ❌ Verified error: Property 'version' is missing in type '{ name: string }'
//    — proves the augmentation truly MERGED into the existing interface.
```

> **Note:** Module augmentation re-opens **interfaces** (which merge). It generally cannot re-open a `type` alias the same way — interfaces are the mergeable building block here. The augmentation block also can't *add a new top-level export* to a module that uses a default export only; it extends existing declarations.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you add `req.user` to Express's `Request` type, and what's the one thing you must not forget?**
A: Use module augmentation: `declare module "express-serve-static-core" { interface Request { user?: User } }`. The must-not-forget part: the module has to be **imported/visible** so TypeScript can resolve and merge into the existing `Request` interface, and the file must itself be a module (have an `import`/`export`). Forget the import and you get *"Invalid module name in augmentation."*

---

### 📦 `declare module "x"` for Untyped Packages & Non-Code Imports

#### 🧩 What is it?
The same `declare module "x"` syntax has a second job: when a module has **no types at all**, you can declare its types *from scratch*. This covers two cases:
1. An npm package that ships only JavaScript, no `.d.ts`.
2. **Non-code imports** — `import styles from "./button.css"` or `import logo from "./logo.svg"`, which your *bundler* understands but TypeScript doesn't.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Import an untyped package and TS errors: *"Could not find a declaration file for module 'left-pad'."* Import a `.css` file and TS errors: *"Cannot find module './button.css'."* In both cases the runtime is fine (the package works; the bundler handles the CSS) — only the type-checker is stuck. You need to *teach* it the shape.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You write a `declare module` block in an **ambient context** — a `.d.ts` file (the standard choice). For one untyped package, name it exactly: `declare module "left-pad"`. For *all* files of a kind, use a **wildcard**: `declare module "*.css"`.

⚠️ **Critical distinction:** this only works as a *fresh* declaration when the module isn't otherwise resolvable, and it belongs in a `.d.ts` (or a global script). If you write `declare module "left-pad"` inside a regular `.ts` file that *also* has imports, TypeScript treats it as *augmentation* of an existing module — and since `left-pad` has no types to augment, you get the *"Invalid module name in augmentation"* error. Put fresh module declarations in a `.d.ts`.

```
  src/types/globals.d.ts          ← ambient context
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ declare module "left-pad" {  │  ← name an untyped pkg
  │   export default fn...        │
  │ }                             │
  │ declare module "*.css" {      │  ← wildcard: ALL .css imports
  │   const c: {[k:string]:string}│
  │   export default c            │
  │ }                             │
  └──────────────────────────────┘
        │   covers every matching import in the project
        ▼
  import styles from "./x.css"  → typed ✅
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like writing your **own instruction manual for a gadget that came with none**. The gadget (the package / the `.css` import) works perfectly when you plug it in (runtime) — but without a manual, *you* (TypeScript) don't know which buttons exist. You jot down "Button A = volume, Button B = power" (the `declare module`). A **wildcard** is one manual that covers *every gadget of that brand* (`*.css`).

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// ---- src/types/globals.d.ts (ambient — the right place) ----

// 1) An untyped npm package: declare its shape from scratch.
declare module "left-pad" {
  export default function leftPad(s: string, n: number): string;
}

// 2) Non-code imports your bundler handles, via a wildcard.
declare module "*.css" {
  const classes: { readonly [key: string]: string };
  export default classes;
}
declare module "*.svg" {
  const src: string;
  export default src;
}

// ---- anywhere in src/*.ts ----
// import leftPad from "left-pad";
// const padded: string = leftPad("7", 3);     // ✅ typed
// import styles from "./button.css";
// const cls: string = styles.primary;         // ✅ typed (string)

// ✅ Verified with `tsc --strict`: the .d.ts placement passes.
// ❌ Putting `declare module "left-pad"` inside a .ts module instead
//    fails with "Invalid module name in augmentation".
```

> **Note (simplification):** `declare module "*.css"` types every CSS import identically (a generic string-map). For *exact* per-file class names you'd use a CSS-Modules typed-plugin that generates a real `.d.ts` per file. The wildcard is the pragmatic, common default.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you add types to an untyped npm package?**
A: First check if `@types/that-package` exists on DefinitelyTyped and install it. If not, write your own ambient declaration in a `.d.ts` file: `declare module "pkg-name" { /* exported shapes */ }`. Even a minimal `declare module "pkg-name";` (which makes it `any`) silences the error. Put it in a `.d.ts`, not a regular module file, or it gets misread as augmentation.

---

### 🏷️ The `@types` Ecosystem & How `types` Resolves

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`@types/*`** packages (from a repository called **DefinitelyType­s**) are community-maintained `.d.ts` files for libraries that ship **without** their own types. `@types/react`, `@types/node`, `@types/lodash` — these contain *only* declaration files, no runtime code. They're the reason you rarely have to hand-write `declare module` yourself.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Tons of popular libraries (especially older ones) are written in plain JavaScript and publish no types. Every TypeScript user shouldn't have to re-write declarations for `lodash`. So the community writes them once, publishes them under the `@types/` scope on npm, and everyone shares them. TypeScript automatically picks them up.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Two resolution paths, in order of preference:

1. **Bundled types (best):** A modern package's `package.json` has a `"types"` (or `"typings"`) field pointing at its own `.d.ts`. TypeScript reads that directly — types and code ship together.
2. **`@types/*` fallback:** If there's no bundled types field, TypeScript looks in `node_modules/@types/<package>` for a matching declaration package, which you installed separately.

```
  import _ from "lodash"
        │
        ▼
  Does lodash/package.json have "types"? ── yes ──▶ use it
        │ no
        ▼
  Look in node_modules/@types/lodash ──── found ──▶ use it
        │ not found
        ▼
  ❌ "Could not find a declaration file..."
     → install @types/lodash, or write declare module
```

By default TypeScript auto-includes everything in `node_modules/@types`. You can narrow that with the `types` *compiler option* in `tsconfig.json` (a different "types" than the package.json field). For packages that support multiple TS versions, a `typesVersions` field maps TS version ranges to different declaration folders.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
DefinitelyTyped is a **community-run lost-and-found for instruction manuals**. A manufacturer (library author) shipped a gadget with no manual; volunteers wrote one and filed it in a shared library under the gadget's name. When you get that gadget, you check: did it *come with* a manual (`"types"` field)? If not, you grab the community one from the lost-and-found (`@types/*`).

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// package.json of a MODERN, self-typed library:
// {
//   "name": "zod",
//   "main": "lib/index.js",      // the runtime code
//   "types": "lib/index.d.ts"    // ← TS reads types straight from here
// }
// → no @types/zod needed.

// An OLDER untyped library (e.g. some-legacy-lib) → you add types yourself:
//   npm install --save-dev @types/some-legacy-lib
// Now TS finds node_modules/@types/some-legacy-lib automatically.

import _ from "lodash";       // works because @types/lodash is installed
const evens = _.filter([1, 2, 3, 4], n => n % 2 === 0); // fully typed

// tsconfig.json — the OTHER "types" (compiler option), to limit auto-include:
// { "compilerOptions": { "types": ["node", "jest"] } }
// ↑ now ONLY @types/node and @types/jest auto-load (perf / scope control).
```

> **Note:** The `"types"` field in a *package.json* (points at a `.d.ts`) and the `"types"` array in *tsconfig.json* (limits which `@types/*` auto-load) are **different things** that share a name. A frequent point of confusion.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Where do `@types/*` packages come from, and how does TypeScript decide which types to use for an import?**
A: They come from **DefinitelyTyped**, a community repo of declaration-only packages published under the `@types/` npm scope. For an import, TypeScript first checks the package's own `"types"`/`"typings"` field for bundled declarations; if absent, it falls back to `node_modules/@types/<name>`; if neither exists, it errors and you install the `@types` package or write a `declare module`.

---

### 🔗 `import type` vs Legacy Triple-Slash Directives

#### 🧩 What is it?
Two ways to pull a *type* from one file into another:
- **`import type { Foo } from "./foo"`** — the modern, explicit "this import is types-only" syntax.
- **Triple-slash directives** — old-style comments like `/// <reference path="./foo.d.ts" />` or `/// <reference types="node" />` that tell the compiler to include another declaration file.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A normal `import { Foo }` is ambiguous: is `Foo` a runtime value, a type, or both? Tools that compile files one at a time (Babel, esbuild, swc — they don't see your whole project) can't always tell, so they might leave a useless runtime `import` that breaks. **`import type`** removes the ambiguity: it's *guaranteed* to be erased and never emits a runtime import.

Before ES modules existed, triple-slash directives were the *only* way to say "also include this declaration file." Today they're **legacy** — kept mainly for ambient `.d.ts` wiring (like `/// <reference types="node" />` in a declaration file). For everyday code, `import type` replaced them.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`import type` is a compile-time-only link: the type is available for checking, then the whole line vanishes from the emitted JavaScript (guaranteed erasure). Triple-slash `/// <reference ... />` is a compiler instruction inside a comment that pulls in another file's declarations during checking; it also emits no JS.

```
  import type { User } from "./user";
        │  used only by the type-checker
        ▼
  emitted .js:  (nothing — line fully erased)

  /// <reference types="node" />   ← legacy, mostly in .d.ts
        │  "also load Node's ambient types"
        ▼
  emitted .js:  (nothing — it's a comment)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`import type` is checking a **blueprint** while building a house — you consult it constantly, but the blueprint isn't nailed into the walls; once the house is built, it's gone from the structure. A triple-slash directive is like an **old hand-written note in the margin** of that blueprint saying "also see drawing #7" — it still works, but modern teams use the clean labeled system (`import type`) instead of margin notes.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// MODERN: explicit, guaranteed-erased type import
import type { User } from "./user";          // types-only
import { saveUser } from "./user";           // runtime value

function show(u: User) {                      // U used as a type only
  return u.name.toUpperCase();
}
// Emitted JS keeps `import { saveUser }` but DROPS the `import type` line.

// You can also mix with an inline `type` modifier:
import { type Role, hasRole } from "./auth";  // Role erased, hasRole kept

// LEGACY (mostly seen inside .d.ts files):
/// <reference types="node" />
// Pulls in @types/node's ambient declarations. In app code today you'd
// just rely on tsconfig "types"/auto-include instead of writing this.
```

> **Note:** With the `verbatimModuleSyntax` / `isolatedModules` settings, using `import type` for type-only imports isn't just style — it's often *required* so single-file transpilers emit correct code. (Covered deeper in the Modules & Interop module.)

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why use `import type` instead of a regular `import`, and what replaced triple-slash directives?**
A: `import type` declares the import is types-only, so it's *guaranteed* erased from the output and never produces a runtime import — essential for single-file transpilers (Babel/esbuild/swc) and required under `isolatedModules`/`verbatimModuleSyntax`. Triple-slash directives (`/// <reference ... />`) were the pre-ESM way to include declaration files; modern `import`/`import type` and tsconfig's `types` largely replaced them, though directives linger in some ambient `.d.ts` plumbing.

---

### 📚 Authoring Types for a Library — What Consumers See

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Library authoring** here means: as the *author* of a package, deliberately shaping the `.d.ts` your users receive. The types you emit *are* your public API contract — separate from, and just as important as, the JavaScript you ship.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Consumers never read your source. They experience your library entirely through autocomplete and type errors — i.e. through your `.d.ts`. If your emitted types are wrong, leak internal helpers, or are confusing, your *users'* code is wrong or confusing, even though your runtime works. And once published, your types are an API: a breaking change to a type can break thousands of builds without changing a single line of runtime behavior.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You compile with `"declaration": true` so `tsc` emits `.d.ts` files next to your `.js`. Your `package.json` points consumers at them via the `"types"` field (and, for modern dual ESM/CommonJS packages, via the `"exports"` map's `"types"` condition). What ends up *exported* from your entry `.d.ts` is exactly what consumers see — so you control the surface by controlling exports.

```
  your-lib/src/index.ts  (types + impl)
        │  tsc --declaration
        ▼
  dist/index.js   + dist/index.d.ts   ← the public contract
        │
  package.json: { "types": "dist/index.d.ts" }
        │
        ▼
  consumer: import { thing } from "your-lib"
            sees ONLY what index.d.ts exports
```

The discipline: export your *intended* public types, keep internal helper types *unexported* (or mark them `@internal`), and treat type changes with the same care as runtime API changes — they're versioned the same way.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Authoring a library's types is like designing the **dashboard and controls of a car** versus the engine under the hood. Drivers (consumers) only ever touch the steering wheel, pedals, and dashboard — they never see the engine internals. If you put a confusing or wrongly-labeled control on the dashboard, every driver suffers, even if the engine is flawless. Your `.d.ts` *is* the dashboard.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// src/internal.ts — helpers you DON'T want consumers to see
export function _hashKey(k: string): number { return k.length; } // internal

// src/index.ts — your PUBLIC entry. Only what you export here is seen.
export interface CacheOptions {
  ttl: number;           // public, documented
  maxSize?: number;
}
export function createCache(opts: CacheOptions) {
  // uses _hashKey internally, but does NOT re-export it
  return { /* ... */ };
}
// NOTE: _hashKey is never exported from index → consumers never see it.

// Emitted dist/index.d.ts (what users get):
//   export interface CacheOptions { ttl: number; maxSize?: number; }
//   export declare function createCache(opts: CacheOptions): { ... };
// → clean public surface; internal helper stays hidden.

// package.json (so consumers resolve the types):
// {
//   "types": "dist/index.d.ts",
//   "exports": { ".": { "types": "./dist/index.d.ts",
//                       "import": "./dist/index.js" } }
// }
```

> **Note (simplification):** Real-world libraries often *bundle* their declarations (tools like `tsup` or `api-extractor`) into a single `.d.ts` and use `@internal` + `--stripInternal` to guarantee internals don't leak. The principle is the same: consumers see exactly your exported surface.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: As a library author, why are your emitted types part of your public API, and how do you control what consumers see?**
A: Consumers interact with your package only through its types (autocomplete, type-checking), so your `.d.ts` *is* a contract — a breaking type change breaks their builds even with identical runtime behavior. You control the surface by emitting declarations (`"declaration": true`), pointing `package.json`'s `"types"`/`exports` `"types"` at them, and **exporting only intended public types** while keeping internals unexported or marked `@internal`. Then version your types as carefully as your runtime API.

---

<h2 id="module-9">Module 9 — Modules, Resolution & Interop</h2>

You wrote `import { thing } from "./thing"` — but who decides *where* `"./thing"` actually lives on disk, whether it's an **ESM** (modern `import`/`export`) or **CommonJS** (older `require`/`module.exports`) file, and what the compiler should *emit* so it still runs? This module is about the rules TypeScript follows to find your imports, the flags that change what code comes out the other side, and the constraints modern build tools quietly impose on you.

---

### 🔀 ESM vs CommonJS — The Two Module Worlds TS Has to Bridge

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **module** is just a file that exports some things and imports others. JavaScript has **two** different module systems, and TypeScript has to understand both:

- **CommonJS (CJS)** — the older Node.js system. You use `require("x")` to import and `module.exports = ...` to export. It loads modules **synchronously** (one at a time, blocking).
- **ES Modules (ESM)** — the modern, official standard. You use `import` and `export` keywords. It's **statically analysable** (the imports are known before the code runs).

#### 🤔 Why do we need to know? (the problem)
TypeScript lets you *write* `import`/`export` syntax no matter what. But the file that actually runs might need to be CJS (because old Node) or ESM (because modern Node or a browser). If TypeScript guesses wrong about which world a file lives in, it can **emit the wrong kind of code**, and you get cryptic runtime errors like `require is not defined` or `Cannot use import statement outside a module`.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The same TypeScript `import` can be **emitted** (compiled out) into either system, depending on your config:

```
   You always WRITE:
     import { foo } from "./foo"
              │
   ┌──────────┴───────────┐
   ▼                      ▼
 emit as CJS         emit as ESM
 const { foo } =     import { foo }
   require("./foo")    from "./foo.js"
```

The deciding factor is the `module` and `moduleResolution` settings, plus the nearest `package.json` `"type"` field (`"type": "module"` means ESM, absent or `"commonjs"` means CJS).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
ESM and CJS are like **metric and imperial measurements**. The blueprint (your TypeScript) can be drawn once, but the factory floor either works in centimetres or inches. TypeScript is the engineer who re-labels your blueprint for whichever factory will build it — and if it mislabels, parts don't fit.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// math.ts — what you author (always looks the same)
export function add(a: number, b: number) {
  return a + b;
}

// ── Emitted as CommonJS (module: "commonjs") ──
// "use strict";
// Object.defineProperty(exports, "__esModule", { value: true });
// exports.add = add;
// function add(a, b) { return a + b; }

// ── Emitted as ESM (module: "esnext" / "nodenext") ──
// export function add(a, b) { return a + b; }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: TypeScript lets me write `import`. So why does my Node app sometimes crash with `Cannot use import statement outside a module`?**
A: Because the *authored* syntax and the *emitted* output are separate. If TypeScript emits real ESM (`import`) but Node is treating the file as CommonJS (no `"type": "module"`, a `.js` extension, and no other ESM signal), Node can't parse `import`. Fix it by aligning `module`/`moduleResolution` and `package.json` `"type"` so the emit matches how the file is actually loaded.

---

### 🏷️ `import type` / `export type` — Imports That Vanish

#### 🧩 What is it?
`import type { User } from "./user"` is an import that brings in **only a type**, never any runtime value. When TypeScript compiles, the whole line is **erased** — it produces no JavaScript at all. The same idea exists for `export type`.

This erasing of type-only imports is called **type elision** ("elision" just means *omitting / deleting* something).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A normal `import { User } from "./user"` is ambiguous: is `User` a *type* (which disappears at runtime) or a *value* (which must stay)? Usually the compiler figures it out — it sees `User` is only used as a type and quietly drops the import. But that "quiet dropping" is exactly the **simplification that bites single-file tools** (more on `isolatedModules` later). Marking the import with `type` makes your intent **explicit and unmissable**.

It also prevents accidental **side-effect imports**: a plain import of a module runs that module's top-level code; a `type` import guarantees it does not.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 import type { User } from "./user"   ──▶ (emits NOTHING)

 import { createUser } from "./user"  ──▶ stays in output
        (a value, used at runtime)

 import { type User, createUser }     ──▶ inline form:
        from "./user"                     User erased,
                                          createUser kept
```

The inline `import { type User, createUser }` form lets you mix a type and a value from the same module while still marking the type explicitly.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of packing for a trip with a **checked bag** and a **fitting-room try-on**. A value import is something you actually pack and take with you (it's there at runtime). A `type` import is clothing you only try on in the store to check the size — useful while planning, but it never comes home in the suitcase.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// user.ts
export interface User { id: string; name: string; }
export function greet(u: User) { return `Hi ${u.name}`; }

// app.ts
import { greet, type User } from "./user";

const u: User = { id: "1", name: "Ada" };
console.log(greet(u));

// Emitted JS — note `User` is gone, only the value `greet` survives:
//   import { greet } from "./user.js";
//   const u = { id: "1", name: "Ada" };
//   console.log(greet(u));
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why would you write `import type` instead of a plain `import`?**
A: To make the import provably type-only — it's guaranteed to be erased (no runtime cost, no accidental side effect of loading the module) and it's explicit for tools that compile files one at a time. It's also required by `isolatedModules`/`verbatimModuleSyntax` when re-exporting types.

---

### 🧭 `moduleResolution` — How TS Finds Your Imports

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Module resolution** is the algorithm TypeScript uses to turn a string like `"./user"` or `"lodash"` into an actual file on disk. The `moduleResolution` setting in `tsconfig.json` picks *which* algorithm. The main modern choices:

- **`node10`** (formerly just `node`) — the **legacy** Node.js CommonJS-style lookup.
- **`node16`** / **`nodenext`** — models how **modern Node** resolves modules, including the `package.json` `"exports"` map and required file extensions.
- **`bundler`** — for when a bundler (Vite, webpack, esbuild) does the real resolving; relaxed about extensions, understands `"exports"`, but doesn't enforce Node's stricter ESM rules.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Different runtimes look up files differently. Modern Node, for example, **requires** you to write the file extension in relative ESM imports (`"./user.js"`, not `"./user"`) and reads the package's `"exports"` field to decide what's importable. The old `node10` algorithm did neither. If TypeScript's resolution doesn't match your real runtime/bundler, types resolve to the wrong file — or an import that type-checks fine **fails at runtime**.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A simplified `node10` lookup for `"./user"` tries, in order:

```
 import "./user"  (moduleResolution: node10)
        │
        ▼ try as a file
   ./user.ts → ./user.tsx → ./user.d.ts → ./user.js
        │ (none found?)
        ▼ try as a folder
   ./user/index.ts → ./user/index.js ...
```

`node16`/`nodenext` instead respect the package's `"exports"` map and won't silently add extensions for ESM:

```
 import "pkg"  (moduleResolution: node16)
        │
        ▼ read pkg/package.json
   "exports": { ".": { "import": "./esm.js",
                       "require": "./cjs.js" } }
        │
        ▼ pick branch by how pkg is loaded
   ESM context → ./esm.js   CJS context → ./cjs.js
```

> **Note (simplification):** the real Node and TypeScript algorithms have many more steps (conditions like `"types"`, `"default"`, nested folders, `node_modules` walking up the tree). The diagrams show the core idea, not every branch.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`moduleResolution` is the **postal system's address rules**. `node10` is an old, lenient mail sorter that guesses missing apartment numbers. `node16`/`nodenext` is a strict modern courier that demands the full, exact address (extension and all) and reads the building's official directory (`"exports"`) before delivering. `bundler` is your office mailroom that knows everyone and is forgiving about format — but only because a human sorts it later.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// With moduleResolution: "node10" (legacy) this is fine:
import { add } from "./math";

// With moduleResolution: "nodenext" in an ESM package,
// the SAME line errors — modern Node ESM needs the extension:
//   error TS2835: Relative import paths need explicit file
//   extensions ... did you mean './math.js'?
import { add } from "./math.js"; // ✅ correct for nodenext ESM

// tsconfig.json
// {
//   "compilerOptions": {
//     "module": "nodenext",
//     "moduleResolution": "nodenext"
//   }
// }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `moduleResolution: "node10"`, `"nodenext"`, and `"bundler"`?**
A: `node10` is the legacy CommonJS lookup — lenient, ignores `"exports"`, auto-adds extensions. `nodenext` (and `node16`) model real modern Node: they honour `package.json` `"exports"`/`"type"` and require explicit extensions in ESM relative imports. `bundler` is for code a bundler will resolve — it understands `"exports"` and lets you omit extensions, since the bundler, not Node, does the final resolving.

---

### 🤝 `esModuleInterop` & `allowSyntheticDefaultImports` — Making CJS Feel Like ESM

#### 🧩 What is it?
Many old packages are **CommonJS** and export with `module.exports = something`. There's no real `export default`. So when you write `import express from "express"`, the **default import** doesn't naturally line up with a CJS module. Two flags fix this:

- **`esModuleInterop`** — changes the **emitted code** so default-importing a CJS module works at runtime, by wrapping it through a helper.
- **`allowSyntheticDefaultImports`** — a **types-only** flag that lets you *write* the default import without a type error. It changes what type-checks, not what's emitted.

#### 🤔 Why do we need them? (the problem)
Without interop, importing a CJS module the "ESM way" either won't type-check, or compiles to code that grabs the wrong object. The classic symptom: `import moment from "moment"` gives you `undefined` or `moment.default` instead of the function, because the CJS export wasn't shaped like an ESM default.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`esModuleInterop` injects a tiny runtime helper (`__importDefault`) that detects "is this a real ESM module, or a CJS one?" and exposes a consistent `.default`:

```
 import express from "express"

 WITHOUT esModuleInterop:
   const express = require("express");
   express(); // 💥 if express used module.exports

 WITH esModuleInterop (emit):
   const express = __importDefault(require("express"));
   express.default();   // helper normalises .default
```

`allowSyntheticDefaultImports` is purely a **type-system** permission — it's automatically turned on by `esModuleInterop`, so you rarely set it alone. It silences the "module has no default export" type error without touching the JavaScript output.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A CJS module is like a **gift wrapped without a "to/from" tag**. ESM expects a tag (`default`). `esModuleInterop` is the helpful assistant who, at runtime, **adds the missing tag** so the recipient can open it the normal way. `allowSyntheticDefaultImports` is just management saying "you're *allowed* to expect a tag" — but it's the assistant (interop) who actually attaches one.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// CommonJS package "greeter" uses:  module.exports = greet;
import greet from "greeter";

greet("world"); // works ONLY if the default import resolves to the fn

// esModuleInterop: true  → emits a __importDefault wrapper:
//   const greeter_1 = __importDefault(require("greeter"));
//   greeter_1.default("world");   // ✅ correct value

// esModuleInterop: false → emits:
//   const greeter_1 = require("greeter");
//   greeter_1.default("world");   // 💥 .default is undefined
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Explain `esModuleInterop` in terms of emitted code, and how it differs from `allowSyntheticDefaultImports`.**
A: `esModuleInterop` changes the **output**: default imports of CJS modules are wrapped in a `__importDefault` helper (and namespace imports in `__importStar`) so `.default`/`* as ns` behave correctly at runtime. `allowSyntheticDefaultImports` only affects **type-checking** — it lets you write the default import without an error — and is implied by `esModuleInterop`. One fixes runtime behaviour; the other just satisfies the type checker.

---

### 🧱 `isolatedModules` — Compiling One File at a Time

#### 🧩 What is it?
`isolatedModules: true` is a flag that tells TypeScript: *"check that every file can be safely compiled completely on its own, with no knowledge of any other file."* It doesn't change emit by itself — it **rejects code patterns** that only work when the compiler can see all files at once.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Fast build tools — **Babel, esbuild, swc** — transpile **one file at a time** ("single-file transpilation"). They strip types but **never type-check** and **can't look across files**. So if a construct's meaning depends on cross-file information, those tools will get it wrong. `isolatedModules` makes `tsc` flag those exact constructs up front, so your fast bundler doesn't silently produce broken output.

The key limitation those tools have: **no cross-file type information**. That causes two concrete rules:

1. **Re-exporting a type needs `export type`.** When you write `export { User } from "./user"`, a single-file tool can't tell whether `User` is a type (must be erased) or a value (must be kept) — it can't peek into `./user`. So you must say `export type { User }`.
2. **No `const enum` across files.** A `const enum`'s members get **inlined** at every use site, which requires reading the enum's definition from another file. A single-file tool can't do that, so `const enum` is disallowed (it errors, or with `preserveConstEnums` is treated like a regular enum).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
        Whole-program tsc            Single-file transpiler
        (sees everything)            (esbuild / babel / swc)

   a.ts ─┐                            a.ts ─▶ transpile ─▶ a.js
   b.ts ─┼─▶ one big picture          b.ts ─▶ transpile ─▶ b.js
   c.ts ─┘   knows all types          c.ts ─▶ transpile ─▶ c.js
                                       ▲ each file alone — NO
                                         cross-file type info
```

Because the right column can't see across files, `isolatedModules` bans anything that needs the left column's whole-program view.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Whole-program `tsc` is a **translator who has read the entire book** before translating, so they know what every pronoun refers to. A single-file transpiler is a translator handed **one page at a time** with no context — fast, but they'll mistranslate anything that depends on another page. `isolatedModules` is the editor's rule: *"don't write sentences that can't be understood from a single page alone."*

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// ❌ Fails under isolatedModules — ambiguous re-export:
//    is `User` a type or a value? A single-file tool can't know.
export { User } from "./user";

// ✅ Explicit — a single-file tool can erase it safely:
export type { User } from "./user";

// ❌ Fails under isolatedModules — needs cross-file inlining:
//    const enum Color { Red, Green }
//    error: const enums are not allowed when isolatedModules is set

// ✅ A normal enum (or a const object) is fine:
enum Color { Red, Green }
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `isolatedModules` change, and why do bundlers need it?**
A: It makes `tsc` reject constructs that can't be compiled correctly when each file is transpiled in isolation — re-exported types need `export type`, and `const enum` is banned — because tools like esbuild/Babel/swc process one file at a time with **no cross-file type information**. Turning it on ensures your code stays compatible with those fast single-file transpilers.

---

### ⛓️ `verbatimModuleSyntax` — Emit Imports Exactly As Written

#### 🧩 What is it?
`verbatimModuleSyntax: true` tells TypeScript to **emit your import/export statements literally** ("verbatim" = word-for-word), with one simple rule: anything written with `type` is **erased**, and everything else is **kept exactly**. It removes the compiler's old, clever guessing about which imports to drop.

It replaces the older, more confusing flags `importsNotUsedAsValues` and `preserveValueImports`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Historically TypeScript would *automatically* delete an import it judged to be type-only. That auto-elision is the same magic that single-file tools can't replicate, and it occasionally dropped imports whose **side effects** you actually wanted. `verbatimModuleSyntax` makes the rule **mechanical and predictable**: `type` → gone, no `type` → stays. What you write is what you get.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
 verbatimModuleSyntax: true

 import { type T, value } from "x"
        │
        ▼  erase the `type` part, keep the rest verbatim
 import { value } from "x"

 import type { T } from "x"   ──▶ erased entirely
 import "./styles.css"        ──▶ KEPT (side-effect import preserved)
```

The trade-off: you must be **disciplined** — every type-only import has to be marked `type`, or it stays in the output (which may break in an ESM-only or CJS-only context).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
The old behaviour was an **over-helpful editor** who silently deleted sentences they assumed you didn't need — occasionally cutting one that mattered. `verbatimModuleSyntax` is a **strict typesetter** who prints exactly what's on the page and only removes text you clearly marked with a "delete this" highlighter (`type`). No surprises, but you have to mark things yourself.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
import { type User, createUser } from "./user";
import "./analytics"; // side-effect only

const u = createUser();

// Emitted with verbatimModuleSyntax: true —
// `type User` erased, value + side-effect import kept verbatim:
//   import { createUser } from "./user.js";
//   import "./analytics.js";
//   const u = createUser();
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What problem does `verbatimModuleSyntax` solve?**
A: It removes TypeScript's automatic, hard-to-predict import elision. The rule becomes simple: imports marked `type` are erased, everything else is emitted exactly as written — preserving side-effect imports and keeping output compatible with single-file transpilers. It supersedes the older `importsNotUsedAsValues` and `preserveValueImports` flags.

---

### 🗂️ Project References & Composite Builds — Splitting a Monorepo

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **monorepo** is one repository holding many packages (e.g. `web`, `api`, `shared`). **Project references** let you split one big TypeScript program into several smaller `tsconfig.json` projects that **depend on each other explicitly**, and build them in the right order. A project that *can be referenced* must set `"composite": true`.

You build the whole graph with `tsc --build` (a.k.a. **build mode**), which figures out dependency order and skips projects that haven't changed.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
With one giant project, **every** type-check re-reads **all** the code — slow, and it couples everything together. Project references give you: (1) faster builds via **incremental, cached** rebuilds of only what changed, (2) enforced **boundaries** (package A can only use what it explicitly references), and (3) correct **build ordering** (`shared` builds before `api` that uses it).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each referenced project emits a `.d.ts` (its public types) plus a `.tsbuildinfo` cache. Dependents read the **emitted `.d.ts`**, not the raw source, so they don't re-check it. `tsc --build` walks the graph:

```
   reference graph (arrows = "depends on")

        web ──────┐
                  ▼
   api ───────▶ shared
   │
   └────────────┘

   tsc --build order: shared → api → web
   (unchanged projects are skipped via .tsbuildinfo)
```

```jsonc
// api/tsconfig.json
{
  "compilerOptions": { "composite": true, "outDir": "dist" },
  "references": [{ "path": "../shared" }]
}
```

> **Note (simplification):** project references is a build-mode feature; bundlers and other tools may build the monorepo differently. The win — incremental, ordered, boundary-enforced builds — is specific to using `tsc --build`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of assembling a car on an **assembly line with stations**. You can't bolt on the doors before the chassis exists. Project references declare *"doors depend on chassis"*, so the line runs stations in the right order — and if only the doors changed, you don't rebuild the whole chassis. The `.tsbuildinfo` cache is the foreman's clipboard tracking which stations are already done.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// Root tsconfig.json — the build graph entry point
{
  "files": [],
  "references": [
    { "path": "./shared" },
    { "path": "./api" },
    { "path": "./web" }
  ]
}
```
```bash
# Build everything in dependency order, skipping unchanged projects:
tsc --build            # or: tsc -b
tsc --build --watch    # rebuild only what changed, on save
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do project references speed up a monorepo build?**
A: They split the codebase into separate `composite` projects with explicit `references`. `tsc --build` builds them in dependency order and **caches** each via `.tsbuildinfo`, so unchanged projects are skipped. Dependents consume each project's emitted `.d.ts` instead of re-type-checking its source, so a change in one package doesn't force a full re-check of the whole repo — and the references also enforce clean boundaries between packages.

---

### 🪧 Path Mapping (`paths` / `baseUrl`) — Pretty Imports That Need a Runtime Helper

#### 🧩 What is it?
`baseUrl` and `paths` in `tsconfig.json` let you create **import aliases** — short, stable names instead of long relative paths. For example, mapping `"@/utils/date"` to `"./src/utils/date"` so you can write `import { fmt } from "@/utils/date"` from anywhere.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Deep relative imports like `import x from "../../../../utils/date"` are ugly, fragile, and break when you move files. Aliases stay stable. **But here's the trap:** `paths` only teaches the **TypeScript type-checker** where things are. TypeScript **does not rewrite** the alias in the emitted JavaScript — `import "@/utils/date"` is left as-is. At runtime, Node has no idea what `@/...` means, so you get `Cannot find module '@/utils/date'` **unless a runtime resolver also knows the mapping**.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
   "@/utils/date"
        │
        ├──▶ TYPE-CHECK time: tsconfig `paths` resolves
        │     it to ./src/utils/date  ✅ (types found)
        │
        └──▶ RUNTIME: emit keeps "@/utils/date" literally.
              Node alone: 💥 Cannot find module
              Needs a resolver:
                • bundler alias (Vite/webpack/tsconfig-paths)
                • tsc-alias / tsconfig-paths at run time
```

So a `paths` alias needs **two** agreements: the compiler (via `tsconfig`) **and** whatever loads the code (bundler config, or a tool like `tsconfig-paths`/`tsc-alias` that rewrites or resolves the alias).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A `paths` alias is a **nickname**. Your TypeScript-savvy friends (the type-checker) know "Beanie" means "Robert." But if you send a parcel addressed to "Beanie," the **postal service** (Node at runtime) has never heard the nickname and returns it undelivered. You must register the nickname with the post office too (a runtime resolver) for delivery to work.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// tsconfig.json
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "baseUrl": ".",
    "paths": {
      "@/*": ["src/*"]   // "@/utils/date" → "src/utils/date"
    }
  }
}
```
```typescript
// Type-checks fine; emitted JS still says "@/utils/date":
import { fmt } from "@/utils/date";

// Run with plain `node dist/app.js`:
//   Error: Cannot find module '@/utils/date'
// Fixes (pick one):
//   • Bundler resolves it (Vite/webpack/Next.js read tsconfig paths)
//   • Register tsconfig-paths at runtime
//   • Rewrite paths after build with tsc-alias
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: My `@/...` imports type-check fine but crash at runtime with "Cannot find module." Why?**
A: `paths`/`baseUrl` only inform the TypeScript **type-checker** — TypeScript does **not** rewrite the alias in emitted JavaScript.
