At runtime the alias is meaningless unless a runtime resolver also maps it: a bundler that reads `tsconfig` paths, or a tool like `tsconfig-paths` (resolve at load) or `tsc-alias` (rewrite after build).

---

<h2 id="module-10">Module 10 — tsconfig & Strictness Flags</h2>

`tsconfig.json` is the **rulebook** you hand the TypeScript compiler — it decides which JavaScript version to emit, where to find your code, and (most importantly) *how paranoid* the type-checker is allowed to be. The same line of code can be perfectly legal or a hard error depending on a single flag. This module walks the dials that matter: the compilation surface, the `strict` family flag-by-flag, the extra-strict flags beyond it, the build-performance flags, and how to roll all of it out on a real codebase without drowning in errors.

---

### 🎛️ The Compilation Surface — Where Code Comes From and Goes To

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **compilation surface** is the group of `tsconfig` options that describe the *mechanics* of compiling: which JavaScript syntax to output (`target`), which built-in APIs to assume exist (`lib`), what kind of `import`/`export` to emit (`module`), how to find imported files (`moduleResolution`), how to handle React-style `<tags>` (`jsx`), and which folders to read from and write to (`rootDir` / `outDir`). None of these change *how strict* type-checking is — they describe the **input and output** of the compiler.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
TypeScript — the language that adds types to JavaScript — has to turn your `.ts` files into plain `.js` that a browser or Node (the JavaScript runtime that runs code outside the browser) can execute. To do that it must answer concrete questions: *Can I emit `async`/`await` directly, or must I rewrite it for an old browser? Does `Array.prototype.flat` exist in the environment I'm targeting? Should `import x from "y"` stay as-is or become a `require`?* Get these wrong and your code either won't run or won't compile against the right APIs.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Think of `tsc` (the TypeScript compiler) as a pipeline. The surface flags configure each end:

```
  YOUR .ts FILES                          EMITTED .js + .d.ts
  ┌──────────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐
  │ rootDir:     │──▶ │   tsc     │──▶ │ outDir:          │
  │   ./src      │    │           │    │   ./dist         │
  └──────────────┘    └───────────┘    └──────────────────┘
        ▲    target  ─┘ │ │ └─ module (import vs require)
        │    lib     ───┘ └─── jsx    (how <Tag/> compiles)
   moduleResolution (how it finds imported files)
```

- **`target`** — the JavaScript *version* of the emitted code, e.g. `"ES2022"`. A lower target makes `tsc` rewrite newer syntax into older equivalents (called **downleveling**); a higher target emits modern syntax untouched.
- **`lib`** — the set of built-in type definitions to assume, e.g. `["ES2022", "DOM"]`. This is what makes `document` or `Array.prototype.at` *known to the type-checker*. `target` picks a sensible default `lib`, but you can override it (e.g. drop `"DOM"` for a Node-only project).
- **`module`** — the module format of the output: `"ESNext"` (keep `import`/`export`), `"CommonJS"` (emit `require`), `"NodeNext"`, etc.
- **`moduleResolution`** — the algorithm for turning `import "./foo"` into an actual file on disk: `"bundler"`, `"node16"`/`"nodenext"`, or the legacy `"node10"`.
- **`jsx`** — how to compile JSX (the `<Component />` syntax): `"preserve"` (leave it for a bundler), `"react-jsx"` (the modern automatic runtime), etc.
- **`rootDir`** / **`outDir`** — the input folder the source tree is measured from, and the output folder for emitted files.

> **Note (simplification):** `target` and `lib` are related but distinct — `target` controls *emitted syntax*, `lib` controls *which API typings exist*. You can target old syntax while still typing against new APIs (and then polyfill them yourself).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a **translation-and-shipping office**. `target` is *which dialect* you translate into (modern slang vs. formal old-style). `lib` is *which dictionary* you allow on the desk (does the word `flatMap` even exist?). `module` is the *envelope format* — a sealed ESM packet or a CommonJS parcel. `rootDir` is the **inbox** the letters arrive in; `outDir` is the **outbox** the translated copies go to.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// tsconfig.json — a typical modern app surface
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "target": "ES2022",          // emit modern syntax (no downleveling)
    "lib": ["ES2022", "DOM"],    // assume ES2022 APIs + browser globals
    "module": "ESNext",          // keep import/export in the output
    "moduleResolution": "Bundler",
    "jsx": "react-jsx",          // modern JSX runtime, no `import React`
    "rootDir": "./src",
    "outDir": "./dist"
  }
}
```

```typescript
// What `lib` controls — this file ONLY type-checks if "ES2022" is in lib:
const last = [1, 2, 3].at(-1);   // Array.prototype.at is ES2022
// With lib: ["ES2015"] you'd get:
//   error TS2339: Property 'at' does not exist on type 'number[]'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between `target` and `lib`?**
A: `target` decides the *JavaScript version of the emitted code* — a lower target makes `tsc` downlevel modern syntax (e.g. rewrite `async`/`await`) into an older form. `lib` decides *which built-in API type-definitions the checker assumes exist* (e.g. `DOM`, `ES2022`). They're independent: you can target old syntax but still type against newer APIs (then polyfill them). `target` ships a default `lib`, but you can override `lib` explicitly — for example dropping `"DOM"` in a pure Node project so `document` is correctly *not* available.

---

### 🛡️ The `strict` Family — One Flag That Turns On Seven

#### 🧩 What is it?
`"strict": true` is a single switch that turns on a **family** of stricter checks at once: `strictNullChecks`, `noImplicitAny`, `strictFunctionTypes`, `strictBindCallApply`, `strictPropertyInitialization`, `useUnknownInCatchVariables`, and `alwaysStrict`. Setting `strict: true` flips all of them on; you can still override any individual member back to `false`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
TypeScript was designed to be adoptable on existing JavaScript, so its *defaults are lenient* — without `strict`, a parameter with no type silently becomes `any` (the "anything goes" type that disables checking), and `null` is assignable to everything. That defeats the entire point of adding types. `strict: true` is the switch that turns TypeScript from "JavaScript with optional hints" into "a type system that actually catches bugs." It's also future-proof: when a new TypeScript version adds a strict check, `strict: true` opts you in automatically.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
`strict` is an *umbrella*. Turning it on is equivalent to listing all seven members as `true`. Each member then becomes the **default** for its check, and an explicit setting wins over the umbrella:

```
  "strict": true
        │  (expands to)
        ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ strictNullChecks            (null/undefined)  │
  │ noImplicitAny               (no silent any)   │
  │ strictFunctionTypes         (param variance)  │
  │ strictBindCallApply         (typed bind/call) │
  │ strictPropertyInitialization(class fields)    │
  │ useUnknownInCatchVariables  (catch e: unknown)│
  │ alwaysStrict                ("use strict")    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  An explicit "strictNullChecks": false OVERRIDES the umbrella.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`strict` is the **"lock all doors" button** by your front entrance. One press bolts every door and window. But you can still walk to one window and manually unlock it (override a single flag). And when the builder later adds a *new* door to the house, the master button locks that one too — you don't have to remember it exists.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// Recommended baseline:
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "strict": true,
    // Override one member if a migration truly needs it (temporarily):
    "strictPropertyInitialization": false
  }
}
```

```typescript
// With strict:false this compiles silently; with strict:true it errors:
function greet(name) {        // error TS7006: Parameter 'name' implicitly
  return "hi " + name;        //   has an 'any' type.  (noImplicitAny)
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `"strict": true` actually do, and can you turn off just one of its checks?**
A: It's an umbrella that enables seven stricter checks at once (null-checking, no implicit `any`, function-param variance, typed `bind`/`call`/`apply`, class property initialization, `unknown` catch variables, and `"use strict"` emit). It also *auto-opts-in* to any new strict check future TypeScript versions add. You can override any single member by listing it explicitly — e.g. `"strict": true` with `"strictPropertyInitialization": false` keeps everything strict except that one check.

---

### 🚫 `strictNullChecks` — Stop `null` From Hiding Everywhere

#### 🧩 What is it?
`strictNullChecks` changes a deep rule: `null` and `undefined` are **no longer assignable to every type**. With it off, a `string` secretly also accepts `null` and `undefined`. With it on, those two become their own types that you must include explicitly (e.g. `string | null`) and **narrow** — prove a value isn't null — before using it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
"Cannot read properties of null" is the single most common JavaScript runtime crash. Without `strictNullChecks`, the type-checker is blind to it: it thinks every `string` is always a real string, so `s.length` always looks safe — even when `s` is `null` at runtime. This flag is the one that actually catches the billion-dollar mistake.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
With the flag **on**, `null` and `undefined` are pulled *out* of every other type and made distinct. A value that might be missing must say so in its type, and the compiler then forces you to check before you dereference it:

```
  strictNullChecks: OFF        strictNullChecks: ON
  ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────┐
  │ string           │         │ string           │  (never null)
  │  ⊇ null          │         └──────────────────┘
  │  ⊇ undefined     │         ┌──────────────────┐
  │ (silently)       │         │ string | null    │  (must narrow)
  └──────────────────┘         └──────────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's the difference between a form that says *"Phone (optional)"* with no validation, versus one that **forces you to tick a box** confirming "I have no phone." In the first, an empty box sails through and breaks something downstream. In the second, the *absence* is made explicit and you can't ignore it.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// strictNullChecks: true
function len(s: string | null): number {
  return s.length;
  //     ^ error TS18047: 's' is possibly 'null'.
}

// The fix — narrow first, then the call is safe:
function lenSafe(s: string | null): number {
  if (s === null) return 0;   // narrowed: below, s is just `string`
  return s.length;            // ✅ no error
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `strictNullChecks` change about the type of every value?**
A: It removes the rule that `null` and `undefined` are members of *every* type. With it off, a `string` silently also accepts `null`/`undefined`, so the compiler can't warn you about `null.length`. With it on, `null` and `undefined` are distinct types you must add explicitly (`string | null`) and narrow away — via a check like `if (s !== null)` — before using the value. It's the flag that catches "cannot read properties of null" crashes at compile time.

---

### 🕵️ `noImplicitAny`, `strictFunctionTypes`, `strictBindCallApply` — Plugging the Quiet Holes

#### 🧩 What is it?
Three more members of the `strict` family, each closing a specific gap:
- **`noImplicitAny`** — error when the compiler would have to *silently fall back* to `any` (e.g. an untyped parameter). You must annotate or let it be inferred from a value.
- **`strictFunctionTypes`** — makes function-*parameter* type-compatibility checked **contravariantly** (more carefully) for function-typed values, so you can't assign a function that demands a narrower argument where a wider one is expected.
- **`strictBindCallApply`** — makes `.bind()`, `.call()`, and `.apply()` (ways to call a function with a chosen `this` and arguments) type-check their arguments instead of accepting anything.

> **Note (simplification):** `strictFunctionTypes` only tightens *function-type* positions, and notably it does **not** apply to **method** syntax (`{ m(x): void }`) — methods stay bivariantly checked for historical reasons. It mainly affects function *properties* and standalone function types.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Each plugs a place where unsafe code used to pass:
- An untyped parameter becoming `any` means a typo on it is never caught.
- Without `strictFunctionTypes`, a callback expecting a *narrower* type could be handed a *wider* value and blow up.
- Without `strictBindCallApply`, `fn.call(null, "oops")` would accept a string where `fn` wanted a number — the exact bug types are supposed to prevent.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- `noImplicitAny`: if the compiler can't *infer* a type and you didn't annotate, it errors instead of defaulting to `any`.
- `strictFunctionTypes`: when checking `funcA` is assignable to `funcB`, parameter types are compared in the **safe (contravariant)** direction — `funcA`'s params must be *as wide or wider* than `funcB`'s.
- `strictBindCallApply`: `bind`/`call`/`apply` use the function's real parameter types, so wrong argument types error.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`noImplicitAny` is a **customs officer** who refuses any package with a blank "contents" label — no more "I'll just wave it through." `strictFunctionTypes` is checking that a *replacement worker* can handle **at least** the range of tasks the job requires, not a narrower slice. `strictBindCallApply` is making sure that when you pre-fill a form's fields (`bind`), the values you write actually fit the boxes.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// noImplicitAny
function greet(name) { return "hi " + name; }
//             ^ error TS7006: Parameter 'name' implicitly has an 'any' type.

// strictFunctionTypes
type Handler = (x: string | number) => void;
const h: Handler = (x: string) => {};
//    ^ error TS2322: Type '(x: string) => void' is not assignable to
//      type 'Handler'. ('number' is not assignable to 'string')

// strictBindCallApply
function fn(a: number) { return a; }
fn.call(null, "oops");
//            ^ error TS2345: Argument of type 'string' is not
//              assignable to parameter of type 'number'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why does `strictFunctionTypes` exist, and when does it *not* apply?**
A: It makes function-parameter compatibility checked contravariantly, so you can't assign a function that demands a *narrower* parameter where a function accepting a *wider* one is expected — which would crash when called with the wider value. The catch: it deliberately does **not** apply to *method* syntax (`{ m(x): void }`), which stays bivariant for historical/practical reasons (arrays and event handlers would otherwise be painful). So a function written as a property is checked strictly; the same thing written as a method is not.

---

### 🏗️ `strictPropertyInitialization`, `useUnknownInCatchVariables`, `alwaysStrict` — The Last Three

#### 🧩 What is it?
The remaining `strict` members:
- **`strictPropertyInitialization`** — a class field with a non-optional type must be assigned a value (in its declaration or the constructor), or it's an error. (Requires `strictNullChecks` to take effect.)
- **`useUnknownInCatchVariables`** — the variable in `catch (e)` is typed `unknown` instead of `any`, forcing you to narrow before using it.
- **`alwaysStrict`** — parses files in ECMAScript **strict mode** and emits `"use strict";` at the top of output (the JS directive that bans sloppy behaviors like implicit globals).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
- A class field declared `name: string` but never assigned is `undefined` at runtime — a crash waiting in any method that reads it. `strictPropertyInitialization` catches that.
- `catch (e)` getting `any` means people write `e.message` blindly, but you can `throw` *anything* in JavaScript (a string, a number) — so `e` genuinely might not have `.message`. `unknown` forces a check.
- `alwaysStrict` removes whole categories of silent footguns (accidental globals, duplicate params) by enforcing strict mode.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- The compiler tracks whether each non-optional field is *definitely assigned* by the end of the constructor; if not, it errors.
- `e` in `catch` is given type `unknown`; you must narrow it (`if (e instanceof Error)`) before accessing members.
- `alwaysStrict` flips the parser into strict mode and prepends `"use strict";` to emitted files.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`strictPropertyInitialization` is a **pre-flight checklist**: the plane can't depart until every required switch is confirmed set — no "I'll fill the fuel field later." `useUnknownInCatchVariables` is treating a **lost-and-found package with no label** as genuinely unknown contents — you inspect it before assuming what's inside, because you don't know who threw it.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// strictPropertyInitialization (needs strictNullChecks)
class User {
  name: string;
  //   ^ error TS2564: Property 'name' has no initializer and is not
  //     definitely assigned in the constructor.
}

// useUnknownInCatchVariables
try {
  // ...
} catch (e) {
  console.log(e.message);
  //          ^ error TS18046: 'e' is of type 'unknown'.
  // Fix: narrow first
  if (e instanceof Error) console.log(e.message); // ✅ safe
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why is the `catch` variable typed `unknown` under strict mode, and what must you do with it?**
A: Because JavaScript lets you `throw` *any* value — a string, a number, `null`, not just an `Error` — so the compiler can't assume `e` has `.message` or a `.stack`. `useUnknownInCatchVariables` types it `unknown` (the safe top type) so you're forced to **narrow** before use, typically `if (e instanceof Error) { ... }`. It prevents the very common bug of reading `e.message` on something that isn't an `Error`.

---

### 🔍 `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` — The Flag `strict` Forgot

#### 🧩 What is it?
`noUncheckedIndexedAccess` makes **indexed access** — reading `arr[i]` on an array, or `obj[key]` through an index signature — add `| undefined` to the result type. So `number[]` indexed by a number gives `number | undefined`, not `number`. It is **not** part of `strict`; you turn it on separately.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
By default, `const x = arr[10]` is typed `number` even though index `10` may not exist — at runtime you get `undefined` and the next line crashes. The default lies to you for ergonomics (indexing would be annoying otherwise). This flag makes the type tell the truth: *you don't actually know this slot is filled.*

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
For any access through a numeric index or an index signature, the compiler unions `undefined` into the type, forcing you to check before using it:

```
  arr: number[]            access      resulting type
  ┌─────────────────┐    ──────────▶   number | undefined
  │  noUncheckedIndexedAccess: true │
  └─────────────────────────────────┘
  Now arr[i].toFixed() errors until you handle `undefined`.
```

> **Note (simplification):** the flag covers index *reads* via signatures and numeric indexing. It does **not** narrow based on a prior length/bounds check, and it doesn't touch direct property access like `obj.known` on a declared field.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's the difference between assuming **seat 10F exists on every plane** and a booking system that answers "seat 10F? *maybe* — let me check the aircraft first." Small planes don't have a row 10. The flag forces you to confirm the seat is real before you try to sit in it.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// noUncheckedIndexedAccess: true
const arr: number[] = [1, 2, 3];
const x = arr[10];        // type is now `number | undefined`
x.toFixed(2);
// ^ error TS18048: 'x' is possibly 'undefined'.

// Fix — check first:
if (x !== undefined) x.toFixed(2);   // ✅ narrowed to `number`
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` protect against, and why isn't it in `strict`?**
A: It adds `| undefined` to anything read via array indexing or an index signature, so `arr[i]` is `T | undefined` rather than `T` — catching the very common crash where you index a position that doesn't exist and get `undefined` at runtime. It isn't part of `strict` because it's noisier and not strictly a "soundness must" — it trades ergonomics for safety, so the team opts in deliberately.

---

### 🧱 Beyond Strict — `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, `noImplicitOverride`, `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`

#### 🧩 What is it?
Three more *opt-in* flags (none included in `strict`) that tighten specific corners:
- **`exactOptionalPropertyTypes`** — an optional property `mode?: "a"` means "absent **or** `"a"`", but **not** "explicitly set to `undefined`". Assigning `{ mode: undefined }` becomes an error.
- **`noImplicitOverride`** — a method that overrides a base-class method must say so with the `override` keyword.
- **`noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`** — values that come from an index signature must be read with bracket syntax (`obj["key"]`), not dot syntax (`obj.key`).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
- Without `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, "the key is missing" and "the key is present but `undefined`" are conflated — yet `"prop" in obj` and `Object.keys` treat them differently, causing subtle bugs.
- Without `noImplicitOverride`, renaming a base method silently turns a child's *override* into a brand-new unrelated method — a classic refactor bug.
- Without `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`, `config.someTypo` reads from a `[key: string]` signature and looks valid even when the key is a typo; bracket syntax makes "this is a dynamic lookup, not a known property" explicit.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The compiler tightens each rule: it distinguishes *missing* from `undefined`; it requires `override` on overriding members; and it forbids dot access for index-signature-sourced properties (declared fields are still dot-accessible).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`exactOptionalPropertyTypes` is the difference between **"no answer given"** and **"answered: blank"** on a survey — they're not the same and shouldn't be treated as such. `noImplicitOverride` is requiring a worker to **sign the form** saying "I am replacing the previous procedure," so renaming the original doesn't leave two procedures running silently.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// exactOptionalPropertyTypes: true
interface Opts { mode?: "a" | "b"; }
const o: Opts = { mode: undefined };
// ^ error TS2375: Type '{ mode: undefined; }' is not assignable ...
//   'undefined' is not assignable to type '"a" | "b"'.

// noImplicitOverride: true
class Base { greet() {} }
class Sub extends Base { greet() {} }
//                      ^ error TS4114: This member must have an
//                        'override' modifier because it overrides ...

// noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature: true
interface Env { [key: string]: string; }
declare const env: Env;
const v = env.PATH;
//            ^ error TS4111: Property 'PATH' comes from an index
//              signature, so it must be accessed with ['PATH'].
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` enforces, and why does it matter?**
A: It separates *"the property is absent"* from *"the property is present and set to `undefined`"*. Without it, an optional `mode?: string` silently accepts `mode: undefined`, even though at runtime `"mode" in obj` and `Object.keys(obj)` would report the key as present. With it on, you can't assign an explicit `undefined` to an optional property unless its type literally includes `undefined` — keeping the type honest about presence vs. value.

---

### ⚡ Build & Declaration Flags — `skipLibCheck`, `declaration`, `incremental`, `composite`

#### 🧩 What is it?
A set of flags about **emit and build performance** rather than strictness:
- **`skipLibCheck`** — skip type-checking *inside* `.d.ts` files (declaration files — type-only files with no runtime code), including ones from `node_modules`.
- **`declaration`** — emit `.d.ts` files alongside your `.js`, so consumers of your library get types.
- **`incremental`** — save a `.tsbuildinfo` cache so the next compile only re-checks what changed.
- **`composite`** — mark a project as a buildable unit that other projects can reference (**project references**); it implies `declaration` and `incremental`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
- `skipLibCheck` exists because third-party `.d.ts` files sometimes conflict with each other or use newer features — checking them all wastes time and surfaces errors you can't fix. Skipping them is a near-universal speed win.
- `declaration` is mandatory for **publishing a library** — without it consumers get no types.
- `incremental` and `composite` make repeated builds (and monorepos) dramatically faster by caching and splitting work.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  First build                Later build (incremental: true)
  ┌──────────────┐           ┌──────────────┐
  │ check ALL    │           │ read         │
  │ files        │           │ .tsbuildinfo │
  │ write        │           │ recheck ONLY │
  │ .tsbuildinfo │ ────────▶ │ changed files│
  └──────────────┘           └──────────────┘
  composite = incremental + declaration + referencable unit
```

> **Note (simplification):** `skipLibCheck` doesn't skip *all* type-checking — your own `.ts` files are still fully checked. It only skips checking the *contents* of `.d.ts` files; their types are still *used* when checking your code.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`skipLibCheck` is **trusting a supplier's safety certificate** instead of re-inspecting every part they ship — you still use the parts, you just don't re-audit their factory. `incremental` is a **bookmark in a long book**: next time you don't re-read from page one, you resume where the changes are.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// Library config emitting types, with fast rebuilds:
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "strict": true,
    "skipLibCheck": true,     // don't re-check node_modules' .d.ts
    "declaration": true,      // emit .d.ts for consumers
    "incremental": true,      // cache build info for fast rebuilds
    "composite": true,        // make this a referencable project
    "outDir": "./dist"
  }
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does `skipLibCheck` do — does it make your code less type-safe?**
A: It skips type-checking the *contents* of `.d.ts` declaration files (especially in `node_modules`), which is a big speed win and avoids errors inside library typings you can't fix. It does **not** weaken checking of your own code — your `.ts` files are still fully checked, and the library types are still *used* when checking your usage of them. The only risk is that a genuine bug *inside* a `.d.ts` won't be reported, which is an acceptable trade-off for almost every app.

---

### 🪜 Strict-by-Default + a Migration Ramp — Rolling It Out Without Drowning

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **strategy**: new projects start with `strict: true` (plus the extra flags) from day one, while *existing* loose codebases turn strictness on **gradually** — one flag at a time — so the error count stays manageable and the team never faces 10,000 errors at once.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Flipping `strict: true` on a large untyped codebase can surface thousands of errors instantly — overwhelming, un-reviewable, and likely to get reverted. A ramp lets you make steady, mergeable progress and lock in each gain so the codebase can't backslide (a **ratchet**: strictness only ever increases).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Enable the *highest-value, lowest-noise* flags first, fix the fallout, then add the next:

```
  Migration ramp (turn on left-to-right, fix, then next):

  noImplicitAny ─▶ strictNullChecks ─▶ rest of strict ─▶
       (annotate)    (biggest payoff,     (function types,
                       most errors)         catch vars, etc.)
  ─▶ noUncheckedIndexedAccess ─▶ exactOptionalPropertyTypes
       (noisier, opt-in extras — last)
```

1. Turn on `noImplicitAny` first — it forces annotations and is mostly mechanical.
2. Then `strictNullChecks` — the biggest payoff, but the most errors; do it module-by-module if needed.
3. Then the rest of `strict` (set `strict: true`, override stragglers temporarily).
4. Finally the opt-in extras (`noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`) once the base is clean.
5. Use a CI check (e.g. counting `@ts-expect-error` markers, or per-file strict opt-in) as a **ratchet** so the number can only go down.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's **acclimatizing to altitude** on a mountain climb. You don't sprint to the summit — you ascend to a camp, let your body adjust, then climb the next stage. Each camp is a checkpoint you don't slide back from. Turning on every flag at once is the altitude sickness that sends you back down.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// Step 1 — start the ramp on a legacy project:
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "noImplicitAny": true       // first rung: force annotations
    // strictNullChecks: NOT YET — turn on after this is green
  }
}
```

```jsonc
// Final state — strict-by-default for a healthy codebase:
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "strict": true,
    "noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true,
    "exactOptionalPropertyTypes": true,
    "noImplicitOverride": true,
    "skipLibCheck": true
  }
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Which strict flags would you turn on first in a legacy codebase, and why?**
A: `noImplicitAny` first — it's mostly mechanical (add annotations) and immediately stops new untyped code. Then `strictNullChecks`, because it catches the highest-value class of bugs (null/undefined crashes) — but expect the most errors, so enable it module-by-module if the codebase is large. After those are green, set `strict: true` for the remaining members, then layer on the noisier opt-in extras (`noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`) last. Throughout, add a CI **ratchet** so strictness can only increase, never regress.

---

<h2 id="module-11">Module 11 — Runtime Validation & The Type Boundary</h2>

You wrote `interface User { age: number }`, your code compiled green, and then production crashed because the server sent `age: "42"` — a string. How is that possible if TypeScript "checked" the type? This module is about the most important truth in TypeScript: **your types are a compile-time promise, not a runtime guarantee.** We'll learn where that promise breaks (the **trust boundary** — the line where data from the outside world enters your program), and the one discipline that fixes it for good: validate untrusted data *once*, at the edge, and never trust a raw `any` again.

---

### 💥 Types Are Not Validation — The Lesson That Bites in Prod

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Type erasure** means that when TypeScript compiles to JavaScript, *all the types are deleted.* The `: number`, the `interface`, the `<T>` — every type annotation vanishes. The JavaScript that actually runs has **zero** knowledge of your types. So "type checking" only ever happens on your screen, before the program runs — never while it runs.

> **Note (simplification):** A few constructs (`enum`, classes, and decorators with metadata) do emit some runtime JavaScript. But pure type annotations, `interface`, and `type` aliases are 100% erased.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Because beginners read `const user: User = await res.json()` and believe TypeScript *guaranteed* `user` matches `User`. It did not. `res.json()` returns the type `Promise<any>` by default (you can opt into stricter wrappers that return `unknown`) — TypeScript just **takes your word for it** when you annotate the result. If the real data is the wrong shape, nothing stops it. The mismatch slips silently past the compiler and explodes later — often far from the cause — when you call `user.name.toUpperCase()` and `name` is actually `undefined`.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
TypeScript checks types, then *throws them away* and emits plain JavaScript:

```
  YOUR .ts FILE                  EMITTED .js FILE
  --------------                 ----------------
  const age: number = x   ──▶    const age = x
  interface User {...}     ──▶   (nothing — gone)
  function f<T>(v: T) {}   ──▶   function f(v) {}

  compile-time only  │  runtime: no types exist
  ───────────────────┼──────────────────────────
   checks happen here │  nothing checks here
```

So a value's *declared* type and its *actual* runtime value can disagree, and the running program has no way to notice.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A type annotation is like a **label on a shipping box that says "Books."** The label is a *claim*. If a warehouse worker writes "Books" on a box that actually holds bricks, nobody finds out until someone lifts it and hurts their back. TypeScript writes labels; it never opens the box to check. Runtime validation is the worker who *actually opens the box* before you trust the label.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
interface User {
  id: number;
  name: string;
}

// Pretend the API is buggy and really returns:
//   { "id": "7", "name": null }   <- id is a STRING, name is NULL
async function getUser(): Promise<User> {
  const res = await fetch("/api/user");
  // res.json() is typed `any` (or `Promise<any>`).
  // Annotating `: User` is a PROMISE WE MAKE, not a check TS runs.
  const user: User = await res.json(); // compiles GREEN. No error.
  return user;
}

const u = await getUser();
console.log(u.id * 2);            // "7" * 2 -> 14 ... wait, JS coerces. Lucky.
console.log(u.name.toUpperCase()); // 💥 TypeError: Cannot read properties of null
// The bug is here, but the CAUSE was the unchecked boundary above.
```
TypeScript reported no error at compile time. The crash is purely a runtime event.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Your API typed a field as `number` but the server actually sent a string. What happens, and how do you prevent it?**
A: Nothing happens *at compile time* — TypeScript erases types, so the annotation `: number` is just an unverified claim. At runtime the value is really a string, and you get a bug (wrong math, or a `TypeError` when you call number methods) somewhere downstream. You prevent it by **validating the data at the boundary** with a runtime schema (e.g. Zod) instead of trusting the annotation, so a wrong shape is caught immediately at the edge with a clear error.

---

### 🚧 The Trust Boundary — Where the Outside World Gets In

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **trust boundary** is the imaginary line between code you control (where types are trustworthy because *you* produced the values) and the outside world (where data could be *anything*). Any data crossing *into* your program from outside is **untrusted** until you check it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Inside your own program, types are reliable: if you wrote `const n = 5`, it's a number, full stop. But the moment data comes from somewhere you don't control, your type annotations become *wishful thinking*. The classic untrusted sources every team forgets:

- **`fetch` / network responses** — the server might change, lie, or error.
- **`JSON.parse(...)`** — returns `any`; the string could be any shape.
- **`localStorage` / cookies** — a user (or an old app version) may have stored garbage.
- **Environment variables** (`process.env.X`) — typed `string | undefined`, often missing.
- **User input** — form fields, URL query params, file uploads.
- **Third-party libraries / webhooks** — payloads you didn't author.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Picture your app as a fortress. The boundary is the gate. Trusted data lives inside; untrusted data must be *checked at the gate*:

```
   OUTSIDE (untrusted)        │  INSIDE (trusted)
                              │
  fetch() ─┐                  │
  JSON.parse ─┐               │
  localStorage ─┐  ┌───────┐  │  ┌──────────────┐
  env vars ─────┼─▶│ GATE  │──┼─▶│ typed values │
  user input ─┘  │ (parse/ │  │  │ you can trust │
  webhooks ─┘    │ validate)│  │  └──────────────┘
                 └───────┘  │
        types are LIES here │ types are SAFE here
```

Everything that enters should pass through the gate once. After that, the typed value is genuinely trustworthy.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's airport security. Inside the secure zone, everyone has already been screened, so you can relax. The **security checkpoint is the trust boundary**: anyone arriving from outside must be checked *before* entering. You don't re-screen people walking between gates inside — you screened them once at the edge. Your program should do the same with data.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Every line below produces `any` or an over-trusting type:

const data = JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem("cart") ?? "{}");
// data: any  -> could be literally anything a user stored

const res = await fetch("/api/order");
const order = await res.json(); // any -> server shape is unverified

const port = process.env.PORT;  // string | undefined -> maybe missing!
const portNum = Number(port);   // NaN if PORT is unset or "abc"

// None of these are "wrong" to TypeScript. All are danger zones.
// Each is a spot where you MUST validate before trusting.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Name some places untrusted data enters a TypeScript app, and why their types can't be trusted.**
A: `fetch`/network responses, `JSON.parse`, `localStorage`/cookies, environment variables, and user input (forms, query params). Their types can't be trusted because TypeScript erases types at runtime and these values originate *outside* your program — TypeScript only knows the type you *claimed*, not the value that actually arrived. Each is a trust boundary that needs runtime validation.

---

### 🛂 Parse, Don't Validate — Turn Unknown Into Typed, Once

#### 🧩 What is it?
**"Parse, don't validate"** is a discipline: at the boundary, take the unknown input and *transform* it into a **specific typed value** in one step — instead of just *asking* "is this OK?" and then continuing to pass the raw, still-untyped data around. Parsing **produces a typed result**; validating only **returns a yes/no** and throws away what it learned.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A plain "validate" function returns a boolean. After you call it, the original variable is *still* `any` or `unknown` — TypeScript learned nothing, so you keep re-checking the same data deeper and deeper in your code ("defensive checks everywhere"). Worse, the check and the type can drift apart. **Parsing** fixes both: you check once, get back a value whose **type reflects the check**, and from then on TypeScript *knows* it's safe — no repeated guards.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A parser is a function shaped like `unknown -> TypedValue` that **throws or returns an error** if the input doesn't fit. Untrusted data goes in; either a typed value comes out, or you handle the failure right there at the edge:

```
  unknown input
       │
       ▼
  ┌─────────┐   fits?  ──no──▶  throw / handle error
  │  PARSE  │
  └─────────┘   ──yes──▶  typed value (trusted from here on)
       │
       ▼
  rest of app uses the TYPED value
  (no more "is this valid?" checks)
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A passport-control officer. **Validating** is glancing at your passport and saying "looks fine" — then handing you back the same unverified document to show again at every desk inside. **Parsing** is stamping your passport and issuing a boarding pass: a *new, trusted token* that proves you're cleared. Everyone downstream trusts the boarding pass and never re-checks the passport.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// ❌ VALIDATE: returns a boolean; data stays `unknown` afterward
function isUser(x: unknown): boolean {
  return typeof x === "object" && x !== null && "name" in x;
}
const raw: unknown = JSON.parse(input);
if (isUser(raw)) {
  // raw is STILL `unknown` here (boolean guards without `is` don't narrow).
  // So you're forced to keep casting / re-checking. Brittle.
}

// ✅ PARSE: returns a typed value (or throws). One check, then trusted.
type User = { name: string };
function parseUser(x: unknown): User {
  if (
    typeof x === "object" && x !== null &&
    "name" in x && typeof (x as { name: unknown }).name === "string"
  ) {
    return x as User; // now genuinely a User
  }
  throw new Error("Invalid user payload");
}
const user = parseUser(JSON.parse(input));
// user: User — TS and runtime AGREE. No more guards downstream.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does "parse, don't validate" mean?**
A: Instead of writing a function that just answers "is this valid?" (a boolean) and leaves your data as `unknown`, you write one that *transforms* the unknown input into a concrete, typed value at the boundary — failing loudly if it doesn't fit. You validate once, capture the result in the type system, and every line afterward gets a trustworthy typed value, eliminating repeated defensive checks deeper in the code.

---

### 🛡️ Schema-First with Zod — One Source of Truth

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **schema library** lets you describe the *runtime* shape of your data as a value — a **schema** — that can actually *check* data while the program runs. **Zod** is the most popular. You write the schema once, then derive the *static* TypeScript type *from* the schema with **`z.infer`**, so the runtime check and the compile-time type can never drift apart. (Alternatives in the same family: **Valibot**, **io-ts**, **ArkType**.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Hand-writing parsers like the previous example is tedious and easy to get subtly wrong, and you'd typically write the `interface` *and* the matching runtime check **separately** — two things that must be kept in sync by hand. The day someone adds a field to the type but forgets the check (or vice versa), you have a silent gap. Zod collapses both into **one definition**: the schema *is* the validator *and* the source of the type.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
        ┌──────────────────────┐
        │   Zod schema (value) │  <- you write this ONCE
        └──────────┬───────────┘
                   │
        ┌──────────┴───────────┐
        ▼                      ▼
  runtime: .parse(x)     compile time: z.infer<typeof schema>
  checks real data       derives the static TS type
        │                      │
        ▼                      ▼
   throws on bad shape    autocomplete & type errors
```

Two key methods:
- **`schema.parse(x)`** — returns the typed value if valid, and **throws a `ZodError`** if not.
- **`schema.safeParse(x)`** — never throws; returns a **result object**: `{ success: true, data }` or `{ success: false, error }`. (You inspect `result.success` to branch.)

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A schema is a **cookie cutter**. The same single cutter both *checks* whether a piece of dough has the right shape (runtime `parse`) and *defines* what a "correct cookie" looks like (the static type). One tool, one source of truth — you can't have the cutter and the definition disagree, because they're the same object.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
import { z } from "zod";

// 1) Define the schema ONCE (this is a runtime value)
const UserSchema = z.object({
  id: z.number(),
  name: z.string(),
  email: z.string().email(),
});

// 2) Derive the STATIC type from it — single source of truth
type User = z.infer<typeof UserSchema>;
// User is exactly: { id: number; name: string; email: string }

// 3a) parse(): returns typed value, THROWS on bad data
async function getUser(): Promise<User> {
  const res = await fetch("/api/user");
  const json = await res.json();          // any
  return UserSchema.parse(json);          // -> User, or throws ZodError
  // If the server sent id:"7", this THROWS here, at the edge — not deep
  // in your app. The error names the bad field. Bug caught immediately.
}

// 3b) safeParse(): never throws, returns a result object
const result = UserSchema.safeParse(JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem("u") ?? "{}"));
if (result.success) {
  console.log(result.data.name); // result.data is a typed User
} else {
  console.warn(result.error.issues); // structured list of what was wrong
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you keep a runtime schema and a static type in sync?**
A: Make the schema the single source of truth and *derive* the type from it, rather than declaring them separately. With Zod you write the schema once and use `type T = z.infer<typeof schema>`. Because the static type is generated from the same object that does the runtime checking, they cannot drift apart — change the schema and the type updates automatically. `parse` throws on invalid input; `safeParse` returns a `{ success, data | error }` result so you can handle failure without exceptions.

---

### 🔍 Type Predicates vs Schema Validators — Where Each Belongs

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **type predicate** (also called a user-defined type guard) is a function whose return type is written `arg is SomeType`. When it returns `true`, TypeScript **narrows** the argument to that type. A **schema validator** (like a Zod schema) is a runtime object that checks data against a declared shape and gives you back a typed value or a detailed error. Both make untrusted data safe — but they suit different jobs.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Type predicates are built into the language and great for *small, simple* checks (`isString`, "is this one of three known variants?"). But they have a trap: TypeScript **trusts your predicate blindly** — if your `x is User` function checks only `name` but forgets `email`, the compiler still believes the lie. Schema validators do the checking *for* you, exhaustively, and produce rich errors — better for *complex, external* data. Knowing which to reach for keeps you from hand-rolling fragile guards for big payloads.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  TYPE PREDICATE                  SCHEMA VALIDATOR (Zod)
  --------------                  ----------------------
  fn(x): x is T                   schema.parse(x): T
  YOU write the check             library checks for you
  TS trusts it (can lie!)         checks every field, exhaustive
  no error detail (just bool)     rich, structured error messages
  best: small internal narrowing  best: big external boundary data
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A type predicate is a **bouncer who eyeballs your ID** — fast and fine for a small venue, but if the bouncer is sloppy, fakes get through, and nobody else double-checks. A schema validator is a **full airport scanner** — thorough, consistent, and it tells you *exactly* what's wrong ("liquid in bag 2"). You use the bouncer for the quiet door inside; you use the scanner at the airport's outer edge.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// TYPE PREDICATE — note the `x is Cat` return type.
type Cat = { kind: "cat"; meow: () => void };
type Dog = { kind: "dog"; bark: () => void };

function isCat(a: Cat | Dog): a is Cat {
  return a.kind === "cat";
}
function handle(animal: Cat | Dog) {
  if (isCat(animal)) {
    animal.meow(); // narrowed to Cat — clean for a small INTERNAL union
  } else {
    animal.bark();
  }
}

// THE TRAP: TS believes a buggy predicate.
type Account = { id: string; balance: number };
function isAccount(x: unknown): x is Account {
  return typeof x === "object" && x !== null && "id" in x;
  // BUG: we never checked `balance`! But TS now TRUSTS this completely.
}
const maybe: unknown = { id: "a1" }; // no balance field at all
if (isAccount(maybe)) {
  console.log(maybe.balance.toFixed(2)); // 💥 TypeError at runtime
  // TS gave zero warning — the predicate lied, and TS took it on faith.
}
// For external data like this, a Zod schema (which checks EVERY field)
// is the safer tool. Reserve hand-written predicates for small,
// fully-controlled internal narrowing.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When would you use a type predicate versus a schema validator like Zod, and what's the risk of a predicate?**
A: Use a type predicate (`arg is T`) for small, internal narrowing where you control the data — e.g. distinguishing members of a known union. Use a schema validator at the trust boundary for complex, external data, because it checks every field for you, derives the type via `z.infer`, and yields detailed errors. The risk of a predicate is that TypeScript trusts it unconditionally: if your check is incomplete or wrong, the compiler still narrows to the type, so a runtime mismatch slips through with no warning.

---

### 🎯 Where to Validate, Where to Trust — The Cost/Benefit Call

#### 🧩 What is it?
This is the architect's judgment call: validation costs real CPU time and code, so you don't validate *everywhere* — you validate at the **edges** (where untrusted data enters) and **trust freely inside** (where you already produced the values). The skill is drawing that line correctly.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Two failure modes. **Under-validating**: trusting a `fetch` response leads to the silent prod crashes from earlier. **Over-validating**: re-parsing the same already-trusted object on every internal function call wastes performance and clutters code with pointless checks. A good rule prevents both: **validate once at the boundary, then trust the typed value everywhere downstream** — exactly the "parse, don't validate" payoff.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  VALIDATE (edges)              TRUST (interior)
  ----------------              ----------------
  fetch / API responses         values you just created
  JSON.parse output             results of your own pure functions
  localStorage / cookies        data already parsed at the boundary
  env vars (at startup)         internal function-to-function calls
  form / user input             config validated once on boot

  rule: parse ONCE at the edge ─▶ trust the typed value after
```

Validate env vars **once at startup** (fail fast if config is wrong, before serving traffic) rather than on every request.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A restaurant inspects ingredients **at the delivery door** (the boundary) — checking freshness when the truck arrives. The chefs don't re-inspect the same tomato every time they pass it across the kitchen; it was checked at the door, so it's trusted inside. Inspecting at the door = safe *and* efficient. Inspecting every tomato at every station = paranoid and slow. Skipping the door entirely = food poisoning.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
import { z } from "zod";

// VALIDATE at startup: fail fast if the environment is misconfigured.
const EnvSchema = z.object({
  PORT: z.coerce.number(),        // coerce: turn the string env var into number
  DATABASE_URL: z.string().url(),
});
const env = EnvSchema.parse(process.env); // throws on boot if config is bad
// env.PORT is now a trusted `number`. Crash NOW, not mid-request.

// VALIDATE at the API edge:
const OrderSchema = z.object({ id: z.string(), total: z.number() });
type Order = z.infer<typeof OrderSchema>;

async function loadOrder(): Promise<Order> {
  const res = await fetch("/api/order");
  return OrderSchema.parse(await res.json()); // checked ONCE, here
}

// TRUST inside: this internal function does NOT re-validate.
function applyDiscount(order: Order, pct: number): number {
  return order.total * (1 - pct); // order is already trusted — no re-check
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: You can't validate everything — how do you decide where to validate and where to trust?**
A: Validate at trust boundaries where untrusted data enters: API/`fetch` responses, `JSON.parse`, `localStorage`, user input, and env vars (validated once at startup so the app fails fast). Once data is parsed into a typed value at the edge, trust it for the rest of its journey — don't re-validate it on every internal call, since those values either came from a boundary you already checked or were produced by your own code. The principle is "parse once at the edge, trust the typed value inside," which is both safe and performant.

---

<h2 id="module-12">Module 12 — Declaration Merging, Enums & Decorators</h2>

TypeScript has a few "old-world" features it inherited from before JavaScript had modules and classes of its own — `enum`, `namespace`, decorators, and the quiet magic of **declaration merging**. They're powerful, but each one carries a trap that has burned real teams. This module shows you what each feature *actually does at runtime*, why some are quietly banned in big codebases, and the modern alternatives that replaced them.

---

### 🧬 Declaration Merging — Two Definitions, One Thing

#### 🧩 What is it?
**Declaration merging** is when you write the *same name twice* and TypeScript silently **combines** the two definitions into one. The most common case: two `interface`s with the same name become a single interface with all the members of both.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Sometimes you need to **add to a type you don't own** — a type from a library, or a global like `window`. You can't edit the library's source. Merging lets you "reopen" an existing interface and bolt on extra members from your own file, without touching the original.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
For interfaces, TypeScript collects every declaration with the same name and unions their members together:

```
  interface Box { a: string }      interface Box { b: number }
          │                                 │
          └──────────────┬──────────────────┘
                         ▼   TypeScript merges them
            interface Box { a: string; b: number }
```

`type` aliases do **not** merge — re-declaring a `type` is a duplicate-identifier error. Only `interface`, `namespace`, `enum`, and a few combinations merge.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like a **shared shopping list on the fridge**. You write "milk," your roommate later writes "eggs" on the *same* list. Nobody made a second list — the fridge now holds one combined list with both items. Each person added to the same name.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Two interfaces with the SAME name → merged into one.
interface User {
  name: string;
}
interface User {
  age: number;
}

// TypeScript now sees ONE User with BOTH fields:
const u: User = { name: "Ana", age: 30 }; // OK
// const bad: User = { name: "Ana" };
//   Error: Property 'age' is missing in type '{ name: string; }'

// A "type" alias canNOT be re-declared like this:
// type Pet = { legs: number };
// type Pet = { tail: boolean };
//   Error: Duplicate identifier 'Pet'.
```

A namespace can also merge **into a function or class** to attach static, type-safe extras:

```typescript
function greet(name: string) {
  return `Hi ${name}`;
}
// Same name → namespace merges onto the function itself.
namespace greet {
  export const version = "1.0";
}

console.log(greet("Bo"));    // "Hi Bo"
console.log(greet.version);  // "1.0"  ← attached via merging
```

> **Note (simplification):** the exact merge rules differ per declaration kind (value vs type vs namespace). The mental model "same name → combined" holds for the common interface case; full rules live in the TS handbook's "Declaration Merging" page.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you add a property to an existing third-party interface or to a global type?**
A: Declaration merging. Re-declare the same `interface` name (often inside `declare global` or `declare module "lib"`) and your members merge in. This is exactly how libraries let you augment things like `Express.Request` or the global `Window`. Note `type` aliases do not support this — only `interface`/`namespace`.

---

### 🔢 Enums — Numeric vs String, and Reverse Mappings

#### 🧩 What is it?
An **`enum`** ("enumeration") is a named set of related constants — like a fixed list of allowed values: `Up`, `Down`, `Left`, `Right`. Unusually for TypeScript, an `enum` is **not just a type** — it compiles to a *real object that exists at runtime*.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Without enums you scatter "magic values" — `if (status === 2)` — and nobody remembers what `2` means. Enums give those values **readable names** and group them under one identifier (`Status.Active`), so the code documents itself.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
By default enum members are **numeric**, auto-numbered from `0`. Numeric enums also get a **reverse mapping**: the runtime object maps name → number *and* number → name. **String enums** (where you assign string values) do **not** get a reverse mapping.

```
   enum Dir { Up, Down }   compiles to an object like:

   Dir = {
     Up: 0,  Down: 1,        ← forward: name → number
     0: "Up", 1: "Down"      ← reverse: number → name (numeric only)
   }
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A numeric enum is like a **coat-check counter**. You hand in a coat and get ticket `0`; the desk can go *coat → ticket* and *ticket → coat* (both directions — that's the reverse mapping). A string enum is like a **name badge**: it reads "Up," but you can't look up a person *by* the word "Up" — only one direction works.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// NUMERIC enum — auto-numbered from 0, and reverse-mapped.
enum Direction {
  Up,    // 0
  Down,  // 1
  Left,  // 2
  Right, // 3
}

console.log(Direction.Up);        // 0          (name → number)
console.log(Direction[0]);        // "Up"       (number → name: reverse mapping)
console.log(Direction["Down"]);   // 1

// STRING enum — explicit values, NO reverse mapping.
enum Status {
  Active = "ACTIVE",
  Closed = "CLOSED",
}

console.log(Status.Active);       // "ACTIVE"
console.log(Status["ACTIVE"]);    // undefined  ← no reverse map for string enums
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's a reverse mapping, and which enums have one?**
A: For numeric enums, TypeScript emits both directions in the runtime object: `Enum.Name` gives the number, and `Enum[number]` gives the name string. String enums only emit the forward direction (`Enum.Name` → string), so indexing by the value returns `undefined`. The reverse mapping is also why numeric enum objects are roughly twice as large at runtime.

---

### 🚫 `const enum` — The Inlining Trap Many Teams Ban

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **`const enum`** is an enum marked `const` that tells the compiler: *don't emit a runtime object at all — instead, paste the literal value directly wherever it's used.* This pasting-in-place is called **inlining**.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A regular enum costs a real object in your bundle. A `const enum` was meant to be **zero-cost**: at compile time `Dir.Up` is replaced by `0`, so there's nothing left at runtime. Sounds great — but the inlining is exactly what makes it dangerous.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The checker erases the enum and **substitutes the literal** at each use site. There's no `Dir` object to look up later:

```
   const enum Dir { Up, Down }
   let x = Dir.Up;

        │  compiler inlines the value
        ▼
   let x = 0; /* Dir.Up */     ← no Dir object exists at runtime
```

**Why teams ban it:**
- **`isolatedModules` breaks it.** Single-file transpilers (Babel, esbuild, swc) compile each file *alone* and can't see the enum's values across files — so they either error or emit wrong output. `isolatedModules` flags this.
- **Cross-package landmine.** If a library ships a `const enum` in its `.d.ts`, a consumer using a single-file transpiler has no values to inline → broken builds.
- **`preserveConstEnums`** keeps the object *and* inlines — undoing the only benefit — so people enable it defensively, which means you pay the cost anyway.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A normal enum is a **phone book** everyone shares — one copy, look up names anytime. A `const enum` is like **memorizing one number and writing it directly into every letter you send**, then throwing the phone book away. Fast — until someone needs a number you never wrote down, with no book left to check.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Regular enum: emits a runtime object you can inspect.
enum Size { S, M, L }
console.log(Size.M);   // 1
console.log(Size[1]);  // "M"   ← object still exists

// const enum: NO object emitted; uses are inlined.
const enum Color { Red, Green }
let c = Color.Green;
// Compiles to roughly:
//   let c = 1 /* Color.Green */;
// console.log(Color[1]);  ← ERROR at compile time:
//   "A const enum member can only be accessed using a string literal."
//   And there is NO Color object at runtime to index.

// Under `isolatedModules`, even importing a const enum from
// another file is an error, because the transpiler can't inline it.
```

> **Note (simplification):** plain `tsc` *can* compile a `const enum` correctly within one project because it sees all files together. The breakage specifically appears with single-file transpilers / `isolatedModules`, and when shipping `const enum`s across package boundaries.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why do many style guides avoid `enum`, especially `const enum`?**
A: Regular enums emit runtime objects (extra bundle weight, plus numeric ones double up for the reverse map) and have quirky type behavior. `const enum` is worse for tooling: it works by inlining literals at compile time, which breaks under `isolatedModules` and single-file transpilers (Babel/esbuild/swc), and is a footgun when exported from a library. Teams using bundlers often forbid `const enum` outright and prefer a union of literals.

---

### 🎯 Union of Literals + `as const` — The Enum Alternative

#### 🧩 What is it?
Instead of an `enum`, model a fixed set of options as a **union of string literals** (`"active" | "closed"`), optionally backed by an **`as const` object** for a runtime lookup. `as const` tells TypeScript to keep the *exact literal values* (and make them `readonly`) instead of widening them to `string`.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
This sidesteps every enum problem at once: **no runtime object** is forced on you, it's fully compatible with `isolatedModules` and every transpiler, and the values are **plain strings** — easy to log, serialize to JSON, and read in a debugger. No reverse-mapping surprises, no inlining.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  const Status = { Active: "active",
                   Closed: "closed" } as const;

      │  derive the type FROM the object
      ▼
  type Status = typeof Status[keyof typeof Status];
      = "active" | "closed"        ← one source of truth
```

The object is an ordinary value (use `Status.Active`), and the type is *derived* from it — so they can never drift apart.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
An enum is a **custom machine you must install and power** in your kitchen. The union-of-literals approach is using a **standard label maker** you already own: the labels ("active," "closed") are just plain words anyone can read, no special device required to interpret them.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Pattern 1: a bare union of literals (zero runtime cost).
type Direction = "up" | "down" | "left" | "right";

function move(d: Direction) { /* ... */ }
move("up");      // OK
// move("UP");   // Error: not assignable to type 'Direction'

// Pattern 2: as const object + derived union (when you need
// a runtime lookup table too).
const Role = {
  Admin: "admin",
  User: "user",
} as const;

// keyof typeof Role  -> "Admin" | "User"
// typeof Role[...]    -> "admin" | "user"
type Role = (typeof Role)[keyof typeof Role]; // "admin" | "user"

const r: Role = Role.Admin;  // "admin"  ← a plain string
console.log(JSON.stringify({ r })); // {"r":"admin"}  ← serializes cleanly
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How would you replace an enum without losing type safety?**
A: Use a union of string literals (`type Status = "active" | "closed"`) for the type. If you also need a runtime object, define it with `as const` and derive the union via `typeof Obj[keyof typeof Obj]` so type and value share one source of truth. You get autocompletion and exhaustiveness checking, plain-string runtime values, and full transpiler compatibility — no enum object, no `const enum` inlining hazard.

---

### 🎀 Decorators — Stage 3 vs Legacy `experimentalDecorators`

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **decorator** is a function you attach to a class (or its members) with `@name` syntax to **wrap or modify** it — add logging, register it, validate it — without rewriting the class body. There are **two different flavors**: the modern **TC39 Stage 3** decorators (standard JavaScript, supported in TS 5.0+) and the older **legacy / "experimental"** decorators (enabled by the `experimentalDecorators` flag).

> Jargon: **TC39** is the committee that standardizes JavaScript; **Stage 3** means the spec is complete and being implemented — close to final. (See Module 0's stage ladder.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Lots of frameworks want to attach behavior to classes declaratively — "this class is a `@Controller`," "this field is a `@Column`." Decorators give that clean, readable syntax. The catch: the **two flavors are not compatible**, and mixing them up causes confusing errors.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A decorator **desugars** (gets rewritten by the compiler) into a function call that receives the thing it decorates and can replace or augment it:

```
   @log
   class Service {}

        │  desugars to roughly
        ▼
   class Service {}
   Service = log(Service, context) ?? Service;
```

The two flavors differ in **what arguments the function receives**:
- **Stage 3:** `(value, context)` — `context` is an object with `kind`, `name`, `addInitializer`, etc. Standard; **no** `experimentalDecorators` flag needed in TS 5+.
- **Legacy:** `(target, propertyKey, descriptor)` — older shape; requires `"experimentalDecorators": true`. This is what NestJS, TypeORM, and Angular still rely on today.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A decorator is **gift-wrapping**. The gift (your class) is unchanged inside, but the wrapping adds something on the outside — a bow, a label. Stage 3 and legacy are **two incompatible wrapping machines**: same idea, but they hand the wrapper a different set of tools, so paper cut for one jams the other.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// --- Stage 3 (standard, TS 5+, NO experimentalDecorators) ---
function logged<T, A extends unknown[], R>(
  target: (this: T, ...args: A) => R,
  context: ClassMethodDecoratorContext, // the new "context" arg
) {
  return function (this: T, ...args: A): R {
    console.log(`calling ${String(context.name)}`);
    return target.call(this, ...args);
  };
}

class Api {
  @logged
  fetch() { return "data"; }
}
new Api().fetch();
// Logs: "calling fetch"
// Returns: "data"

// --- Legacy (requires "experimentalDecorators": true) ---
// Different signature: (target, propertyKey, descriptor)
function Legacy(target: object, key: string, desc: PropertyDescriptor) {
  // ...wrap desc.value...
}
// Using the wrong flavor for your tsconfig = compile error.
```

> **Note (simplification):** the Stage 3 decorator *function signatures* vary by what you decorate (class, method, field, accessor, getter/setter). The example shows a method decorator; class and field decorators receive different `context.kind` values and return different things.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What's the difference between Stage 3 and legacy decorators?**
A: Legacy ("experimental") decorators are an older design enabled by `experimentalDecorators`; their functions receive `(target, propertyKey, descriptor)`. Stage 3 decorators are the standardized TC39 proposal that TypeScript 5.0+ supports natively *without* the flag; their functions receive `(value, context)` where `context` carries metadata and helpers like `addInitializer`. They are not interchangeable — frameworks like Nest/Angular/TypeORM are still built on the legacy form, so those projects keep `experimentalDecorators` on.

---

### 🔍 `reflect-metadata` & `emitDecoratorMetadata` — How DI Works

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`reflect-metadata`** is a small library that lets you **store and read extra data ("metadata") attached to classes** at runtime. **`emitDecoratorMetadata`** is a `tsconfig` flag that makes the compiler **automatically emit type information** (like a parameter's type) into that metadata store — *only* for legacy decorators.

> Jargon: **DI (Dependency Injection)** = a framework automatically *creating and supplying* the objects a class needs, instead of you writing `new Thing()` by hand.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Remember from earlier modules: **types are erased** — they vanish at runtime. So how does NestJS see `constructor(private db: Database)` and know to inject a `Database`? Normally it couldn't — the type `Database` is gone. `emitDecoratorMetadata` is the escape hatch that **preserves that one type** as runtime data the framework can read back.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
When a class has a decorator *and* both flags are on, the compiler emits a metadata entry recording the constructor's parameter **types**. The framework reads `design:paramtypes` via `reflect-metadata`, sees `[Database]`, constructs one, and passes it in:

```
  @Injectable()
  class UserService {
    constructor(private db: Database) {}
  }
        │  emitDecoratorMetadata writes runtime metadata:
        ▼
  Reflect.getMetadata("design:paramtypes", UserService)
        = [Database]      ← the framework reads this
        ▼
  DI container: new UserService(new Database())
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Types are normally **invisible ink** that fades at runtime. `emitDecoratorMetadata` is like **photocopying the recipe's ingredient list onto a sticky note** before the ink fades, and `reflect-metadata` is the **drawer where the sticky note is filed**. Later the kitchen (the DI container) reads the note — "needs one Database" — and fetches that ingredient automatically.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// tsconfig.json needs BOTH:
//   "experimentalDecorators": true,
//   "emitDecoratorMetadata": true
// and at app start:  import "reflect-metadata";
import "reflect-metadata";

class Database {}

function Injectable(): ClassDecorator {
  return () => { /* mark as injectable */ };
}

@Injectable()
class UserService {
  constructor(private db: Database) {}
}

// The compiler emitted parameter-type metadata. A DI container reads it:
const deps = Reflect.getMetadata("design:paramtypes", UserService);
console.log(deps);          // [ [Function: Database] ]
console.log(deps[0] === Database); // true
// → the container now knows to build a Database and inject it.
```

> **Note (simplification):** `design:paramtypes` only records *class* types reliably. Interfaces, unions, and generics erase to `Object` (they have no runtime value), which is why DI in these frameworks injects by class/token, not by interface. Note also `emitDecoratorMetadata` is a **legacy-decorator** feature; the Stage 3 standard uses a separate metadata mechanism.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How does `reflect-metadata` enable dependency injection?**
A: Types are erased at runtime, so a DI container normally can't see what a constructor needs. With legacy decorators plus `emitDecoratorMetadata`, the compiler emits the constructor's parameter *types* as metadata (`design:paramtypes`) into the `reflect-metadata` store. The framework reads that list at runtime, instantiates each dependency, and passes them in. It only works for class types — interfaces erase to `Object` — which is why these frameworks inject by class or by explicit token.

---

### 🗂️ Namespaces — The Legacy Module System Modules Replaced

#### 🧩 What is it?
A **`namespace`** (originally "internal module") is TypeScript's *own* way to group related code under one name and avoid polluting the global scope — invented **before** JavaScript had a standard module system.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Back when scripts just dumped everything into the global scope, two libraries could clobber each other's variables. Namespaces wrapped code in one named object (`MyLib.helper()`) to prevent collisions. But this was a **TypeScript-only invention** — the language solved the same problem properly later.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
A namespace compiles to a plain object with an **IIFE** (Immediately Invoked Function Expression — a function that runs the moment it's defined) attaching the `export`ed members:

```
  namespace Geo { export const pi = 3.14; }

        │  compiles to roughly
        ▼
  var Geo;
  (function (Geo) { Geo.pi = 3.14; })(Geo || (Geo = {}));
```

**Why modules replaced them:** ES2015 added **ES Modules** (`import`/`export`) — the *standard* JavaScript way to split code into files. Modules give real per-file scoping, work with bundlers and tree-shaking (dead-code removal), and are understood by every tool. Namespaces don't tree-shake well and are a TS-only concept, so the official guidance is: **use modules** (files with `import`/`export`); reach for `namespace` mainly when authoring ambient `.d.ts` declarations.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A namespace is like **keeping all your files in one big labeled cardboard box** so nothing gets lost on the floor (the global scope). ES Modules are like a proper **filing cabinet with separate drawers** — each file is its own drawer with its own lock, and the building's standard system (every tool) knows how to use it. The box worked, but the cabinet is the standard now.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Legacy namespace — TS-only grouping.
namespace MathUtils {
  export const pi = 3.14159;
  export function area(r: number) { return pi * r * r; }
}
console.log(MathUtils.area(2)); // 12.56636

// Modern, preferred equivalent — ES modules (separate files):
//
//   // math-utils.ts
//   export const pi = 3.14159;
//   export function area(r: number) { return pi * r * r; }
//
//   // main.ts
//   import { area } from "./math-utils";
//   console.log(area(2)); // 12.56636
//
// Modules give real file scope + tree-shaking; prefer them.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why did ES modules replace TypeScript namespaces?**
A: Namespaces were a pre-ES2015, TypeScript-only solution for grouping code and avoiding global collisions. ES Modules (`import`/`export`) are the *standard* JavaScript mechanism: real per-file scoping understood by every bundler and runtime, with proper tree-shaking. Namespaces don't tree-shake cleanly and tie you to TS-specific output, so modern guidance is to use modules for application code and keep namespaces only for ambient declaration files (`.d.ts`) and declaration merging.

---

<h2 id="module-13">Module 13 — The Compiler & Type-Check Performance</h2>

Most people think of TypeScript as "JavaScript with type annotations". But `tsc` (the TypeScript compiler) is really a small, sophisticated program that *reads your code as a tree*, *reasons about every value's type*, and only then turns it into plain JavaScript. Understanding how that machine works — and why it sometimes grinds to a halt on a big repo — is exactly what separates an engineer who *uses* TypeScript from one who can *defend its build setup* in an architect interview.

---

### 🏭 The `tsc` Pipeline — How Source Becomes JavaScript

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **`tsc` pipeline** is the fixed sequence of stages the TypeScript compiler runs every time it processes your code. Each stage takes the output of the previous one and refines it. The stages are: **scanner → parser → binder → checker → emitter**.

A quick gloss of the jargon before we dive in:
- **token** — the smallest meaningful chunk of code, like the keyword `const`, the name `user`, or the symbol `=`.
- **AST** — short for *Abstract Syntax Tree*, a tree-shaped data structure that represents your code's grammar (a function "contains" parameters, which "contain" names, and so on).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
You can't check or transform code by treating it as a flat string of characters — `"const x = 1"` is just text. The compiler needs to *understand structure* ("this is a variable declaration whose value is the number `1`") and *understand meaning* ("`x` has type `number`"). Splitting the work into stages keeps each step simple and lets tools reuse the early stages (more on that in the language-service topic).

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Each stage feeds the next:

```
 your .ts file (raw text)
        │
        ▼
 ┌──────────┐  text → tokens
 │ SCANNER  │  "const","x","=","1"
 └────┬─────┘
      ▼
 ┌──────────┐  tokens → AST (a tree)
 │  PARSER  │  VariableDecl(x = 1)
 └────┬─────┘
      ▼
 ┌──────────┐  links names to their
 │  BINDER  │  declarations (symbols)
 └────┬─────┘
      ▼
 ┌──────────┐  THE BRAIN: infers &
 │ CHECKER  │  verifies every type
 └────┬─────┘
      ▼
 ┌──────────┐  AST → plain .js
 │ EMITTER  │  (strips the types)
 └────┬─────┘
      ▼
   output .js  (+ optional .d.ts)
```

Walking the stages in plain words:
- **Scanner (a.k.a. lexer)** — chops the raw text into tokens.
- **Parser** — assembles those tokens into the AST, catching *grammar* mistakes (a missing `)` ).
- **Binder** — walks the tree and builds **symbols**: it connects each name to the thing it declares, so the compiler knows that the `x` you *use* on line 9 is the `x` you *declared* on line 2. It also maps out scopes.
- **Checker** — the heart of TypeScript. It infers types, verifies assignments, and produces the red squiggly errors. This is where almost all the *time* goes.
- **Emitter** — produces the actual JavaScript output by **stripping** all the type annotations and downleveling syntax (e.g. turning modern syntax into older syntax if your `target` is old). Optionally it also emits `.d.ts` declaration files (type-only files — see Module 8).

> **Note (simplification):** these stages aren't a rigid one-way assembly line. The checker is *lazy* — it computes a type only when something asks for it — and the emitter can run even when the checker found errors (TypeScript emits by default unless you set `noEmitOnError`). Think of the diagram as the *logical* flow, not a strict batch order.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of translating a novel from French to English. The **scanner** splits the page into individual words. The **parser** groups those words into grammatical sentences. The **binder** is the footnote editor who notices "the *captain* mentioned in chapter 9 is the same captain from chapter 2". The **checker** is the strict proofreader who flags every logical contradiction ("you said she was 30 here and 25 there"). The **emitter** is the typesetter who finally prints the clean English book. Only the proofreader (checker) does deep reasoning — and it's by far the slowest step.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Each stage "sees" this one line differently:
const total: number = price * qty;

// SCANNER  → tokens: const | total | : | number | = | price | * | qty | ;
// PARSER   → AST: VariableDeclaration(name: total, type: number,
//                  init: BinaryExpression(price * qty))
// BINDER   → symbol "total" created; "price"/"qty" linked to their decls
// CHECKER  → are price & qty both number? is their product assignable
//            to number? (if price is a string → ERROR here)
// EMITTER  → emits:  const total = price * qty;   // ":number" stripped
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Walk me through what `tsc` does from source file to output, and which stage is the expensive one.**
