A: It runs **scanner → parser → binder → checker → emitter**. The scanner turns text into tokens, the parser builds an AST, the binder links names to their declarations (symbols/scopes), the checker infers and verifies types, and the emitter strips types to produce JavaScript (and optionally `.d.ts` files). The **checker** is the costly stage — type inference and assignability checks dominate build time — which is why "make `tsc` faster" almost always means "make the checker do less work".

---

### 🧠 The Language Service — The Brain Behind Your Editor

#### 🧩 What is it?
The **language service** is the same TypeScript engine running *as a long-lived background server* inside your editor (VS Code, etc.). It powers autocomplete, hover tooltips, go-to-definition, rename, inline errors, and quick-fixes. It's `tsc`'s machinery, but optimized to answer *small questions fast and repeatedly* as you type.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A batch compiler answers one big question: "is the whole project valid?" An editor needs the *opposite*: tiny, instant answers ("what are the members of `user.`?") thousands of times per session, while files keep changing. Re-running the full compiler on every keystroke would be unusably slow.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The language service keeps the program **in memory** and updates it *incrementally* as you type, reusing the parts that didn't change.

```
   YOU TYPE in the editor
        │  "user."
        ▼
 ┌────────────────────────────┐
 │  LANGUAGE SERVICE (server)  │
 │  • keeps AST + types in RAM │
 │  • reparses only edited file│
 │  • reuses unchanged results │
 └──────────┬─────────────────┘
            ▼
   answers: autocomplete list,
   hover type, errors, rename…
```

Because the same checker backs both, an error you see live in the editor and an error from `tsc` on the command line come from the *one* type system — they agree. The difference is *delivery*: the CLI does a full batch run; the language service does fast, partial updates.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`tsc` is a **publisher printing the whole finished book** in one big run. The language service is a **live editor sitting next to you as you write**, instantly underlining a typo the moment you make it — same grammar rules, but applied continuously to the paragraph you're touching instead of reprinting the entire book each time.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// There is no "code" you write for the language service — it's the
// engine your editor talks to. But you CAN observe its features:

interface User { id: number; name: string; }
declare const user: User;

user. // ← the autocomplete popup ("id", "name") comes from the
      //   language service inferring `user`'s type, NOT from `tsc`.

// Tip: in VS Code, "TypeScript: Restart TS Server" reboots exactly
// this background service when your editor's errors get out of sync.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Are the errors in my editor the same as the errors from `tsc`? Why does VS Code sometimes show different errors than my build?**
A: They come from the **same** type checker, so logically they agree. Differences are usually about *state*, not rules: the editor's **language service** holds an in-memory, incrementally-updated view that can drift (stale cache, a different `tsconfig` picked up, or an unsaved file), while `tsc` reads the saved files fresh. Restarting the TS server or re-running `tsc` makes them line up again.

---

### ✂️ Type-Checking vs. Transpiling — Stripping Types Is Not Checking Them

#### 🧩 What is it?
There are two completely different jobs hiding inside "compiling TypeScript":
- **Transpiling** (a.k.a. *type-stripping*): delete the type annotations and downlevel the syntax to produce runnable JavaScript. Fast and mechanical.
- **Type-checking**: actually *verify* that the types are correct. Slow and intelligent.

Tools like **Babel**, **esbuild**, and **swc** (popular fast build tools) do the *first* job only. `tsc` does both, but is mainly valued for the second.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Removing type annotations is easy — annotations are just extra text the runtime ignores, so a tool can delete them without understanding them. **Verifying** them requires the whole checker (inference, assignability, generics). esbuild/swc/Babel skip the checker entirely to be 10–100× faster. The catch: **they will happily compile code that is full of type errors**, because they never looked.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  ┌─ esbuild / swc / Babel ─────────────┐
  │  parse → STRIP types → emit .js     │
  │  (no checker!) FAST, but blind to   │
  │  type errors                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─ tsc ───────────────────────────────┐
  │  parse → bind → CHECK → emit         │
  │  SLOW, but catches type errors       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┘

  Typical real-world setup:
     esbuild/swc  →  builds the app (speed)
     tsc --noEmit →  checks types in CI (safety)
```

The standard pattern: use a fast tool to *build* and a separate `tsc --noEmit` step to *check*. `--noEmit` means "do the type-checking but don't write any `.js` files" — it runs the checker purely as a safety gate (typically in CI — your *Continuous Integration* server that runs checks on every push).

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A photocopier (esbuild/swc) duplicates a contract instantly — it doesn't read a word, so it can't tell you the contract has a contradictory clause. A lawyer (`tsc`) reads every line and flags the problems, but takes far longer. Sensible teams use **both**: photocopy for speed, lawyer for safety. You wouldn't ship a contract just because the photocopier didn't complain.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// This file is OBVIOUSLY wrong:
const age: number = "forty-two";   // string assigned to number 🔴
greet(undefined);                  // wrong argument

function greet(name: string) { return "Hi " + name.toUpperCase(); }

// esbuild / swc / Babel: ✅ emit JavaScript with NO complaints.
//   The bug only blows up at RUNTIME (or never warns at all),
//   because they stripped the types without checking them.
//
// tsc (or `tsc --noEmit`): 🔴 reports:
//   Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'.
//   Argument of type 'undefined' is not assignable to 'string'.
```

```jsonc
// package.json — the two-tool combo most teams actually use
{
  "scripts": {
    "build": "esbuild src/index.ts --bundle --outfile=out.js",
    "typecheck": "tsc --noEmit",      // the real safety net
    "ci": "npm run typecheck && npm run build"
  }
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: If esbuild already compiles my TypeScript, why do I still need to run `tsc`?**
A: Because esbuild (like swc and Babel) only **strips types** — it transpiles without ever **type-checking**. It will emit JavaScript even when your code is full of type errors. So you keep a separate `tsc --noEmit` step (usually in CI) as the actual type-safety gate. In short: the fast tool *builds*, `tsc` *verifies*. They do different jobs, and you want both.

---

### 🐌 What Makes Type-Checking Slow — The Checker's Worst Enemies

#### 🧩 What is it?
Certain type-system features force the checker to do an enormous amount of work. The slowness almost never comes from the *number of files* — it comes from a few **expensive type computations** that explode in size. Knowing the usual suspects lets you fix a slow build instead of just suffering it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A `tsc` run that takes 90 seconds wrecks your feedback loop and slows down CI for the whole team. But "TypeScript is slow" is not actionable. The useful question is: *which specific types* are blowing up the checker? Usually it's a handful.

#### ⚙️ How does it work? — the usual culprits
- **Large unions** — a union with hundreds/thousands of members (e.g. a giant string-literal union). Comparing a value against each member is roughly proportional to the union's size, and unions can multiply when combined.
- **Deep conditional & recursive types** — clever type-level code (`T extends U ? ... : ...` that calls itself). Each level of recursion is more work, and TypeScript caps recursion depth (you may hit `Type instantiation is excessively deep and possibly infinite`).
- **Inference blow-ups** — asking the compiler to *infer* a hugely complex type instead of you writing it down. A function whose return type the compiler must compute through many generic layers can balloon.
- **Huge `.d.ts` files** — giant library declaration files (some SDKs ship enormous ones). The checker loads and processes them all unless told otherwise (see the next topic on `skipLibCheck`).

A simplified picture of why a recursive/distributing type explodes:

```
  type Heavy = Permute<["a","b","c","d"]>
     │
     ▼  each element forks the work
   a─┐   b─┐   c─┐   d─┐
     ├..    ├..    ├..    ├..   ← branches multiply
   4 × 3 × 2 × 1  = 24 type instantiations for 4 items…
   …and the count EXPLODES (factorially) as items grow.
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Imagine planning a dinner. Checking "is this dish vegetarian?" for a 5-item menu is trivial. But "find every possible *ordering* of a 12-course menu" forces you to consider millions of combinations — the *menu* didn't get much bigger, but the *question you asked about it* exploded. Heavy conditional/recursive types ask the checker combinatorial questions like that.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// 🔴 A type that makes the checker sweat: every string literal in a
// big union, run through a recursive conditional type.
type BigUnion = "evt_1" | "evt_2" | /* …imagine 2,000 more… */ "evt_2003";

// A distributive recursive type re-runs for EACH union member:
type Prefix<T extends string> =
  T extends `${infer Head}_${infer Tail}` ? Prefix<Tail> : T;

type Result = Prefix<BigUnion>; // checker recurses per member → slow

// ✅ The cheap fix when you don't need the cleverness:
//    write the concrete type, or narrow the input, so the checker
//    has nothing to compute.
type ResultFast = string;
```

```bash
# Find the slow spot instead of guessing. This prints where time goes:
tsc --noEmit --extendedDiagnostics
# Look at "Check time" — if it dwarfs "Parse time", your TYPES (not
# your file count) are the bottleneck.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Your `tsc` build suddenly takes 2 minutes. How do you find out why, and what's usually the cause?**
A: Don't guess — measure with `tsc --extendedDiagnostics` (or `--generateTrace` for a detailed timeline) to see where the time goes; if "Check time" dominates, the **types** are the problem, not the file count. The usual culprits are a few heavy type computations: very large unions, deeply recursive/conditional types, inference blow-ups, and processing huge library `.d.ts` files. The fix is to simplify or pin those specific types (and turn on `skipLibCheck`), not to delete files.

---

### 🛠️ Speed Tools — `--incremental`, `--build`, `skipLibCheck` & Diagnostics

#### 🧩 What is it?
TypeScript ships several built-in switches whose whole purpose is to make checking faster or to tell you *why* it's slow:
- **`--incremental`** — remembers results between runs so it only rechecks what changed.
- **`--build` / `-b`** (a.k.a. *build mode*) — orchestrates **project references**, building separate sub-projects in order and skipping any that are unchanged.
- **`skipLibCheck`** — skip type-checking inside `.d.ts` declaration files.
- **`--extendedDiagnostics`** and **`--generateTrace`** — measurement tools that show where the time goes.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
By default, `tsc` rechecks **everything** on every run, even if you changed one line. On a large codebase that's wasteful. These tools let the compiler *reuse prior work* and let *you* see exactly where the time is being spent so you can fix the right thing.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **`--incremental`** writes a `.tsbuildinfo` file recording what it computed. Next run, files that didn't change (and whose dependencies didn't change) are skipped.
- **`--build`** is for **project references** — splitting a big repo into smaller TypeScript sub-projects (e.g. `core`, `api`, `web`) that depend on each other. Each is built once into its types and reused, so editing `web` doesn't recheck `core`.

```
   project references + tsc --build
   ┌────────┐     ┌────────┐     ┌────────┐
   │  core  │ ──▶ │  api   │ ──▶ │  web   │
   └────────┘     └────────┘     └────────┘
   built once,    reuses core's  reuses both
   cached         emitted types  → only web
                                    rebuilds
   Edit only `web` → core & api are SKIPPED.
```

- **`skipLibCheck`** — tells the checker: *don't* re-verify the internals of `.d.ts` files. Your *own* code is still fully checked against those types; only the *inside* of the library declarations is skipped. This is a huge, common speedup.

> **Note (simplification):** `skipLibCheck` does **not** mean "skip libraries". Your code is still checked against library types. It only skips checking the *declaration files against each other* — which is where conflicting/duplicate `@types` cause slow, noisy errors you usually can't fix anyway.

- **Diagnostics:** `--extendedDiagnostics` prints a timing/counters summary; `--generateTrace ./trace` writes a trace folder you open in a viewer to see the slowest type operations.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`--incremental` is a **chef prepping ingredients once and storing them in the fridge** — tomorrow's service reuses last night's prep instead of starting from scratch. **Project references with `--build`** is a **factory with separate assembly lines** for engine, body, and wheels: change the wheel line and you don't rebuild the engine. `skipLibCheck` is **trusting the certified parts you bought in** instead of re-inspecting every screw of every supplied component.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// tsconfig.json — the common "make it fast" baseline
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "incremental": true,        // cache results in .tsbuildinfo
    "skipLibCheck": true,       // don't re-check .d.ts internals (big win)
    "composite": true           // required to be referenced by other projects
  },
  // project references: this project depends on ../core being built first
  "references": [{ "path": "../core" }]
}
```

```bash
# Incremental check — fast on the 2nd+ run (reuses .tsbuildinfo):
tsc --noEmit --incremental

# Build mode for a monorepo of referenced projects (builds in order,
# skips unchanged ones):
tsc --build            # or: tsc -b
tsc --build --verbose  # see which projects were skipped vs rebuilt

# Measure where time goes:
tsc --noEmit --extendedDiagnostics
tsc --noEmit --generateTrace ./trace   # open in a trace viewer
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: A teammate sets `skipLibCheck: true` to speed up the build. Does that make your own code less type-safe?**
A: No — it skips checking the *internals* of `.d.ts` declaration files against each other, not the checking of *your* code against those types. Your code is still fully verified. The trade-off is that you might miss a genuine error *inside* a library's declarations, but those are rare and usually unfixable on your end, so most teams enable it. For broader speedups you'd add `--incremental` (cache between runs) and **project references with `tsc --build`** (skip unchanged sub-projects).

---

### 📜 `isolatedDeclarations` — Generating `.d.ts` Files Without the Checker

#### 🧩 What is it?
**`isolatedDeclarations`** is a `tsconfig` flag (added in TypeScript 5.5) that requires every *exported* value to have an **explicit, written-out type**. In return, it guarantees a `.d.ts` declaration file can be generated for any file *by looking at that one file alone* — without needing the full, slow checker to *infer* the exported types.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Normally, generating `.d.ts` files (the type-only files libraries ship) requires the checker, because TypeScript often *infers* the type of an export ("this function returns... let me compute it"). That inference is slow and forces the whole project to be type-checked together, file by file in dependency order — you can't easily parallelize it. `isolatedDeclarations` removes the guesswork: if every export's type is already written down, a *fast, non-type-checking tool* can produce the `.d.ts` files, one file at a time, in parallel.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
```
  NORMAL .d.ts emit:
    needs the CHECKER to INFER export types
    → must process files together, in order → slow

  isolatedDeclarations:
    every export's type is WRITTEN explicitly
    → no inference needed
    → any tool can emit .d.ts per-file, in PARALLEL → fast
```

The cost is on *you*, the author: you must annotate exported function return types, exported constants, etc. If you leave an export's type to be inferred, the compiler raises an error telling you to write it out. This unlocks fast, parallel declaration emit by *non-`tsc`* tools (like swc or esbuild-based pipelines), which couldn't produce correct `.d.ts` before because they don't type-check.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's the difference between a recipe that says *"season to taste"* (someone has to taste-test the whole pot to know the final result — slow, central) versus one that says *"add exactly 5g salt"* (any cook can follow it independently, in parallel, no tasting required). Writing explicit export types is "5g salt": it lets many tools work independently and fast.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// tsconfig: "isolatedDeclarations": true (also needs "declaration": true)

// 🔴 ERROR under isolatedDeclarations: return type is INFERRED,
//    so a per-file tool can't know it without the checker.
export function makeUser(name: string) {
  return { id: Date.now(), name };
}

// ✅ OK: the exported type is written explicitly → no inference needed,
//    a fast tool can emit the .d.ts from THIS FILE ALONE.
export function makeUserOk(name: string): { id: number; name: string } {
  return { id: Date.now(), name };
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What problem does `isolatedDeclarations` solve, and what does it cost you?**
A: It lets `.d.ts` declaration files be generated **without the type checker** — per-file and in parallel — by requiring every export to have an *explicit* type instead of an inferred one. That makes declaration emit much faster and lets non-`tsc` tools (swc/esbuild-based) produce correct types. The cost is author effort: you must annotate the types of all exports, and the compiler errors if you leave one to be inferred. It's especially valuable for **library authors** and large monorepos where `.d.ts` generation is a bottleneck.

---

### 🟢 Node's `--experimental-strip-types` — Running TypeScript Directly

#### 🧩 What is it?
Recent versions of **Node.js** can run a `.ts` file directly by **stripping the type annotations** at load time, using the flag **`--experimental-strip-types`** (and, in newer Node, this is increasingly on by default for simple cases). It is *type-stripping*, not type-checking — Node deletes the types and runs the resulting JavaScript.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Historically, to run a TypeScript file you had to compile it first (with `tsc`, `ts-node`, `tsx`, etc.) — an extra build step. Node's built-in stripping removes that friction for quick scripts and development: you just `node app.ts`. But — and this is the whole point of this module — because it only *strips*, it gives you **zero type-safety guarantees** at run time.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Node uses a fast type-stripper (built on swc) the moment it loads the file: it removes the annotations and executes the JavaScript that's left. It deliberately does **not** type-check, so it's fast and dependency-free — and it fits the exact same "strip vs check" lesson from earlier in this module.

```
   node --experimental-strip-types app.ts
        │
        ▼  Node STRIPS the types (no checking!)
   runs plain JS immediately
        │
        ▼
   ⚠️ type errors are NOT caught here
      → still run `tsc --noEmit` in CI
```

There's a stricter cousin, `--experimental-transform-types`, for TypeScript features that need *real code generation* (like enums and namespaces) rather than pure deletion — those can't be handled by stripping alone.

> **Note (simplification):** the exact flag names and which behavior is default keep changing across Node versions as the feature stabilizes. The durable takeaway is the category: **Node strips types to run them; it does not check them.**

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like an **airport scanner that's been switched to "let everyone through" mode** to clear the line faster — passengers (your code) move quickly, but nobody actually verified their tickets. Great for speed in the terminal (development), but you'd still want a real check (`tsc`) before anything important takes off (production/CI).

#### 💻 Code example
```bash
# Run a TypeScript file directly — Node strips the types and executes:
node --experimental-strip-types app.ts
# (in newer Node, plain `node app.ts` may work without the flag)

# ⚠️ This will RUN even if app.ts has type errors, because Node only
#    strips — it never checks. So you STILL keep this in CI:
tsc --noEmit
```

```typescript
// app.ts
const port: number = "3000";   // 🔴 a real type error...
console.log("starting on", port);

// `node --experimental-strip-types app.ts` → prints "starting on 3000"
//   (it ran! the bad type was simply stripped, never checked)
// `tsc --noEmit` → 🔴 Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Node can now run `.ts` files directly. Does that replace `tsc`?**
A: No. Node's `--experimental-strip-types` (built on swc) only **strips** type annotations and runs the JavaScript — it never type-checks, exactly like esbuild/swc/Babel. It's great for skipping a build step in development, but it gives no type-safety guarantee, so you still run `tsc --noEmit` (typically in CI) to actually catch type errors. It removes a *build step*, not the *checker*.

---

<h2 id="module-14">Module 14 — Migration & Library Authoring</h2>

You inherited a 200,000-line JavaScript app and a deadline. Nobody is going to let you stop the world and rewrite it in TypeScript over six months. So how do you add type safety *gradually* — while the team keeps shipping? And once your code is typed, how do you package it so that *other* people's editors light up with autocomplete the moment they `npm install` it? This module is about those two real jobs: **migrating** an existing codebase into types, and **authoring a library** whose types "just work" for everyone who uses it.

---

### 🪜 JS → TS Migration — typing a codebase one step at a time

#### 🧩 What is it?
Migration is the process of turning an existing JavaScript codebase into a TypeScript one **incrementally** — file by file — instead of all at once. TypeScript is built for this: it can read your plain `.js` files, type-check them in place, and let you convert one file to `.ts` at a time without breaking the rest. (**"Type-check"** = the compiler reads your code and flags type mistakes, like calling `.toUpperCase()` on a number.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A "big-bang rewrite" — converting everything at once — is the riskiest move in software. The whole app breaks until *every* file is fixed, you can't ship features in the meantime, and the giant pull request is impossible to review. Real codebases are too big and too important to freeze. We need a way to gain type safety **progressively**: every week the code is a little safer, the app never stops working, and the team keeps shipping.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
TypeScript gives you a set of dials in `tsconfig.json` (**the config file that controls the compiler**) to mix JS and TS, then tighten safety over time:

- **`allowJs: true`** — tells the compiler "also read my `.js` files," so JS and TS live side by side in one project.
- **`checkJs: true`** — "also *type-check* those `.js` files," surfacing errors in plain JavaScript before you've converted it.
- **`// @ts-check`** — a comment at the top of a single `.js` file that turns on checking for just *that* file (useful when you don't want `checkJs` globally yet).
- **JSDoc types** — special `/** */` comments that add types to JavaScript *without any new syntax* (more on these below).

The recommended path is a **migration ramp** — start permissive, then ratchet (tighten one notch at a time, never loosening):

```
  Phase 1        Phase 2          Phase 3         Phase 4
  ────────       ────────         ────────        ────────
  allowJs    →   checkJs +    →   rename .js  →   strict:
  (JS+TS         JSDoc on         to .ts,         true,
  coexist)       key files        fix errors      noImplicitAny
                                                  on
   loosest ───────────────────────────────────▶ strictest
   safety grows at every step; you never go back
```

**Ratcheting strictness** means turning on stricter flags gradually and never turning them back off — like a ratchet wrench that only clicks one direction. You might enable `noImplicitAny` (**ban silently-untyped values**) only after every file compiles cleanly without it.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Renovating a house you still live in. You don't demolish the whole place and sleep in a tent for six months. You renovate **one room at a time** — kitchen this month, bathroom next — while the rest of the house stays livable. `allowJs` is "the old rooms still work while I redo this one." Ratcheting strictness is the building inspector who signs off room by room and never lets you downgrade a finished room back to code-violation.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// tsconfig.json — a sane Phase-1/2 migration config
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "allowJs": true,        // compile .js alongside .ts
    "checkJs": false,       // don't check ALL .js yet — opt in per file
    "strict": false,        // ratchet this on LATER
    "noImplicitAny": false, // turn on once files are clean
    "outDir": "dist"
  }
}
```

```javascript
// payments.js — opt THIS file into checking, no .ts rename needed
// @ts-check

/**
 * @param {number} cents
 * @returns {string}
 */
function format(cents) {
  return "$" + (cents / 100).toFixed(2);
}

format("500");
//     ~~~~~  Error: Argument of type 'string' is not
//            assignable to parameter of type 'number'.
// ^ Real type-checking on a plain .js file — NO build step,
//   NO rename. Just a comment turned the checker on.
```

> **Note (simplification):** A real migration also involves installing `@types/*` packages for your dependencies and may need a tool like `ts-migrate` for bulk conversion. The ramp above is the strategy, not the full checklist.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How would you migrate a 200k-line JS codebase to TS without a big-bang rewrite?**
A: Incrementally. Turn on `allowJs` so JS and TS coexist in one project, then add types *in place* using `// @ts-check` plus JSDoc comments on the highest-value files first — this catches bugs with **no rename and no build step**. Convert files `.js` → `.ts` a few at a time, fixing errors as you go. Crucially, **ratchet strictness**: keep `strict` and `noImplicitAny` off at first, then enable them one flag at a time once the codebase compiles cleanly — and never turn them back off. The app keeps shipping the whole way.

---

### 📝 JSDoc Types & `@ts-check` — type safety with zero new syntax

#### 🧩 What is it?
**JSDoc** is a long-standing convention for documenting JavaScript with structured `/** */` comments. TypeScript understands a subset of JSDoc as *real types*: write `@param {string} name` in a comment and TypeScript treats `name` as a `string` — full checking and autocomplete — even though the file is still plain `.js` with no TypeScript syntax in it.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
Early in a migration, renaming a file to `.ts` forces it through a **build step** (a compile pass that turns `.ts` into runnable `.js`) and may break your existing tooling, scripts, or `require` paths. That's friction you don't want yet. JSDoc lets you get **typing without a build step**: the file runs *as-is* in Node or the browser because it's still valid JavaScript, while your editor and `tsc` check it as if it were typed.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You put `// @ts-check` at the top of a `.js` file (or set `checkJs: true` globally), then annotate with JSDoc tags. The compiler parses the comments and applies the same type rules it would for `.ts`:

```
  plain .js file
       │
       │  add  // @ts-check  +  /** @param {T} */
       ▼
  tsc / editor reads the COMMENTS as types
       │
       ├──▶ errors + autocomplete (compile time)
       │
       ▼
  file STILL runs unchanged at runtime
  (comments are invisible to the JS engine)
```

Common tags: `@param`, `@returns`, `@type`, `@typedef` (define a reusable type), and `@template` (a generic type parameter). You can even import a TypeScript type into a JS file with `@type {import('./types').User}`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Sticky-note labels on unlabeled jars in your pantry. You haven't bought new jars (no rename, no new syntax) — you've just added notes saying "this one is *flour*, this one is *sugar*." Anyone reading the shelf (the compiler, your editor) now knows what's inside and warns you if you reach for sugar when the recipe said flour. Peel the notes off and the jars are exactly as before — just like JSDoc comments vanish at runtime.

#### 💻 Code example
```javascript
// user.js
// @ts-check

/**
 * @typedef {Object} User
 * @property {string} name
 * @property {number} age
 */

/**
 * @param {User} u
 * @returns {string}
 */
function greet(u) {
  return `Hi ${u.name}, age ${u.age}`;
}

greet({ name: "Ada", age: 36 }); // ok
greet({ name: "Ada" });
//    ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~  Error: Property 'age' is missing
//                      in type '{ name: string; }'.

// This file runs in Node TODAY with `node user.js`.
// The types are checked, but NOTHING was transpiled.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Can you get TypeScript's safety without adding a build step?**
A: Yes — use `// @ts-check` plus JSDoc type comments in plain `.js` files. The compiler (and your editor) reads the JSDoc as real types and reports errors and autocomplete, but the file stays valid JavaScript that runs unchanged — there's no `.ts` → `.js` transpile. It's the lowest-friction first step in a migration, and some projects (notably parts of the TypeScript and Node ecosystems) ship typed JSDoc'd JS permanently.

---

### 🧤 Taming `any` — `unknown` at the edges, `@ts-expect-error` over `@ts-ignore`

#### 🧩 What is it?
During a migration you'll hit values you *can't* type yet, plus errors you must temporarily silence. **Taming `any`** is the discipline of doing this safely: using **`unknown`** instead of `any` at the untyped boundaries of your code, preferring **`@ts-expect-error`** over **`@ts-ignore`** when you must suppress an error, and tracking every such suppression as **debt** (work you've deliberately postponed). (**`any`** = "turn off all type checking for this value"; **`unknown`** = "I don't know the type yet, so force me to check before I use it.")

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
`any` is **infectious**: once a value is `any`, anything it touches becomes unchecked too, and the holes spread silently through your codebase. Suppression comments are worse when misused — `@ts-ignore` *silently* swallows whatever error is on the next line, **even after you've fixed the underlying problem**, so it rots into a permanent blindfold. We need tools that fail *safe* and *clean up after themselves*.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Two key moves:

**1. `unknown` at boundaries.** Anywhere untyped data enters — `JSON.parse`, a `fetch` response, a legacy module — type it as `unknown`, not `any`. `unknown` accepts any value but forbids *using* it until you narrow it (check its shape), so the unsafety stays contained at the edge.

**2. `@ts-expect-error` over `@ts-ignore`.** This is the most important accuracy point in the module:

```
  @ts-ignore                  @ts-expect-error
  ──────────                  ────────────────
  suppress error on           suppress error on
  next line, ALWAYS           next line, BUT...
                              if there is NO error,
  no error? still silent.     IT becomes an error.
                              "unused @ts-expect-error"
  → rots forever              → SELF-CLEANING
```

`@ts-expect-error` *expects* the next line to have an error. If you later fix the code so the error is gone, the directive itself now errors with "Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive" — a reminder to delete it. `@ts-ignore` gives you no such signal; it stays silent whether the error exists or not, so dead suppressions accumulate.

**3. Track the debt.** Add a reason and a ticket to every suppression so you can grep for and burn down the count over time.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
`@ts-ignore` is a **permanent "OUT OF ORDER" sign** taped over a machine — even after a technician fixes the machine, the sign stays up and people keep avoiding a perfectly working device. `@ts-expect-error` is a **sign wired to the machine's sensor**: the moment the fault clears, the sign starts *beeping* at you to take it down. One rots into a lie; the other forces honesty.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// 1. unknown at the boundary — contains the unsafety
async function loadUser(): Promise<unknown> {
  const res = await fetch("/api/user");
  return res.json(); // .json() returns `any` — we widen to unknown
}

const data = await loadUser();
// data.name;  // Error: 'data' is of type 'unknown'.
if (typeof data === "object" && data && "name" in data) {
  console.log(data.name); // ok — narrowed before use
}

// 2. @ts-expect-error is SELF-CLEANING
// @ts-expect-error legacy lib has wrong types — TICKET-482
legacyWidget.render({ mode: "dark" });

// If legacyWidget's types get fixed so this line is valid,
// TS reports:  Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive.
// → it nags you to delete the now-pointless suppression.

// 3. @ts-ignore would suppress SILENTLY, forever — avoid it.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: `@ts-ignore` vs `@ts-expect-error` — which and why?**
A: Prefer `@ts-expect-error`. Both suppress the error on the following line, but `@ts-expect-error` *expects* an error to be there: if the underlying code is later fixed and the error disappears, the directive itself becomes an error ("unused directive"), prompting you to remove it. `@ts-ignore` suppresses unconditionally and stays silent forever, so stale suppressions pile up and can hide *new*, real errors that drift onto that line. `@ts-expect-error` is self-cleaning; `@ts-ignore` rots. (And at boundaries, reach for `unknown` over `any` so you must narrow before use.)

---

### 📦 Library Authoring — shipping types so consumers "just work"

#### 🧩 What is it?
Library authoring is publishing an npm package whose **types ship with it**, so anyone who installs it gets autocomplete and type-checking automatically — without needing a separate `@types/...` package. The two pieces that make this work are **declaration files** (`.d.ts` files — type-only descriptions of your code with no implementation) and the **`exports` map** in `package.json` (a modern field that tells tools which file to load for each scenario, including a special `types` slot).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The JavaScript world ships packages in two incompatible module formats: **ESM** (`import`/`export`, the modern standard) and **CommonJS** (`require`/`module.exports`, the old Node style). (**"Module format"** = how a file declares its imports and exports.) A consumer might use either. If your package points everyone at one `.d.ts` file regardless, the types can subtly mismatch the actual code they load — leading to the dreaded "the types are wrong" or "could not find a declaration file" errors. We need each consumer to resolve the *right* types for the format they're actually using.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Modern tools read the **`exports` map** and pick a branch based on **conditions** — `import` (ESM consumer), `require` (CJS consumer), and a `types` condition that points at the matching `.d.ts`:

```
  consumer writes:                    package.json "exports"
  ─────────────────                   resolves to:
  import x from "lib"   ─────────────▶ exports["."].import.types  → esm.d.ts
                                       exports["."].import.default → esm.js

  require("lib")        ─────────────▶ exports["."].require.types  → cjs.d.cts
                                       exports["."].require.default → cjs.js
```

The golden rules:
- Put the **`types` condition FIRST** inside each branch — tools match conditions top-to-bottom and the `types` key must be found before `default`.
- Provide **separate `.d.ts` for ESM and `.d.cts` for CJS** when their shapes differ (this is "dual" type resolution). For TypeScript to verify this is correct, set `moduleResolution` to `node16`/`nodenext` and check with `@arethetypeswrong/cli` (a tool that simulates how consumers resolve your types).
- Always include a top-level `"types"` field too, as a fallback for older tooling that doesn't read `exports`.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A power adapter for international travel. Your appliance (the consumer's project) might have a UK, EU, or US plug (ESM or CJS). A good adapter doesn't hand everyone the same prong and hope — it has a **labeled socket for each plug type** and routes you to the matching one. The `exports` map is that adapter; the `types` condition is the little label that also tells the device *how much voltage to expect* so nothing sparks.

#### 💻 Code example
```jsonc
// package.json — dual ESM/CJS with correct type resolution
{
  "name": "my-lib",
  "type": "module",
  "types": "./dist/esm/index.d.ts",   // fallback for old tooling
  "exports": {
    ".": {
      "import": {
        "types": "./dist/esm/index.d.ts",  // ESM types — FIRST
        "default": "./dist/esm/index.js"
      },
      "require": {
        "types": "./dist/cjs/index.d.cts", // CJS types — FIRST
        "default": "./dist/cjs/index.cjs"
      }
    }
  }
}
// ⚠️ Order matters: "types" must come BEFORE "default" in each
// branch, or tools resolve the .js file and find no types.
// Verify with:  npx @arethetypeswrong/cli  &&  tsc --noEmit
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What does a library author need to get right so consumers' types "just work"?**
A: Ship the `.d.ts` declaration files *inside* the package (so no separate `@types` is needed), and resolve them correctly per module format using the `package.json` `exports` map. Each branch (`import` for ESM, `require` for CJS) needs a `types` condition listed **before** `default`, pointing at the matching declaration file — `.d.ts` for ESM, `.d.cts` for CJS when the shapes differ. Keep a top-level `"types"` field as a fallback, build under `moduleResolution: nodenext`, and verify with `@arethetypeswrong/cli` so you catch the classic "wrong types for CJS consumers" bug before publishing.

---

### 🧰 DTS Bundling & Type Versioning — your types are a public API

#### 🧩 What is it?
**DTS bundling** is the step that rolls all your scattered `.d.ts` declaration files into one (or a few) clean public type files, while **hiding internal types** that consumers shouldn't see. **Type versioning** is treating those public types as part of your API contract — marking things `@internal` (not for consumers), `@deprecated` (don't use, will be removed), and documenting them — so changing a type is a deliberate, communicated act, not an accident. Tools: **`api-extractor`** (Microsoft's DTS roll-up + API report tool) and **`tsup`** (a zero-config bundler with a `dts` option).

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
By default `tsc` emits one `.d.ts` per source file, mirroring your folder structure — including all your *internal* helper types. Two problems: (1) consumers see and may start depending on types you meant to keep private, so you can never refactor them without a breaking change; (2) dozens of `.d.ts` files with internal cross-imports are fragile and leak implementation detail. And once a type is public, **changing it can silently break every consumer** — so you need a way to mark and retire types safely.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
DTS bundling traces your *entry point* (the file listed in `exports`) and pulls in only the types actually reachable from your public exports, inlining them into one file and **dropping anything marked `@internal`**:

```
  src/index.d.ts ─┐
  src/utils.d.ts ─┤   api-extractor / tsup --dts
  src/secret.d.ts─┘            │
   (@internal)                 ▼
              ┌──────────────────────────────┐
              │  dist/index.d.ts  (ONE file)  │
              │  • only public types inlined  │
              │  • @internal types STRIPPED   │
              └──────────────────────────────┘
```

Versioning markers (read by tooling and shown in editors):
- **`@internal`** — "not part of the public API"; bundlers can strip these so consumers never see them.
- **`@deprecated`** — keeps the type but flags it; editors show a ~~strikethrough~~ so users migrate off before you remove it.
- Doc comments (`/** ... */`) — become the hover documentation consumers read in their IDE.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A restaurant. The **menu** (your bundled `.d.ts`) is what guests see and order from — clean, curated, no mention of the freezer brand or the prep-cook's knife technique. The **kitchen** (your `@internal` types) stays behind the door; guests never depend on how you chop onions, so you can re-organize the kitchen freely. A `@deprecated` dish is one you've crossed off and labeled "available this week only" so regulars stop ordering it before it disappears for good.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// src/index.ts
export interface PublicConfig {
  /** The API key. @deprecated Use `token` instead. */
  apiKey?: string;
  /** Bearer token for auth. */
  token: string;
}

/**
 * Internal cache shape — NOT for consumers.
 * @internal
 */
export interface CacheEntry { hash: string; ttl: number; }
```

```jsonc
// tsup.config — bundle the .d.ts and strip internals
{
  "entry": ["src/index.ts"],
  "format": ["esm", "cjs"],
  "dts": { "resolve": true }   // roll up types into one file
  // api-extractor equivalent: set "stripInternal": true so
  // every @internal export is REMOVED from the published .d.ts.
}
// Result: consumers see PublicConfig (with `apiKey` shown
// struck-through as deprecated) but NEVER see CacheEntry.
```

> **Note (simplification):** `tsc` itself has a `stripInternal` flag that drops `@internal` declarations from emitted `.d.ts`; `api-extractor` adds API reports and review gating on top. Exact behavior depends on which tool and config you use.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Why bundle your `.d.ts` files, and how do you keep internal types out of your public API?**
A: Bundling (via `api-extractor` or `tsup --dts`) rolls your many per-file declarations into one clean entry-point file that exposes *only* what's reachable from your public exports — fewer files, no leaked cross-imports. To hide internals, mark helper types `@internal` and enable stripping (`stripInternal` / api-extractor), so they're removed from the published declarations and consumers can't depend on them — which keeps you free to refactor. Then treat the remaining public types as an API: use `@deprecated` to retire things gracefully and doc comments for IDE hover help, so type changes are versioned and communicated rather than silently breaking consumers.

---

<h2 id="module-15">Module 15 — Type-Driven Design & Architecture</h2>

You've learned how the type system *works*. Now you use it as a **design tool**: shaping your types so the compiler refuses to let bad code exist in the first place. An architect doesn't just describe data — they make wrong states impossible to write, design APIs that feel effortless to use, and know exactly when a clever type is worth its weight (and when it isn't). This module is about wielding types as **guardrails for an entire codebase**.

---

### 🚫 Make Illegal States Unrepresentable — the compiler as a bouncer

#### 🧩 What is it?
A design principle: shape your types so that a **bad combination of values literally won't type-check**. Instead of allowing a wrong state to exist and then *checking* for it at runtime, you model the data so the wrong state **cannot be written down at all**. ("Unrepresentable" just means: there is no value of that type that represents the bad case.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A very common but *leaky* way to model a network request looks like this:

```typescript
// LEAKY: every field is optional, so nonsense states are allowed
interface RequestState<T> {
  isLoading: boolean;
  data?: T;
  error?: string;
}
```

With four independent fields, the compiler happily accepts states that make no sense: `isLoading: true` **and** `data` present, or `data` **and** `error` both set at once. Now every reader of this object must remember the unwritten rules ("if loading, ignore data"). Those rules live in your head and in comments — not in the type — so eventually someone breaks one.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Replace the loose object with a **discriminated union** (a union of object types that share one literal "tag" field — here, `status`). Each variant carries **only** the fields that are valid in that state. There is no variant where `loading` and `data` coexist, so that combination simply has no type.

```
 LEAKY: 1 shape, 4 free fields   GOOD: 4 exact shapes (a union)
 ┌───────────────────────────┐    ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐
 │ isLoading │ data │ error  │    │ "idle"     │ │ "success" + data │
 │  any combo is allowed (!) │    ├────────────┤ ├──────────────────┤
 └───────────────────────────┘    │ "loading"  │ │ "error" + message│
   → bad states representable      └────────────┘ └──────────────────┘
                                     only ONE status at a time
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A nightclub bouncer (the compiler) checks IDs at the door. The leaky design is *no bouncer* — anyone walks in, and you check IDs later at the bar (runtime), after the trouble's already inside. The discriminated union is a **strict guest list**: if your combination isn't on the list, you never get through the door. The bad state never enters the building.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Each variant lists ONLY the fields legal in that state.
type RequestState<T> =
  | { status: "idle" }
  | { status: "loading" }
  | { status: "success"; data: T }
  | { status: "error"; message: string };

function render(state: RequestState<string>): string {
  switch (state.status) {
    case "idle":    return "Nothing yet";
    case "loading": return "Spinner...";
    case "success": return state.data;     // `data` exists ONLY here
    case "error":   return state.message;  // `message` exists ONLY here
  }
}

// The compiler REJECTS illegal combinations (verified, tsc 5.9 --strict):
const bad: RequestState<string> = { status: "loading", data: "oops" };
// ERROR TS2353: 'data' does not exist in type '{ status: "loading"; }'

function broken(state: RequestState<string>) {
  return state.data;
}
// ERROR TS2339: Property 'data' does not exist on type
// '{ status: "idle"; }' — you must narrow on `status` first.
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How would you design types so an invalid state can't be constructed?**
A: Use a **discriminated union** keyed on a literal tag, where each variant holds only the fields valid in that state — rather than one object full of optional fields. The illegal combinations (e.g. "loading" with data) then have no corresponding type, so they fail to compile. To read a field, you must first narrow on the tag, which the compiler enforces. This moves invariants from runtime checks and comments into the type system itself.

---

### 📡 Type-Safe Patterns — a typed event emitter, builders, DI & reducers

#### 🧩 What is it?
A toolbox of design patterns wired up so the types flow automatically. The star example is a **typed event emitter** — an object you call `on('login', user => ...)` on, where the compiler *already knows* that a `login` handler receives a user object, a `logout` handler receives nothing, and a typo'd event name is a compile error. (An **event emitter** is just a tiny pub/sub hub: code *subscribes* to named events with `on`, and other code *fires* them with `emit`.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
The classic Node `EventEmitter` is typed with `(event: string, ...args: any[])`. Every handler argument is `any` — the compiler knows nothing. You can subscribe to `"loginn"` (a typo), expect the wrong payload shape, and nothing complains until it breaks in production. The same looseness plagues hand-rolled **builders** (chained `.setX().setY()` calls), **dependency injection** (passing a bag of services), and **reducers** (the `switch` in a state container) — each is a place where `any` and stringly-typed keys silently creep in.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
You describe every event and its payload **once** in a single `interface`, then make the emitter **generic over that map**. `keyof Events` restricts the event name to real keys; indexed access `Events[K]` looks up the exact payload type for whichever key you passed. One source of truth, total inference.

```
        AppEvents (one source of truth)
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ login:  { userId; name }             │
   │ logout: undefined                    │
   │ message:{ from; text }               │
   └──────────────────────────────────────┘
        │  on<K>(event: K, h: Handler<Events[K]>)
        ▼
   on("login", user => …)   user: { userId; name }
   on("typo",  …)           ✗ compile error: not a key
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Think of a hotel switchboard with **clearly labelled extensions**. Dialling "login" always connects you to the front desk, "message" to the concierge — the labels and who picks up are fixed in advance. A stringly-typed emitter is dialling **random digits and hoping** the right person answers; the typed emitter is the printed extension list where wrong numbers don't exist.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// 1) Describe events + payloads ONCE.
interface AppEvents {
  login: { userId: string; name: string };
  logout: undefined;
  message: { from: string; text: string };
}

type Handler<E> = (payload: E) => void;

class TypedEmitter<Events> {
  // For each event key K, store an array of handlers for THAT payload.
  private handlers: {
    [K in keyof Events]?: Array<Handler<Events[K]>>;
  } = {};

  // `event` must be a real key; `handler` must match that key's payload.
  on<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, handler: Handler<Events[K]>): void {
    (this.handlers[event] ??= []).push(handler);
  }

  emit<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, payload: Events[K]): void {
    this.handlers[event]?.forEach((h) => h(payload));
  }
}

const bus = new TypedEmitter<AppEvents>();

bus.on("login", (user) => {
  // `user` is inferred as { userId: string; name: string } — no annotation!
  console.log(user.userId, user.name);
});

bus.emit("login", { userId: "u1", name: "Ada" });
bus.emit("logout", undefined);

// All three below are COMPILE ERRORS (verified, tsc 5.9 --strict):
// bus.on("login", (user) => console.log(user.text)); // TS2339: no `text`
// bus.emit("login", { userId: "u1" });               // TS2345: missing `name`
// bus.on("typo", () => {});                           // TS2345: not an event
```

> **Note (simplification):** An `interface` like `AppEvents` does not satisfy a `Record<string, unknown>` constraint (interfaces have no implicit index signature), so we leave `Events` unconstrained for clarity. A production library might constrain it with a `type` alias instead, e.g. `type EventMap = Record<string, unknown>`.

The same "describe once, infer everywhere" idea powers the other patterns:

```typescript
// BUILDER: `return this` keeps the chain fully typed end to end.
class QueryBuilder {
  private parts: string[] = [];
  select(cols: string): this { this.parts.push(`SELECT ${cols}`); return this; }
  from(table: string): this  { this.parts.push(`FROM ${table}`);  return this; }
  build(): string { return this.parts.join(" "); }
}
const q = new QueryBuilder().select("*").from("users").build();
// q === "SELECT * FROM users"

// DEPENDENCY INJECTION: pass a typed bag of services (no globals).
interface Logger { log(msg: string): void; }
interface Database { query(sql: string): string[]; }
function createUserService(deps: { logger: Logger; db: Database }) {
  return {
    list() { deps.logger.log("listing"); return deps.db.query("SELECT 1"); },
  };
}

// EXHAUSTIVE REDUCER: assertNever makes a forgotten case a COMPILE error.
type CounterAction =
  | { type: "increment"; by: number }
  | { type: "decrement"; by: number }
  | { type: "reset" };

function assertNever(x: never): never {
  throw new Error("Unhandled: " + JSON.stringify(x));
}
function reducer(state: number, action: CounterAction): number {
  switch (action.type) {
    case "increment": return state + action.by;
    case "decrement": return state - action.by;
    case "reset":     return 0;
    default:          return assertNever(action); // add a case → this errors
  }
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: Type a fully type-safe event emitter where `on('login', user => ...)` infers the payload.**
A: Define an interface mapping each event name to its payload type, then make the emitter generic over that map (`class TypedEmitter<Events>`). In `on<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, handler: Handler<Events[K]>)`, `keyof Events` constrains the name to real events and the **indexed access** `Events[K]` looks up the exact payload, so the handler argument is inferred and typos are rejected. Store handlers in a mapped-type record `{ [K in keyof Events]?: Handler<Events[K]>[] }` so each bucket stays typed.

---

### 🎁 Inference-Friendly Public API Design — make the easy thing the typed thing

#### 🧩 What is it?
Designing the **signatures of functions and generics that other people call** so the right types appear *automatically*, with no manual `<TypeArgs>` and no `as` casts. "Inference-friendly" means: the consumer writes ordinary code, and TypeScript figures out the precise types from the *values they passed in*. (**Inference** = the compiler deducing a type you didn't write explicitly.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
An API can be type-*correct* yet miserable to use. If consumers must spell out generics on every call — `getFirst<User>(users)` — or sprinkle `as` to fix what inference got wrong, your "type safety" has become a tax. Worse, a poorly-placed type parameter can infer too *wide* (`string` instead of the literal `"login"`), throwing away the very precision you wanted. Good API design puts the type parameters where the compiler can fill them in from the arguments.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
The core rule: **make type parameters inferable from a value argument**, and constrain them just enough to look up related types. Put `<T>` on the parameter the caller supplies, return a type derived from it, and let inference cascade. Compare:

```
 Consumer must help (bad)        Inference does it (good)
 ┌──────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────┐
 │ getFirst<number>([1,2,3])│    │ getFirst([1, 2, 3])          │
 │ ^ caller spells out type │    │ → returns number | undefined │
 └──────────────────────────┘    │   inferred from the argument │
                                  └──────────────────────────────┘
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
A great API is like a **well-designed power socket**: you push the plug in and it just fits — you don't measure voltages or carry adapters. A badly-designed one makes every user bring their own converter (manual type args, casts) before anything works. The ergonomics live in the socket's design, not in the user's effort.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// Type parameter T is inferred from the ARRAY argument — caller writes nothing.
function first<T>(arr: readonly T[]): T | undefined {
  return arr[0];
}
const f = first([1, 2, 3]);   // f: number | undefined  (inferred)
const s = first(["a", "b"]);  // s: string | undefined  (inferred)

// A typed "pluck": K is constrained to real keys, return type is exact.
function pluck<T, K extends keyof T>(items: readonly T[], key: K): T[K][] {
  return items.map((item) => item[key]);
}
const names = pluck([{ id: 1, name: "Ada" }], "name");
// names: string[]  — inferred from the objects AND the key "name".
// pluck([{ id: 1 }], "nope"); // ERROR: "nope" is not a key of the object.
```

> **Note (simplification):** When you *do* want literal-precise inference (e.g. capturing `"login"` not `string`), reach for `const` type parameters (`<const T>`) or the `satisfies` operator from Module 7 — those are the precision dials for public APIs.

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: What makes a generic API "ergonomic," and how do you achieve it?**
A: Ergonomic means consumers get precise types **without** annotating type arguments or casting. Achieve it by placing type parameters where they're inferable from a value argument, constraining them (`K extends keyof T`) so you can derive related types via indexed access, and returning a type computed from the inputs. Avoid forcing the caller to supply `<T>` and avoid signatures that infer too wide; use `const` type params or `satisfies` when you need literal precision.

---

### 🏷️ Domain Modelling — branded IDs, value objects & result types

#### 🧩 What is it?
Encoding the **rules of your business domain** directly in types. Three pillars: **branded IDs** (making a `UserId` and a `PostId` non-interchangeable even though both are strings), **value objects** (small types that carry meaning and validity, like an `Email` that's *guaranteed* well-formed), and **result/either types** (returning success-or-failure as a value instead of `throw`ing). (A "domain" is just the real-world problem your app models — orders, users, payments.)

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
TypeScript is **structural**: any two `string`s are interchangeable, so nothing stops you passing a `postId` where a `userId` was expected — a bug the compiler can't see. And `throw` is invisible in a function's type: `function getUser(id): User` gives no hint it might explode. Callers forget to wrap it in `try/catch`, and the failure path is untested. Domain modelling makes **identity and fallibility visible in the types**.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
- **Branding** intersects the base type with a phantom marker (`& { __brand: "UserId" }`) that exists only at compile time — erased at runtime, so there's zero cost. Different brands are now incompatible.
- **Result types** are a discriminated union `{ ok: true; value } | { ok: false; error }`. The caller *must* check `ok` before touching `value`, so the error path can't be skipped.

```
   throw style                 Result style
   getUser(id): User           getUser(id): Result<User, NotFound>
        │                            │
   may explode (invisible)      ok? ─┬─ true  → use .value
   caller may forget try/catch       └─ false → handle .error
                                   compiler forces the check
```

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
Branded IDs are like **colour-coded keycards** in a building: the cleaning and server-room cards are both plastic rectangles, but the reader rejects the wrong colour — you physically can't swap them. A Result type is a **delivery with a signature slip**: you can't just grab the parcel; you must first acknowledge whether it arrived or was marked "address not found."

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// BRANDED IDs — a string tagged with a compile-time-only marker.
type Brand<T, B> = T & { readonly __brand: B };
type UserId = Brand<string, "UserId">;
type PostId = Brand<string, "PostId">;

function asUserId(s: string): UserId { return s as UserId; } // the one cast
function getUser(id: UserId): void { console.log("fetching", id); }

const uid = asUserId("u1");
getUser(uid);            // ok
// getUser("u1");        // ERROR TS2345: string is not a UserId
// const p: PostId = uid;// ERROR TS2322: UserId is not a PostId
// (Both verified to error with tsc 5.9 --strict.)

// RESULT TYPE — success or failure as a value, not a throw.
type Result<T, E> =
  | { ok: true; value: T }
  | { ok: false; error: E };

function parseAge(input: string): Result<number, string> {
  const n = Number(input);
  if (Number.isNaN(n)) return { ok: false, error: "not a number" };
  if (n < 0)           return { ok: false, error: "negative age" };
  return { ok: true, value: n };
}

const r = parseAge("42");
if (r.ok) {
  console.log("age:", r.value);   // `value` exists ONLY when ok === true
} else {
  console.log("error:", r.error); // `error` exists ONLY when ok === false
}
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: How do you stop two `string` IDs being interchangeable, and why prefer a Result type over throwing?**
A: Use **branded (nominal) types** — intersect `string` with a unique phantom field like `& { __brand: "UserId" }`. The brand is erased at runtime (zero cost) but makes `UserId` and `PostId` incompatible, so the compiler catches mixed-up IDs. A **Result type** (`{ ok: true; value } | { ok: false; error }`) makes failure part of the function's return type, so callers are forced to handle the error branch before using the value — unlike `throw`, which is invisible in the signature and easy to forget.

---

### ⚖️ Architect Trade-Offs — when a clever type is *not* worth it

#### 🧩 What is it?
The judgement call every architect makes: **how much type-level machinery is the right amount** for this codebase, this team, this moment. Type-level power (deep generics, conditional and recursive types) isn't free — it costs **readability**, **compile time**, and demands a **team skill ceiling** high enough to maintain it. This topic is about consciously balancing those, not maximising cleverness.

#### 🤔 Why do we need it? (the problem)
A type so clever that only its author understands it is a **liability**: teammates can't change it, error messages become walls of text, and the build slows as the checker grinds through huge conditional/recursive types. The opposite extreme — `any` everywhere — gives up safety entirely. The cost of getting this wrong is real: a stuck build, a PR no reviewer can approve, or a runtime bug that "strong types" gave false confidence about.

#### ⚙️ How does it work?
Weigh four forces on every typing decision:

```
  TYPE-LEVEL POWER ──────────────────────────────────────►
  catches more bugs          but costs more of ↓↓↓
  ┌──────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │ READABILITY  │ COMPILE COST  │ TEAM SKILL CEILING │
  │ can a peer   │ does tsc/IDE  │ can the team SAFELY │
  │ read & edit? │ stay fast?    │ maintain & extend?  │
  └──────────────┴───────────────┴────────────────────┘
        choose the SIMPLEST type that closes the bug class
```

Rule of thumb: prefer a plain discriminated union or a small generic that a mid-level dev reads at a glance. Escalate to heavy conditional/recursive types only when they **eliminate a real, recurring class of bug**, and isolate that complexity behind a clean public type so consumers never see the gore.

#### 🌍 Real-life analogy
It's like **seasoning a dish**. A pinch of salt (a discriminated union) transforms the meal. Dumping the whole jar (five nested conditional types to save one cast) ruins it — and now nobody else in the kitchen can adjust the recipe. The master chef adds *exactly enough*, and no more.

#### 💻 Code example
```typescript
// OVER-ENGINEERED: a recursive conditional type to "prettify" — clever,
// but slow to compile, hard to read, and the payoff is cosmetic.
type DeepReadonly<T> = T extends (infer U)[]
  ? ReadonlyArray<DeepReadonly<U>>
  : T extends object
  ? { readonly [K in keyof T]: DeepReadonly<T[K]> }
  : T;

// PRAGMATIC: for most cases, the built-in shallow `Readonly` is enough,
// communicates intent instantly, and costs the checker almost nothing.
interface Config { host: string; port: number; }
function start(cfg: Readonly<Config>) { /* cfg.host = "x" → error */ }

// The architect's question is NOT "can I write DeepReadonly?" (yes),
// but "does THIS code actually need it, or is Readonly clearer & cheaper?"
```

#### 🎤 Interview question
**Q: When is a clever type *not* worth it?**
A: When its cost outweighs the bugs it prevents. The costs are **readability** (can a teammate read, review, and safely edit it?), **compile/IDE performance** (deep conditional or recursive types slow `tsc` and autocomplete), and the **team skill ceiling** (if only one person can maintain it, it's a bus-factor risk). I reach for heavy type-level machinery only when it eliminates a genuine, recurring class of bug, and I hide that complexity behind a simple public type. Otherwise the simplest type that closes the bug — usually a discriminated union or a small generic — wins.

---

## Conclusion

You've gone from *"types are just annotations"* to wielding the type system as a **design tool** — modelling domains so illegal states can't even be constructed, bending inference to your will, and knowing exactly where compile-time guarantees end and runtime validation must begin. You can read a gnarly conditional type, justify a `tsconfig` flag, speed up a slow build, and lead a JS→TS migration.

The real skill was never memorising utility types — it's knowing **when a type earns its complexity** and when plain code is clearer. That judgement is what an architect brings.

> **How to lock it in:** revisit each module's 🎤 interview questions and answer them **out loud** before reading the answer. If you can teach it, you own it.
